arXiv:2305.18990v2 [math.MG] 23 Jan 2024




Identifiability of Points and Rigidity of Hypergraphs
under Algebraic Constraints00footnotetext: 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: Primary 52C25, 05E14; Secondary 05C65, 68R05, 14N07.



James Cruickshank

   
Fatemeh Mohammadi

   
Anthony Nixon

   
Shin-ichi Tanigawa



Abstract
    

The identifiability problem arises naturally in a number of contexts in mathematics and computer science. Specific instances include local or global rigidity of graphs and unique completability of partially-filled tensors subject to rank conditions. The identifiability of points on secant varieties has also been a topic of much research in algebraic geometry. It is often formulated as the problem of identifying a set of points satisfying a given set of algebraic relations. A key question then is to prove sufficient conditions for relations to guarantee the identifiability of the points.
    

This paper proposes a new general framework for capturing the identifiability problem when a set of algebraic relations has a combinatorial structure and develops tools to analyse the impact of the underlying combinatorics on the local or global identifiability of points. Our framework is built on the language of graph rigidity, where the measurements are Euclidean distances between two points, but applicable in the generality of hypergraphs with arbitrary algebraic measurements. We establish necessary and sufficient (hyper)graph theoretical conditions for identifiability by exploiting techniques from graph rigidity theory and algebraic geometry of secant varieties. In particular our work analyses combinatorially the effect of non-generic projections of secant varieties.
  




1 Introduction




Suppose one is given a set of points in  \mathbb{R}^{d} ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d} whose positions are unknown and a measuring device which provides relations among those points.
The fundamental question arises: Can the locations of the points be uniquely identified from the measurements?
This identifiability problem is ubiquitous across various applications in data science and engineering. Furthermore, it appears in several context of mathematics such as the identifiability of secant varieties in algebraic geometry.
A key question in the identifiability problem is to determine a sufficient condition for the observations to guarantee the unique identification of the points. Such a question is often challenging if the observations are not sufficiently generic.




In this paper, we introduce a general framework for capturing the identifiability problem when a set of algebraic relations has a combinatorial structure as follows.
Suppose there are  n nn unknown points  p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{n} p1,p2,…,pnp_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{n} in  \mathbb{R}^{d} ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Let  g gg be a polynomial map, which is a measurement function representing a measurement device,
and suppose that the value of  g gg is determined for each tuple of  k kk points in  \mathbb{R}^{d} ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}.




To represent a set of possible observations, we utilise a  k kk-uniform hypergraph denoted as  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E). In this case,  V VV corresponds to the set  \{1,\dots,n\} {1,…,n}\{1,\dots,n\} representing the points. Essentially, the observer can obtain measurements  g(p_{v_{1}},\dots,p_{v_{k}}) g⁡(pv1,…,pvk)g(p_{v_{1}},\dots,p_{v_{k}}) for all  \{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in E {v1,…,vk}∈E\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in E.
Then the identifiability problem revolves around determining whether the polynomial system given by:





 g(x_{v_{1}},\dots,x_{v_{k}})=g(p_{v_{1}},\dots,p_{v_{k}})\hskip 20.00003pt(\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in E) g⁡(xv1,…,xvk)=g⁡(pv1,…,pvk)({v1,…,vk}∈E)g(x_{v_{1}},\dots,x_{v_{k}})=g(p_{v_{1}},\dots,p_{v_{k}})\hskip 20.00003pt(\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in E)

(1)


has a unique solution (up to certain symmetry) in the variables  x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n} x1,x2,…,xnx_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n} in  \mathbb{R}^{d} ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}.
This formulation is based on graph rigidity theory, which specifically addresses the case when  g gg represents the Euclidean distance between two points. Our framework extends beyond this specific case, providing a general approach for the identifiability problem in cases involving algebraic relations with combinatorial structures.




Graph rigidity theory has a long history with its roots in mathematics - arising from Cauchy and Euler’s investigations of Euclidean polyhedra [18] - and engineering - Maxwell’s analysis of the stiffness of frames [49].
The question is related to various branches of mathematics such as graph theory, matroid theory, and algebraic geometry, and it also appears in various modern engineering topics such as molecular conformation, network localisation, and multi-agent formation control, see, e.g., [68, 26].
The two central notions in rigidity theory are global rigidity and local rigidity, which concern whether the system (1) has a unique solution or a finite number of solutions (up to Euclidean isometry), respectively, when  G GG is an ordinary graph and  g gg is the Euclidean distance.
Accordingly, one can define the notion of local rigidity and global rigidity of hypergraphs for each model of measurement map  g gg using the system (1).




A key aim of the paper is to present a generalisation of graph rigidity theory that covers a wide range of algebraic topics by looking at general measurement maps  g gg.
While the concept of graph rigidity is intuitive, several variants of the problem have already been explored. Notable examples include rigidity in different metric spaces such as spherical space or  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-space, as well as rigidity concerning other geometric constraints (see, for instance, [13, 17, 28, 55, 59, 43]).
While it is indeed possible to consider more general algebraic systems, doing so may result in a loss of the combinatorial perspective, which serves as the core of rigidity theory.
In this paper, we demonstrate that this new rigidity model of hypergraphs is general enough to address the identifiability problem across various applications and it also provides new mathematical challenges from the graph rigidity viewpoint.




One prominent example of our rigidity model is the uniqueness problem of low-rank tensor completions. Identifiability in tensor decompositions arises naturally in a panorama of application areas, including phylogenetics [5], quantum information [36] and signal processing [52]. Our model can further deal with situations when some entries are missing, thus enhancing its applicability.
Our rigidity-based approach to the unique low-rank tensor completion problem can be viewed as an extension of the rigidity-based analysis of low-rank matrix completions pioneered by
Singer-Cucuringu [59] and Király-Theran-Tomioka [43]. However, as we will demonstrate in Section 5,
the tensor case gives rise to substantially more challenging mathematical questions.




The key idea of combinatorial rigidity theory is the genericity assumption on point configurations.
In the case of local rigidity, under this genericity assumption, the core question becomes equivalent to determining the dimension of the image of a measurement map. Notably, these images typically correspond to projections of
well-studied algebraic varieties such as the secant variety of a Veronese variety or of a Grassmaniann variety. Consequently, our rigidity problem can be formulated as an algebraic sensing problem,
similar to the approach presented
in [15]. While Noether’s normalization lemma shows how the dimension changes under a generic projection [15], this general theory does not provide a solution to our specific problem. In our case, the projections are non-generic as they are consistently performed along a coordinate axis of the ambient affine space.




A particularly interesting case is when the measurement map is the sum of simpler measurement maps.
In such a case, the image of the measurement map is a secant of a simpler variety.
The computation of the dimension of secant varieties and the identifiability of secant points have been studied extensively in algebraic geometry (see, e.g., [69, 10]), and results in that context give rise to rigidity theorems for complete hypergraphs in our language.
For example, the Alexander-Hirschowitz theorem [4] on the dimension of secant varieties of Veronese varieties provides a characterisation for complete hypergraphs to be locally rigid, specifically when the measurement map  g gg is the sum of copies of the product map over coordinates. These results highlight the connection between algebraic geometry and our notion of rigidity, offering new insights into the identifiability and rigidity properties of complete hypergraphs.
Subsequent results [22, 31] on identifiability even give a characterisation of global rigidity for complete hypergraphs.
(More details will be discussed in Section 5.)
However, there seems to be few results on the projections of secant varieties.
Our rigidity problem for general hypergraphs deals with two structures: the geometry of secant varieties and the combinatorics of coordinate projections.
The goal is to establish a link between these two structures, shedding light on their connections and implications.




By leveraging techniques from both rigidity theory and secant varieties, we will derive several necessary and sufficient conditions for local and global rigidity.
Our contributions include a graph-packing-type sufficient condition for local rigidity and a stress-matrix-type sufficient condition for global rigidity.
We believe that our new rigidity model and the results of this article are the start of a larger effort to exploit the link between combinatorial rigidity and the geometry of secant varieties. This connection is likely to be instrumental in solving further rigidity and identifiability problems in the future. To justify this we provide two concrete applications of our work. The first concerns random projections of secant varieties along coordinate axes.
Our rigidity framework enables us to reduce this to the problem of analysing a property of the Erdős-Rényi random hypergraph, and we establish the threshold dimension for a random axis-parallel projection of a secant variety to be full dimension with high probability.
The second analyses the identifiability of the  p pp-Cayley-Menger variety (an analogue of the Cayley-Menger variety for  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-norms) and global rigidity in the  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-space.
A combination of tools from algebraic geometry such as tangential weak defectiveness and graph-theoretical tools from rigidity theory leads to a precise combinatorial characterisation of the 2-identifiability of the axis-parallel projections of the  p pp-Cayley-Menger variety.
This result is also important in rigidity theory since it affirmatively answers the question of whether or not global rigidity in the  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-plane is a generic property of the graph when  p pp is even.
Both applications provide novel connections between our graph-theoretical approach of rigidity theory to the non-degeneracy and the identifiability of secant varieties in algebraic geometry.




Outline. In Section 2, we recall the basics of Euclidean rigidity theory and provide a new generalisation to the setting of arbitrary algebraic constraints on hypergraphs. Section 3 presents a range of examples that
are modelled by our generalisation,
including those arising from rigidity theory, matrix and tensor product completions and Chow decompositions. In Section 4, we provide an analysis of the basic theorems from rigidity theory in our general context providing, for example, a precise analogue of Asimow and Roth’s theorem. This section also presents our generalised rigidity theory in the algebraic geometry context, in particular explaining the notions of defectivity and identifiability. Further, we define the generic  g gg-rigidity matroid, compute the combinatorially expected rank of this matroid and pose the combinatorial characterisation problem.




Section 5 provides detailed information and references for what is known in each of the concrete instances from Section 3, particularly illustrating the abstract theory of Section 4. In section 6, we extend combinatorial techniques from graph rigidity theory to  k kk-uniform hyper-frameworks. In particular, we present a technique to construct a larger rigid hyperframework from a given one, and, in one of our main results, we provide a
packing condition that is sufficient
for rigidity when the constraints arise from multilinear  k kk-forms (Theorem 6.6). We then move on to analyse global rigidity for the generalised rigidity model in Section 7, noting first that global rigidity is a generic property in the complex case but not in the real case. Our main results here are Theorems 7.13 and 7.15 which provide sufficient conditions for global rigidity in the determinant and tensor product cases, analogous to a well known sufficient condition in the Euclidean rigidity case due to Connelly [25].




We then consider some variants of our generalisation in Section 8. Section 9 contains two applications of our work. Theorem 9.1 which is motivated by sampling complexity for low rank tensor completions shows the threshold, in the Erdős-Rényi subgraph model, for a random hypergraph for which the dimension of a random axis-parallel projection of a secant variety is full dimensional.
The second analyses global rigidity in the  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-plane.
We finish with some comments for further exploration (Section 10) including a potential application in algebraic statistics.

2 Rigidity of Hypergraphs




2.1 Euclidean rigidity




In this subsection, we review basic terminologies of the Euclidean rigidity.
The ordinary Euclidean rigidity concerns the rigidity of graphs drawn in Euclidean space.
Such a realisation of a graph in Euclidean space is called a (bar-and-joint) framework.
Conventionally a framework is defined as a pair  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) of a finite graph  G GG and a point-configuration  p:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d} p:V→ℝdp:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}.
The framework is rigid if the only edge-length preserving continuous motions of its vertices arise from isometries of  \mathbb{R}^{d} ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, and otherwise it is called flexible. (See Figure 1 for basic examples.) The study of the rigidity of frameworks has its origins in the work of Cauchy and Euler on Euclidean polyhedra [18] and Maxwell [49] on frames.


Figure 1: Simple examples of frameworks in the Euclidean plane: (left) a flexible framework with motion indicated; and (right) a rigid framework.




Abbot [1] showed that it is NP-hard to determine whether a given  d dd-dimensional framework is rigid whenever  d\geq 2 d≥2d\geq 2. The problem becomes more tractable for generic frameworks  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) since we can linearize the problem and consider ‘infinitesimal rigidity’ instead. In this setting, the measurement map  f_{G}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{E} fG:(ℝd)V→ℝEf_{G}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{E} is defined by putting  f_{G}(p)=(\dots,\|p(i)-p(j)\|^{2},\dots)_{ij\in E} fG​(p)=(…,‖p⁡(i)−p⁡(j)‖2,…)i​j∈Ef_{G}(p)=(\dots,\|p(i)-p(j)\|^{2},\dots)_{ij\in E}, where
 \lVert.\lVert ∥.∥\lVert.\lVert denotes the Euclidean metric.
The rigidity matrix  R_{d}(G,p)=\frac{1}{2}df_{G} Rd​(G,p)=12​d​fGR_{d}(G,p)=\frac{1}{2}df_{G} is the  |E|\times d|V| |E|×d​|V||E|\times d|V| matrix in which, for  e=ij\in E e=i​j∈Ee=ij\in E, the entries in row  e ee and columns  i ii and  j jj are  p(i)-p(j) p⁡(i)−p⁡(j)p(i)-p(j) and  p(j)-p(i) p⁡(j)−p⁡(i)p(j)-p(i), respectively, and all other entries in row  e ee are zero. Here,  g gg is the usual Euclidean distance.
A map  \dot{p}:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d} p˙:V→ℝd\dot{p}:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d} is called an infinitesimal motion of  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) if





 \langle p(i)-p(j),\dot{p}(i)-\dot{p}(j)\rangle=0\mbox{ for all }ij\in E, ⟨p⁡(i)−p⁡(j),p˙​(i)−p˙​(j)⟩=0​ for all ​i​j∈E,\langle p(i)-p(j),\dot{p}(i)-\dot{p}(j)\rangle=0\mbox{ for all }ij\in E,



where  \langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle ⟨⋅,⋅⟩\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle denotes the Euclidean inner product.
An infinitesimal motion  \dot{p} p˙\dot{p} is trivial if there exists a skew-symmetric matrix  S SS and a vector  t tt such that  \dot{p}(i)=Sp(i)+t p˙​(i)=S​p​(i)+t\dot{p}(i)=Sp(i)+t for all  i\in V i∈Vi\in V. A framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally rigid if every infinitesimal flex of  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is trivial. Equivalently,  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally rigid if  G GG is complete on at most  d+1 d+1d+1 vertices or  |V|\geq d+2 |V|≥d+2|V|\geq d+2 and  \rank\,R_{d}(G,p)=d|V|-{d+1\choose 2} rank⁡Rd​(G,p)=d​|V|−(d+12)\rank\,R_{d}(G,p)=d|V|-{d+1\choose 2}.
Asimow and Roth [7] showed that infinitesimal rigidity is equivalent to rigidity for generic frameworks (and hence that generic rigidity depends only on the underlying graph of the framework). Figure 2 illustrates these concepts in dimension 2.


Figure 2: (Left) An infinitesimal flex of the flexible framework in Figure 1. (Middle) A generically rigid graph whose edge set is dependent in the generic 2-dimensional rigidity matroid since  |E|=6>2|V|-3 |E|=6>2​|V|−3|E|=6>2|V|-3. (Right) A generically rigid graph realised as a framework that has an infinitesimal flex (indicated).




The generic  d dd-dimensional rigidity matroid of a graph  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) is the matroid  \mathcal{R}_{d}(G) ℛd​(G)\mathcal{R}_{d}(G) on  E EE in which a set of edges  F\subseteq E F⊆EF\subseteq E is independent if the corresponding rows of  R(G,p) R⁡(G,p)R(G,p) are linearly independent, for some (or, equivalently, for every) generic  p pp.




A framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) in  \mathbb{R}^{d} ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d} is globally rigid if every  q\in f_{G}^{-1}(f_{G}(p)) q∈fG−1​(fG​(p))q\in f_{G}^{-1}(f_{G}(p)) satisfies  t\cdot q=p t⋅q=pt\cdot q=p for some isometry  t tt of  \mathbb{R}^{d} ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d} (i.e. some composition of translations and rotations).
See Figure 3 for an example.


Figure 3: (Left and middle) rigid but not globally rigid frameworks in the plane. (Right) a globally rigid framework in the plane.





2.2 Rigidity under algebraic constraints




The concept of rigidity is flexible in the sense that all notations can be extended by replacing the Euclidean distance function with a general smooth function. Since a measurement may involve more than two vertices, we will use hypergraphs to capture the underlying combinatorics.




Extending the central object from rigidity, a pair  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) of a hypergraph  G GG and a point-configuration  p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} p∈(𝔽d)Vp\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} is called a  d dd-dimensional hyper-framework or a hyper-framework in  \mathbb{F}^{d} 𝔽d\mathbb{F}^{d}.




Throughout the paper, we use the following setup.



Setup 2.1.



Let  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E).
Suppose we are given a  k kk-uniform hyper-framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) and a polynomial map  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}.
The  g gg-measurement map of  G GG is defined as a polynomial map  f_{g,G}:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{E} fg,G:(𝔽d)V→𝔽Ef_{g,G}:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{E} that sends  p pp to the list of the  g gg-values of the tuples  (p(v_{1}),p(v_{2}),\dots,p(v_{k})) (p⁡(v1),p⁡(v2),…,p⁡(vk))(p(v_{1}),p(v_{2}),\dots,p(v_{k})) over the hyperedges  \{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\} {v1,…,vk}\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\} in  E EE, i.e.,





 f_{g,G}(p):=\left(g(p(v_{1}),p(v_{2}),\dots,p(v_{k})):e=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in E\right). fg,G(p):=(g(p(v1),p(v2),…,p(vk)):e={v1,…,vk}∈E).f_{g,G}(p):=\left(g(p(v_{1}),p(v_{2}),\dots,p(v_{k})):e=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in E\right).







In terms of the discussion in the introduction,  g gg represents a measurement device and  f_{g,G}(p) fg,G​(p)f_{g,G}(p) is the actual list of measurements that the observer can obtain. The observer is asked to recover  p pp from  f_{g,G}(p) fg,G​(p)f_{g,G}(p). The  g gg-rigidity defined below captures the identifiability of  p pp.




Note that, in order to make  f_{g,G} fg,Gf_{g,G} well-defined,  g gg must be either symmetric or anti-symmetric with respect to the ordering of the points, and if  g gg is anti-symmetric
we always assume that  v_{1},\dots,v_{k} v1,…,vkv_{1},\dots,v_{k} are aligned in increasing order in  g(p(v_{1}),\dots,p(v_{k})) g⁡(p⁡(v1),…,p⁡(vk))g(p(v_{1}),\dots,p(v_{k})), assuming a (fixed) total order on the vertices of  G GG.




In most practical applications, there is a nontrivial group action that does not change the value of the  g gg-measurement map, and rigidity has to be defined by taking care of the degree of freedom caused by such actions.
Suppose the general affine group  {\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) Aff⁡(d,𝔽){\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) acts on  \mathbb{F}^{d} 𝔽d\mathbb{F}^{d} by  \gamma\cdot x=Ax+t γ⋅x=A​x+t\gamma\cdot x=Ax+t for  x\in\mathbb{F}^{d} x∈𝔽dx\in\mathbb{F}^{d} and each pair  \gamma=(A,t) γ=(A,t)\gamma=(A,t) of  A\in{\rm GL}(d,\mathbb{F}) A∈GL⁡(d,𝔽)A\in{\rm GL}(d,\mathbb{F}) and  t\in\mathbb{F}^{d} t∈𝔽dt\in\mathbb{F}^{d}.
The action of  {\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) Aff⁡(d,𝔽){\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) on  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} (𝔽d)V(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} is also defined by  (\gamma\cdot p)(v)=Ap(v)+t\ (v\in V) (γ⋅p)​(v)=A​p​(v)+t⁡(v∈V)(\gamma\cdot p)(v)=Ap(v)+t\ (v\in V) for any  \gamma=(A,t)\in{\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) γ=(A,t)∈Aff⁡(d,𝔽)\gamma=(A,t)\in{\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) and  p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} p∈(𝔽d)Vp\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}.
Then the induced action on a polynomial map  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} is given by
 \gamma\cdot g(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=g(\gamma^{-1}\cdot x_{1},\dots,\gamma^{-1}\cdot x_{k}) γ⋅g⁡(x1,…,xk)=g⁡(γ−1⋅x1,…,γ−1⋅xk)\gamma\cdot g(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=g(\gamma^{-1}\cdot x_{1},\dots,\gamma^{-1}\cdot x_{k}) for  x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\mathbb{F}^{d} x1,…,xk∈𝔽dx_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\mathbb{F}^{d} and  \gamma\in{\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) γ∈Aff⁡(d,𝔽)\gamma\in{\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}).
We say that  \gamma γ\gamma stabilises  g gg if  g gg is invariant by the action of  \gamma γ\gamma.
The set of pairs  (A,t) (A,t)(A,t) that stabilise  g gg forms a subgroup of  {\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) Aff⁡(d,𝔽){\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}), called the stabiliser  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} of  g gg.
Since the action is smooth,  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} is a closed subgroup of  {\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) Aff⁡(d,𝔽){\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}), so  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} is also a Lie group by the closed-subgroup theorem.




We are now ready to give a formal definition of  g gg-rigidity.



Definition 2.2.



We say that  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is globally  g gg-rigid if
for any  q\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p)) q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))q\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p)) there is  \gamma\in\Gamma_{g} γ∈Γg\gamma\in\Gamma_{g} such that
 q=\gamma\cdot p q=γ⋅pq=\gamma\cdot p.
We say that  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is locally  g gg-rigid if there is an open neighbourhood  N NN of  p pp in  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} (𝔽d)V(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} (in the Euclidean topology) such that
for any  q\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p))\cap N q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))∩Nq\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p))\cap N there is  \gamma\in\Gamma_{g} γ∈Γg\gamma\in\Gamma_{g} such that  q=\gamma\cdot p q=γ⋅pq=\gamma\cdot p.




Remark 2.3.



In ordinary Euclidean rigidity, the following fact is implicitly used to capture rigidity in several different but equivalent forms.



Proposition 2.4.



Suppose  V VV is a finite set and  p,q\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{V} p,q∈(ℝd)Vp,q\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{V}.
Then





 \|p(u)-p(v)\|=\|q(u)-q(v)\|\hskip 20.00003pt(u,v\in V) ‖p⁡(u)−p⁡(v)‖=‖q⁡(u)−q⁡(v)‖(u,v∈V)\|p(u)-p(v)\|=\|q(u)-q(v)\|\hskip 20.00003pt(u,v\in V)



holds if and only if
there is an Euclidean isometry  (A,t) (A,t)(A,t) such that  q(u)=Ap(u)+t\ (u\in V) q⁡(u)=A​p​(u)+t⁡(u∈V)q(u)=Ap(u)+t\ (u\in V).





Let  |V|=n |V|=n|V|=n and  K_{n}^{2} Kn2K_{n}^{2} denote the complete graph on the vertex set  V. V.V.
Proposition 2.4 implies that the point configuration is uniquely determined up to Euclidean isometry by measuring inter-point distances for all edges in
 K_{n}^{2} Kn2K_{n}^{2},
and hence the framework of a complete graph is always locally/globally rigid in the ordinary Euclidean rigidity.
By this fact, we may also define the local/global rigidity of a graph  G GG by comparing  f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p)) fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p)) with  f^{-1}_{g,K_{n}^{2}}(f_{g,K_{n}^{2}}(p)) fg,Kn2−1​(fg,Kn2​(p))f^{-1}_{g,K_{n}^{2}}(f_{g,K_{n}^{2}}(p)).
However, for a general measurement  g gg, the analogue of Proposition 2.4 is no longer true, that is if we replace  \lVert.\lVert ∥.∥\lVert.\lVert with a general measurement  g gg, the statement does not hold in general.
A notable example is the case of symmetric tensor completion,
where the Alexander-Hirschowitz theorem (cf. Theorem 5.1) shows that Proposition 2.4 does not hold for the corresponding polynomial map  g gg. A more basic example is provided by rigidity theory under a smooth  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p} norm; as described in [30, Figure 2], the proposition fails in the  \ell_{4} ℓ4\ell_{4}-plane where two realisations of the complete graph  K_{4} K4K_{4} can have the same edge lengths but there is no isometry mapping one onto the other.
In general, the validity of the counterpart statement to Proposition 2.4 also depends on the number of vertices  |V| |V||V| and the size of each hyperedge  k kk. It can be either true or false in different cases. However, our definition of  g gg-rigidity for hyper-frameworks, as stated earlier, is formulated to solely depend on the measurement map  g gg itself, remaining independent of factors such as  |V| |V||V| and  k kk.







3 Examples of  g gg-rigidity Models




We give a list of primary examples of  g gg-rigidity.



Ordinary Euclidean rigidity.




A fundamental example is the case when
 G GG is 2-uniform (i.e., a graph) and
 g(x,y)=(\lVert x-y\lVert_{2})^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2} g(x,y)=(∥x−y∥2)2=∑i=1d(xi−yi)2g(x,y)=(\lVert x-y\lVert_{2})^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2} for  x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d} x,y∈ℝdx,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Then the Euclidean group  E(d) E⁡(d)E(d) is the stabilizer of  g gg,
and its Lie algebra is the set of pairs  (S,t) (S,t)(S,t) of skew-symmetric matrices  S SS and  t\in\mathbb{R}^{d} t∈ℝdt\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
In this case,  g gg-rigidity is nothing but the standard rigidity of bar-and-joint frameworks.




Rigidity in pseudo-Euclidean space.




In the pseudo-Euclidean rigidity context
 G GG is 2-uniform (i.e., a graph) and
 g(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2}-\sum_{i=d_{1}+1}^{d}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2} g⁡(x,y)=∑i=1d1(xi−yi)2−∑i=d1+1d(xi−yi)2g(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2}-\sum_{i=d_{1}+1}^{d}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2} for  x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d} x,y∈ℝdx,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d} and  d_{1}\leq d d1≤dd_{1}\leq d.




 \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-norm rigidity.




An alternative generalisation of Euclidean rigidity is to allow the distance function to be replaced by distance under another norm [44]. Specifically  G GG is 2-uniform (i.e., a graph) and
 g(x,y)=(\lVert x-y\lVert_{p})^{p}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}|x_{i}-y_{i}|^{p} g(x,y)=(∥x−y∥p)p=∑i=1d|xi−yi|pg(x,y)=(\lVert x-y\lVert_{p})^{p}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}|x_{i}-y_{i}|^{p} for  x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d} x,y∈ℝdx,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d} and  1<p<\infty 1<p<∞1<p<\infty. In the case where  p pp is a positive even integer,  g gg is a polynomial.




Volume-constrained rigidity.




Given a  d dd-dimensional pure simplicial complex realised in  \mathbb{R}^{d} ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d},
the notion of volume-constrained rigidity concerns whether there is a motion of vertices keeping the (signed) volume of each  d dd-simplex [17]. A  d dd-dimensional pure simplicial complex can be identified with a  (d+1) (d+1)(d+1)-uniform hyper-framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) with a simple hypergraph  G GG.
Hence, the volume-constrained rigidity can be captured as the  g gg-rigidity of a simple  (d+1) (d+1)(d+1)-uniform hyper-framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) with  g:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{d+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} g:(ℝd)d+1→ℝg:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{d+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} defined by





 g(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d+1})=\det\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{d+1}\\
1&1&\dots&1\end{pmatrix}. g⁡(x1,x2,…,xd+1)=det(x1x2…xd+111…1).g(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d+1})=\det\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{d+1}\\
1&1&\dots&1\end{pmatrix}.



A rigidity-based analysis was initiated by Borcea and Streinu [13]. Recent studies can be found in [17, 61].




Positive semidefinite symmetric matrix completion.




Let  T TT be a positive semidefinite symmetric matrix of size  n nn over  \mathbb{R} ℝ\mathbb{R}.
If the rank of  T TT is  d dd, then the spectral decomposition implies that





 T=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}x_{i}^{\top} T=∑i=1dxi​xi⊤T=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}x_{i}^{\top}

(2)


for some vectors  x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} x1,x2,…,xd∈ℝnx_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Let  p pp be a  d\times n d×nd\times n-matrix obtained by aligning  x_{i} xix_{i} as the  i ii-th row vector.
Then  p(i)\cdot p(j) p⁡(i)⋅p⁡(j)p(i)\cdot p(j) is equal to the  (i,j) (i,j)(i,j)-th entry  t_{ij} ti​jt_{ij} of  T TT.




In the positive semidefinite symmetric matrix completion problem, we are given a partially-filled positive semidefinite symmetric matrix  T TT and asked to recover the positive semidefinite symmetric matrix by filling missing entries.
If we use a graph  G=([n],E) G=([n],E)G=([n],E) (which may contain self-loops) to denote a set of indices  e=\{i,j\} e={i,j}e=\{i,j\} of known entries  t_{e} tet_{e} of  T TT, then the problem is to find  p\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n} p∈(ℝd)np\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n} satisfying





 \langle p(i),p(j)\rangle=t_{e}\hskip 20.00003pt(e=\{i,j\}\in E), ⟨p⁡(i),p⁡(j)⟩=te(e={i,j}∈E),\langle p(i),p(j)\rangle=t_{e}\hskip 20.00003pt(e=\{i,j\}\in E),

(3)


where  \langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle ⟨⋅,⋅⟩\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle denotes the Euclidean inner product.
We are, in particular, interested in the uniqueness of the completions rather than finding a completion.
In the unique completion problem, we are given a solution  p\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{V} p∈(ℝd)Vp\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{V} of Equation (3) and are asked if it is the unique solution of (3).
This uniqueness question is equivalent to asking the  g gg-rigidity of a framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) for an appropriate choice of  g gg.




Specifically, consider a 2-uniform hypergraph (i.e., a graph)  G GG and
 g(x,y)=\langle x,y\rangle g⁡(x,y)=⟨x,y⟩g(x,y)=\langle x,y\rangle for  x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d} x,y∈ℝdx,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Then the orthogonal group  O(d) O⁡(d)O(d) is the stabiliser of  g gg,
and the global  g gg-rigidity decides if Equation (3) has the unique solution up to the action of  O(d) O⁡(d)O(d), or equivalently a completion is unique.




This rigidity-based formulation of matrix completion coincides with that introduced by Singer and Cucuringu [59] whose paper also describes a number of detailed examples of matrix completions from this viewpoint.




Symmetric tensor completions.




The idea of converting the unique low-rank matrix completion problem to the  g gg-rigidity of frameworks can be extended naturally to tensors as follows.




For a vector space  V VV of dimension  n nn over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C}, let  V^{\otimes k} V⊗kV^{\otimes k} be the  k kk-fold tensor product of  V VV.
We fix a basis of  V VV, and assume that each  T\in V^{\otimes k} T∈V⊗kT\in V^{\otimes k} is represented by a  k kk-dimensional array over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C}.
A tensor  T\in V^{\otimes k} T∈V⊗kT\in V^{\otimes k} is said to be symmetric if for any permutation  \sigma σ\sigma on  [k] [k][k]
we have  T_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k}}=T_{\sigma(i_{1}),\sigma(i_{2}),\dots,\sigma(i_{k})} Ti1,i2,…,ik=Tσ⁡(i1),σ⁡(i2),…,σ⁡(ik)T_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k}}=T_{\sigma(i_{1}),\sigma(i_{2}),\dots,\sigma(i_{k})}. The set of symmetric tensors in  V^{\otimes k} V⊗kV^{\otimes k} is denoted by
 S^{k}(V) Sk​(V)S^{k}(V).
It is known that any symmetric tensor can be written as





 T=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{\otimes k}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}\otimes x_{i}\otimes\dots\otimes x_{i} T=∑i=1dxi⊗k:=∑i=1dxi⊗xi⊗⋯⊗xiT=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{\otimes k}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}\otimes x_{i}\otimes\dots\otimes x_{i}

(4)


for some vectors  x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d}\in V x1,x2,…,xd∈Vx_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d}\in V.
For  T\in S^{k}(V) T∈Sk​(V)T\in S^{k}(V), the smallest possible  d dd for which we can write  T TT in the form of Equation (4) is called the symmetric rank of  T TT.




Once we introduce a notion of rank, the corresponding low-rank completion problem can be defined automatically.
In the symmetric tensor completion problem,
given a partially-filled tensor of order  k kk and size  n nn,
we are asked to fill the remaining entries to obtain a symmetric tensor of symmetric rank at most  d dd.
Recall that  \genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{X}{k} {Xk}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{X}{k} denotes the set of multisets of  k kk elements of a finite set  X XX.
Due to the symmetry condition, each entry of a symmetric tensor can be indexed by an element in  \genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{k} {[n]k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{k}.
In this manner, we encode the underlying combinatorics of each instance of the completion problem using a  k kk-uniform hypergraph  ([n],E) ([n],E)([n],E).




We can also convert the decomposition in Equation (4) to a form of an algebraic relation among points in  \mathbb{C}^{d} ℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}.
For this, let  p pp be the  d\times n d×nd\times n matrix with the  i ii-th row equal to  x_{i} xix_{i}.
Then, for each  e\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{k} e∈{[n]k}e\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{k}, the  e ee-th entry of Equation (4) is equal to  \mathbf{1}\cdot\bigodot_{v\in e}p(v) 𝟏⋅⨀v∈ep⁡(v)\mathbf{1}\cdot\bigodot_{v\in e}p(v),
where  \mathbf{1} 𝟏\mathbf{1} denotes the all-one vector and  \bigodot ⨀\bigodot denotes the Hadamard product of vectors, that is the component-wise product.
Hence, the symmetric tensor completion problem can be reformulated as a hypergraph realisation problem as follows:
Given a  k kk-uniform hypergraph  G=([n],E) G=([n],E)G=([n],E) and  a_{e}\in\mathbb{C} ae∈ℂa_{e}\in\mathbb{C} for each  e\in E e∈Ee\in E, find  p\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{V} p∈(ℂd)Vp\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{V} such that





 \mathbf{1}\cdot\bigodot_{v\in e}p(v)=a_{e}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $e\in E$}, 𝟏⋅⨀v∈ep⁡(v)=aefor e∈E,\mathbf{1}\cdot\bigodot_{v\in e}p(v)=a_{e}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $e\in E$},

(5)


The corresponding unique completion problem is captured by  g gg-rigidity for  g:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} g:(ℂd)k→ℂg:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} with





 g(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})=\mathbf{1}\cdot\bigodot_{i\in\{1,\dots,k\}}y_{i}. g⁡(y1,…,yk)=𝟏⋅⨀i∈{1,…,k}yi.g(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})=\mathbf{1}\cdot\bigodot_{i\in\{1,\dots,k\}}y_{i}.

(6)


The stabiliser  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} of  g gg is the set of matrices of the form  \Sigma D Σ​D\Sigma D with a permutation matrix  \Sigma Σ\Sigma and a diagonal matrix  D DD over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C} whose diagonal entries are  k kk-th roots of unity.




Let  h_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{F}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} hprod:𝔽k→𝔽h_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{F}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} be the product map that takes  k kk values and returns their product.
Then the function  g gg defined in Equation (6) is written as the sum of  d dd copies of  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod}.
This structure will be important in the analysis of  g gg-rigidity in the subsequent discussion.




Skew-symmetric tensor completions.




A tensor  T\in V^{\otimes k} T∈V⊗kT\in V^{\otimes k} is said to be skew-symmetric if for any permutation  \sigma σ\sigma on  [k] [k][k]
we have  T_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k}}={\rm sign}(\sigma)T_{\sigma(i_{1}),\sigma(i_{2}),\dots,\sigma(i_{k})} Ti1,i2,…,ik=sign⁡(σ)​Tσ⁡(i1),σ⁡(i2),…,σ⁡(ik)T_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k}}={\rm sign}(\sigma)T_{\sigma(i_{1}),\sigma(i_{2}),\dots,\sigma(i_{k})}.
The set of skew-symmetric tensors in  V^{\otimes k} V⊗kV^{\otimes k} is denoted by
 A^{k}(V) Ak​(V)A^{k}(V).
It is known that  A^{k}(V) Ak​(V)A^{k}(V) is linearly isomorphic to the  k kk-th component  \bigwedge^{k}V ⋀kV\bigwedge^{k}V of the exterior algebra  \bigwedge V ⋀V\bigwedge V
and any skew-symmetric tensor can be written as





 T=\sum_{i=1}^{r}x_{1}^{i}\wedge x_{2}^{i}\wedge\dots\wedge x_{k}^{i} T=∑i=1rx1i∧x2i∧⋯∧xkiT=\sum_{i=1}^{r}x_{1}^{i}\wedge x_{2}^{i}\wedge\dots\wedge x_{k}^{i}

(7)


for some  kr k​rkr vectors  x_{j}^{i} xjix_{j}^{i} ( 1\leq i\leq r 1≤i≤r1\leq i\leq r,  1\leq j\leq k 1≤j≤k1\leq j\leq k), where  \wedge ∧\wedge denotes the tensor product of vectors.
The smallest possible  r rr for which we can write  T TT in the form of Equation (7) is called the skew-symmetric rank of  T TT.
When  V=\mathbb{F}^{n} V=𝔽nV=\mathbb{F}^{n}, each element in  A^{k}(V) Ak​(V)A^{k}(V) is called a skew-symmetric tensor of order  k kk of size  n nn (over  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}).




Recall that  {X\choose k} (Xk){X\choose k} denotes the family of sets of  k kk elements of a finite set  X XX.
Due to the skew-symmetry condition, each entry of a skew-symmetric tensor can be indexed by an element in
 {X\choose k} (Xk){X\choose k}.
Hence, we encode the underlying combinatorics of each instance of the skew-symmetric completion problem using a simple  k kk-uniform hypergraph  ([n],E) ([n],E)([n],E) (where each hyperedge is a set unlike the symmetric case).




In the same manner as the correspondence between Equations (4) and (5) in the symmetric tensor case, we shall convert Equation (7) to a realisation problem of the underlying hypergraph.
This can be done by picking column vectors in the matrix obtained by stacking  x_{j}^{i} xjix_{j}^{i} as row vectors. Specifically, consider the  k\times n k×nk\times n matrix  Q_{i} QiQ_{i} obtained by stacking  x_{j}^{i} xjix_{j}^{i} for  1\leq j\leq k 1≤j≤k1\leq j\leq k for each  1\leq i\leq r 1≤i≤r1\leq i\leq r as row vectors,
and let  P PP be the  kr\times n k​r×nkr\times n matrix obtained by stacking  Q_{i} QiQ_{i}’s.
Let  q_{i}\in(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{n} qi∈(ℂk)nq_{i}\in(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{n} (and respectively  p\in(\mathbb{C}^{kr})^{n} p∈(ℂk​r)np\in(\mathbb{C}^{kr})^{n}) be the point configuration such that  q_{i}(v) qi​(v)q_{i}(v) (resp.,  p(v) p⁡(v)p(v)) is the  v vv-th column of  Q_{i} QiQ_{i} (resp.,  P PP).
Then, for each  e=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\} e={v1,…,vk}e=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}, the  e ee-th entry of  T TT in Equation (7) is equal to
 \sum_{i=1}^{r}\det\begin{pmatrix}q_{i}(v_{1})\dots q_{i}(v_{k})\end{pmatrix} ∑i=1rdet(qi​(v1)​…​qi​(vk))\sum_{i=1}^{r}\det\begin{pmatrix}q_{i}(v_{1})\dots q_{i}(v_{k})\end{pmatrix}.
This gives the following equivalent formulation of the skew-symmetric tensor completion problem:
Given a simple  k kk-uniform hypergraph  G=([n],E) G=([n],E)G=([n],E) and  a_{e}\in\mathbb{C} ae∈ℂa_{e}\in\mathbb{C} for each  e\in E e∈Ee\in E, find an  r rr-tuple  p=(q_{1},\dots,q_{r}) p=(q1,…,qr)p=(q_{1},\dots,q_{r}) of  q_{i}\in(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{V} qi∈(ℂk)Vq_{i}\in(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{V} such that





 \sum_{i=1}^{r}\det\begin{pmatrix}q_{i}(v_{1})\dots q_{i}(v_{k})\end{pmatrix}=a_{e}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $e=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in E$}. ∑i=1rdet(qi​(v1)​…​qi​(vk))=aefor e={v1,…,vk}∈E.\sum_{i=1}^{r}\det\begin{pmatrix}q_{i}(v_{1})\dots q_{i}(v_{k})\end{pmatrix}=a_{e}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $e=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in E$}.

(8)





Let  h_{\rm det}:(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} hdet:(ℂk)k→ℂh_{\rm det}:(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} be the determinant as a multilinear  k kk-form over  \mathbb{C}^{k} ℂk\mathbb{C}^{k}.
Then the corresponding unique completion problem is captured by  g gg-rigidity for  g:(\mathbb{C}^{kr})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} g:(ℂk​r)k→ℂg:(\mathbb{C}^{kr})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} defined as the sum of  r rr copies of  h_{\rm det} hdeth_{\rm det}.
Since the stabiliser of  h_{\rm det} hdeth_{\rm det} is  {\rm SL}(k,\mathbb{C}) SL⁡(k,ℂ){\rm SL}(k,\mathbb{C}),
the stabiliser of  g gg is





 \{(A_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus A_{r})(\Sigma\otimes I_{k})\mid A_{i}\in{\rm SL}(k,\mathbb{C}),\Sigma:\text{ a permutation matrix of size $r$}\}. {(A1⊕⋯⊕Ar)(Σ⊗Ik)∣Ai∈SL(k,ℂ),Σ: a permutation matrix of size r}.\{(A_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus A_{r})(\Sigma\otimes I_{k})\mid A_{i}\in{\rm SL}(k,\mathbb{C}),\Sigma:\text{ a permutation matrix of size $r$}\}.






Chow decompositions.




Replacing the determinant with the permanent in the above discussion, we obtain the corresponding problem for Chow decompositions.
Suppose  f ff is a homogeneous polynomial in  n nn variables of degree  k kk.
A Chow decomposition of  f ff is a representation of  f ff as the sum of  r rr polynomials written as a product of  k kk linear forms, i.e.,





 f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(a_{i,j,1}x_{1}+\dots+a_{i,j,n}x_{n}) f⁡(x1,…,xn)=∑i=1r∏j=1k(ai,j,1​x1+⋯+ai,j,n​xn)f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(a_{i,j,1}x_{1}+\dots+a_{i,j,n}x_{n})

(9)


for some  a_{i,j,1},\dots,a_{i,j,n}\in\mathbb{F} ai,j,1,…,ai,j,n∈𝔽a_{i,j,1},\dots,a_{i,j,n}\in\mathbb{F} ( 1\leq i\leq r 1≤i≤r1\leq i\leq r and  1\leq j\leq d 1≤j≤d1\leq j\leq d).
The smallest possible  r rr for which  f ff has an expression of the form given in Equation (9) is called the Chow rank of  f ff.
A low Chow-rank decomposition problem asks to compute a Chow decomposition of a given polynomial  f ff that achieves its Chow rank [65].
In this paper, we consider the completion version, where we are given a partial list of the coefficients of a polynomial  f ff of Chow rank at most  r rr and we are asked to recover  f ff.




Now we reformulate this decomposition or completion problem in terms of a hypergraph realisation problem.
As usual, the list of indices of given coefficients of  f ff is represented by a  k kk-uniform hypergraph on  [n] [n][n].
Consider the right hand side of Equation (9).
Let  Q_{i} QiQ_{i} be the  k\times n k×nk\times n matrix whose  j jj-th row is
 (a_{i,j,1},a_{i,j,2},\dots,a_{i,j,n})\in\mathbb{F}^{n} (ai,j,1,ai,j,2,…,ai,j,n)∈𝔽n(a_{i,j,1},a_{i,j,2},\dots,a_{i,j,n})\in\mathbb{F}^{n}
for each  1\leq i\leq r 1≤i≤r1\leq i\leq r,
and let  P PP be the  kr\times n k​r×nkr\times n matrix obtained by stacking  Q_{i} QiQ_{i}’s.
Let  q_{i}\in(\mathbb{F}^{k})^{n} qi∈(𝔽k)nq_{i}\in(\mathbb{F}^{k})^{n} (and respectively  p\in(\mathbb{F}^{kr})^{n} p∈(𝔽k​r)np\in(\mathbb{F}^{kr})^{n}) be the point configuration such that  q_{i}(v) qi​(v)q_{i}(v) (resp.,  p(v) p⁡(v)p(v)) is the  v vv-th column of  Q_{i} QiQ_{i} (resp.,  P PP).
Then, the coefficient of the squarefree monomial  x_{v_{1}}x_{v_{2}}\dots x_{v_{k}} xv1​xv2​…​xvkx_{v_{1}}x_{v_{2}}\dots x_{v_{k}}
in the right hand side of Equation (9) is equal to





 \sum_{i=1}^{r}\perm\begin{pmatrix}q_{i}(v_{1})\dots q_{i}(v_{k})\end{pmatrix}, ∑i=1rperm⁡(qi​(v1)​…​qi​(vk)),\sum_{i=1}^{r}\perm\begin{pmatrix}q_{i}(v_{1})\dots q_{i}(v_{k})\end{pmatrix},

(10)


where  {\rm perm} perm{\rm perm} denotes the permanent.
Even if  x_{v_{1}}x_{v_{2}}\dots x_{v_{k}} xv1​xv2​…​xvkx_{v_{1}}x_{v_{2}}\dots x_{v_{k}} is not squarefree, the coefficient of  x_{v_{1}}\dots x_{v_{k}} xv1​…​xvkx_{v_{1}}\dots x_{v_{k}} is still a scalar multiple of Equation (10).
Thus, (by scaling coordinates along each axis appropriately) the low Chow-rank completion problem can be reduced to the following hypergraph embedding problem:
Given a  k kk-uniform hypergraph  G=([n],E) G=([n],E)G=([n],E) and  a_{e}\in\mathbb{F} ae∈𝔽a_{e}\in\mathbb{F} for each  e\in E e∈Ee\in E, find an  r rr-tuple  p=(q_{1},\dots,q_{r}) p=(q1,…,qr)p=(q_{1},\dots,q_{r}) of  q_{i}\in(\mathbb{F}^{k})^{V} qi∈(𝔽k)Vq_{i}\in(\mathbb{F}^{k})^{V} such that





 \sum_{i=1}^{r}\perm\begin{pmatrix}q_{i}(v_{1})\dots q_{i}(v_{k})\end{pmatrix}=a_{e}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $e=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in E$}. ∑i=1rperm⁡(qi​(v1)​…​qi​(vk))=aefor e={v1,…,vk}∈E.\sum_{i=1}^{r}\perm\begin{pmatrix}q_{i}(v_{1})\dots q_{i}(v_{k})\end{pmatrix}=a_{e}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $e=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in E$}.

(11)





The corresponding unique completion problem checks if  f ff can be uniquely recovered from a partial list of coefficients using the information that the Chow rank of  f ff is at most  r rr.
Let  h_{\rm perm}:(\mathbb{F}^{k})^{r}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} hperm:(𝔽k)r→𝔽h_{\rm perm}:(\mathbb{F}^{k})^{r}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} be the permanent as a multilinear  k kk-form.
Then the unique completion problem is captured by  g gg-rigidity for  g:(\mathbb{F}^{kr})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽k​r)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{kr})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} defined by the sum of  r rr copies of  h_{\rm perm} hpermh_{\rm perm}.




By [14], the stabilizer  \Gamma_{h_{\rm perm}} Γhperm\Gamma_{h_{\rm perm}} of  h_{\rm perm} hpermh_{\rm perm} is
the set of matrices of the form  \Sigma D Σ​D\Sigma D
with a permutation matrix  \Sigma Σ\Sigma and a diagonal matrix  D DD with  \det D=1 detD=1\det D=1.
The stabilizer of  g gg is  \{(A_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus A_{r})(\Sigma\otimes I_{k}):A_{i}\in\Gamma_{h_{\rm perm}},\Sigma:\text{ a permutation matrix of size $r$}\} {(A1⊕⋯⊕Ar)(Σ⊗Ik):Ai∈Γhperm,Σ: a permutation matrix of size r}\{(A_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus A_{r})(\Sigma\otimes I_{k}):A_{i}\in\Gamma_{h_{\rm perm}},\Sigma:\text{ a permutation matrix of size $r$}\}.






4 Tools for Analysing  g gg-rigidity




In this section, we give several fundamental tools for analysing local/global  g gg-rigidity.
Applications of these tools to specific problems are explained in Section 5.




4.1 Basic facts on polynomial maps




Determining the local or global  g gg-rigidity of a hyper-framework is a NP-hard computational problem. A formal proof of its computational hardness can be found in [1, 56], specifically addressing ordinary Euclidean rigidity. Therefore, a common approach to studying  g gg-rigidity involves analyzing the linear approximation of the concept and leveraging existing tools to fill the remaining gaps.
In this strategy the following facts are often used in graph rigidity theory.



Definition 4.1 (Generic point).



Let  \mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\} 𝔽∈{ℝ,ℂ}\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}.
A point-configuration  p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{n} p∈(𝔽d)np\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{n}
is said to be generic if
the set of coordinates of  p pp is algebraically independent over the rationals.
More generally, a point in an algebraic set  {\cal A} 𝒜{\cal A} defined over  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q} is generic if its coordinates do not satisfy any  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}-polynomial besides those that are satisfied by every point on  {\cal A} 𝒜{\cal A}.





Let  X XX be a smooth manifold and  f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{m} f:X→𝔽mf:X\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{m} be a smooth map. Then  x\in X x∈Xx\in X is
said to be a regular point of  f ff if the Jacobian matrix  {\rm J}f(x) J​f​(x){\rm J}f(x) of  f ff has maximum rank. Otherwise,  x xx is called a critical point of  f ff. Also  f(x) f⁡(x)f(x) is said to be a regular value of  f ff if, for all  y\in f^{-1}(f(x)) y∈f−1​(f​(x))y\in f^{-1}(f(x)),  y yy
is a regular point of  f ff.




The following proposition summarises the basic geometric tools we shall use. All four parts are known in the rigidity literature (see, e.g., [33, 32]) though may not have previously been stated in the full generality of  g gg-rigidity.



Proposition 4.2.



Let  \mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\} 𝔽∈{ℝ,ℂ}\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}.

(i) 



Let  f:\mathbb{F}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{m} f:𝔽n→𝔽mf:\mathbb{F}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{m} be a polynomial map and  p\in\mathbb{F}^{n} p∈𝔽np\in\mathbb{F}^{n}.
If  f(p) f⁡(p)f(p) is a regular value, then  f^{-1}(f(p)) f−1​(f​(p))f^{-1}(f(p)) is a smooth manifold with codimension equal to the  \rank rank\rank of  {\rm J}f(p) J​f​(p){\rm J}f(p), where  {\rm J}f(p) J​f​(p){\rm J}f(p) is the Jacobian derivative of  f ff evaluated at  p pp.

(ii) 



Let  V,W V,WV,W be smooth manifolds,
 f:V\rightarrow W f:V→Wf:V\rightarrow W a surjective  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}-polynomial map
and  p\in V p∈Vp\in V.
If  p pp is generic in  V VV, then  f(p) f⁡(p)f(p) is generic in  W WW.

(iii) 



Let  f:\mathbb{F}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{m} f:𝔽n→𝔽mf:\mathbb{F}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{m} be a  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}-polynomial map
and  p\in\mathbb{F}^{n} p∈𝔽np\in\mathbb{F}^{n} a generic point.
Then,  f(p) f⁡(p)f(p) is generic if and only if  {\rm J}f(p) J​f​(p){\rm J}f(p) is row independent.

(iv) 



Let  f:\mathbb{F}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{m} f:𝔽n→𝔽mf:\mathbb{F}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{m} be a  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}-polynomial map and  p\in\mathbb{F}^{n} p∈𝔽np\in\mathbb{F}^{n} a generic point. Then  f(p) f⁡(p)f(p) is a regular value of  f ff.






Proof.



(i) 



This is simply a consequence of the implicit function theorem, see, e.g.,  [50].
The inverse function theorem states that, if  q\in\mathbb{F}^{n} q∈𝔽nq\in\mathbb{F}^{n} is a regular point, then there is a (Euclidean) neighbourhood  N NN of  q qq such that  f^{-1}(f(q))\cap N f−1​(f​(q))∩Nf^{-1}(f(q))\cap N is a smooth manifold of codimension equal to  \rank{\rm J}f(q) rank⁡J​f​(q)\rank{\rm J}f(q).
But, since  f(p) f⁡(p)f(p) is a regular value, this property holds for any  q\in f^{-1}(f(p)) q∈f−1​(f​(p))q\in f^{-1}(f(p)), meaning that  f^{-1}(f(p)) f−1​(f​(p))f^{-1}(f(p)) is a manifold.

(ii) 



Suppose that there is a polynomial  g gg that is vanishing at  f(p) f⁡(p)f(p). Then  g\circ f:V\rightarrow W g∘f:V→Wg\circ f:V\rightarrow W is a polynomial map.
Since  p pp is generic in  V VV, we have that  g\circ f g∘fg\circ f is vanishing on  V VV.
Since  f ff is surjective, this implies that  g gg is vanishing on  W WW.

(iii) 



Suppose that  {\rm J}f(p) J​f​(p){\rm J}f(p) is row independent.
Then  p pp is a regular point of  f ff, and hence  f({\mathbb{F}}^{n})\cap M f⁡(𝔽n)∩Mf({\mathbb{F}}^{n})\cap M is an  m mm-dimensional manifold in a neighbourhood  M MM of  f(p) f⁡(p)f(p) by the inverse function theorem. This in turn implies that, for a small neighbourhood  V VV of  p pp in  \mathbb{F}^{n} 𝔽n\mathbb{F}^{n}, the restriction  f|_{V} f|Vf|_{V} of  f ff to  V VV is a surjective polynomial map from  V VV to an  m mm-dimensional manifold  f(V) f⁡(V)f(V). Applying (ii) to  f|_{V} f|Vf|_{V}, we have that  f(p) f⁡(p)f(p) is generic in  f(V) f⁡(V)f(V). Since  f(V) f⁡(V)f(V) is  m mm-dimensional, we conclude that  f(p) f⁡(p)f(p) is also generic in  \mathbb{F}^{m} 𝔽m\mathbb{F}^{m}.




Conversely, if  {\rm J}f(p) J​f​(p){\rm J}f(p) is row dependent then, since the rank of  {\rm J}f J​f{\rm J}f is locally constant in a neighbourhood of  p pp, if follows that  f(p) f⁡(p)f(p) lies on a proper algebraic subset of  \mathbb{F}^{m} 𝔽m\mathbb{F}^{m}. Using either the Tarski-Seidenberg Theorem (when  \mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R} 𝔽=ℝ\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}) or Chevalley’s Theorem (when  \mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C} 𝔽=ℂ\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}) we can show that this algebraic subset is defined over  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q} and so  f(p) f⁡(p)f(p) is not generic. (A short exposition on Chevalley’s theorem is given in the proof below.)

(iv) 



Suppose  \mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C} 𝔽=ℂ\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}.
Let  X XX be the set of points  q\in\mathbb{F}^{n} q∈𝔽nq\in\mathbb{F}^{n} such that  f(q) f⁡(q)f(q) is not regular.
Chevalley’s theorem implies that any quantifier can be eliminated in an expression of a set given by boolean operations, quantifiers, and  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}-algebraic equations, see, e.g., [9].
This, in particular, implies that  X XX is a  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}-algebraically constructible set.
On the other hand, Bertini’s theorem states that there is a dense open set  U\subseteq\overline{{\rm im}f} U⊆im​f¯U\subseteq\overline{{\rm im}f} in which every point is regular, see, e.g., [58]. Since  \overline{{\rm im}f} im​f¯\overline{{\rm im}f} is irreducible,  U^{c} UcU^{c} is a lower dimensional algebraic subset of  \overline{{\rm im}f} im​f¯\overline{{\rm im}f}. Since  f(X)=U^{c} f⁡(X)=Ucf(X)=U^{c},  X XX must be a lower-dimensional  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}-algebraically constructible set in  \mathbb{C}^{n} ℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}, and the genericity of  p pp implies  p\notin X p∉Xp\notin X.




When  \mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R} 𝔽=ℝ\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}, we can apply the same argument by using Tarski-Seidenberg quantifier elimination (see, e.g., [9]) instead of Chevalley’s theorem.



∎






4.2 Infinitesimal  g gg-rigidity




Let  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) be a  k kk-uniform hyper-framework
with  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) and  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} a polynomial map.
Then the Jacobian  {\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) J​fg,G​(p){\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) of the measurement map  f_{g,G}:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{E} fg,G:(𝔽d)V→𝔽Ef_{g,G}:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{E}
is a linear map from  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} (𝔽d)V(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} to  \mathbb{F}^{E} 𝔽E\mathbb{F}^{E}.
Hence the right kernel of  {\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) J​fg,G​(p){\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) is a linear subspace  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} (𝔽d)V(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}, which is defined as the space of infinitesimal  g gg-motions of the framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p).




If the stabiliser  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} of  g gg is positive dimensional,
every framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) has a nonzero infinitesimal  g gg-motion.
This fact can be seen as follows.
Recall that  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} is a Lie group,
so let  \mathfrak{g} 𝔤\mathfrak{g} be the Lie algebra of  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g}.
Observe that  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} acts on  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} (𝔽d)V(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} via  (\gamma\cdot p)(v)=\gamma\cdot(p(v)) (γ⋅p)​(v)=γ⋅(p⁡(v))(\gamma\cdot p)(v)=\gamma\cdot(p(v)) for  v\in V v∈Vv\in V. This induces a map from  \mathfrak{g}\times(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} 𝔤×(𝔽d)V\mathfrak{g}\times(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} to  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} (𝔽d)V(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}, denoted  (\dot{\gamma},p)\mapsto\dot{\gamma}\cdot p (γ˙,p)↦γ˙⋅p(\dot{\gamma},p)\mapsto\dot{\gamma}\cdot p, whose image lies in the right kernel of  {\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) J​fg,G​(p){\rm J}f_{g,G}(p).
Then  \dot{p}\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} p˙∈(𝔽d)V\dot{p}\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} defined by
 \dot{p}=\dot{\gamma}\cdot p p˙=γ˙⋅p\dot{p}=\dot{\gamma}\cdot p for  \dot{\gamma}\in\mathfrak{g} γ˙∈𝔤\dot{\gamma}\in\mathfrak{g} is called a trivial infinitesimal  g gg-motion of  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p).
For  p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{n} p∈(𝔽d)np\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{n}, define the space of trivial
infinitesimal motions of  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) to be





 {\rm triv}_{g}(p)=\{\dot{\gamma}\cdot p:\dot{\gamma}\in\mathfrak{g}\}. trivg​(p)={γ˙⋅p:γ˙∈𝔤}.{\rm triv}_{g}(p)=\{\dot{\gamma}\cdot p:\dot{\gamma}\in\mathfrak{g}\}.



Note that  {\rm triv}_{g}(p)\subseteq\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) trivg​(p)⊆ker⁡J​fg,G​(p){\rm triv}_{g}(p)\subseteq\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p).



Definition 4.3.



Let  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) be a  k kk-uniform hyper-framework and  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} a polynomial map. We say that  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid if
 {\rm triv}_{g}(p)=\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) trivg​(p)=ker⁡J​fg,G​(p){\rm triv}_{g}(p)=\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p).





The following proposition generalises the fundamental theorem of Asimow and Roth [7] which provides the central motivation to look at infinitesimally rigidity in graph rigidity theory.



Proposition 4.4.



Let  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} be a  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}-polynomial map
and  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) be a  k kk-uniform hyper-framework.

• 



If  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid, then it is locally  g gg-rigid.

• 



If  p pp is generic, then  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid if and only if it is locally  g gg-rigid.






Proof.



Let  T_{p}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} Tp:𝔤→(𝔽d)VT_{p}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} be the linear map  \dot{\gamma}\mapsto\dot{\gamma}\cdot p γ˙↦γ˙⋅p\dot{\gamma}\mapsto\dot{\gamma}\cdot p. Then
 \dim({\rm triv}_{g}(p)) dim(trivg​(p))\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(p)) =  \rank(T_{p}) rank⁡(Tp)\rank(T_{p}) is a lower semicontinuous function of  p pp. It follows that there is a neighbourhood  U UU of  p pp such that,
for  q\in U q∈Uq\in U,  \dim({\rm triv}_{g}(q))\geq\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(p)) dim(trivg​(q))≥dim(trivg​(p))\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(q))\geq\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(p)).
Also for some neighbourhood  W WW of  p pp we have  \rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(q))\geq\rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)) rank⁡(J​fg,G​(q))≥rank⁡(J​fg,G​(p))\rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(q))\geq\rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)) for  q\in W q∈Wq\in W, again by the semicontinuity of rank.




If  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid
then, by definition,  \rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(p))=d|V|–\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(p)) rank⁡(J​fg,G​(p))=d​|V|​–​dim(trivg​(p))\rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(p))=d|V|–\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(p)).
Therefore, for  q\in U\cap W q∈U∩Wq\in U\cap W, we have  \rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(q))\geq\rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(p))=d|V|–\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(p))\geq d|V|–\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(q))\geq\rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(q)) rank⁡(J​fg,G​(q))≥rank⁡(J​fg,G​(p))=d​|V|​–​dim(trivg​(p))≥d|V|–​dim(trivg​(q))≥rank⁡(J​fg,G​(q))\rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(q))\geq\rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(p))=d|V|–\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(p))\geq d|V|–\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(q))\geq\rank({\rm J}f_{g,G}(q)).
So we have equality throughout and we can apply the constant rank theorem to deduce that, in a neighbourhood of  p pp,  f_{g,G} fg,Gf_{g,G} is locally equivalent (i.e. up to a choice of coordinates) to a coordinate projection from  \mathbb{F}^{d|V|}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{d|V|-\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(p))} 𝔽d​|V|→𝔽d​|V|−dim(trivg​(p))\mathbb{F}^{d|V|}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{d|V|-\dim({\rm triv}_{g}(p))}. It follows that  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is locally  g gg-rigid. This proves the first statement.




The second statement, that generic locally  g gg-rigid hyper-frameworks are infinitesimally  g gg-rigid, follows from
Proposition 4.2(i).
∎





We now compare the dimension of  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} and the dimension of the space of all trivial infinitesimal  g gg-motions.
Define  d_{\Gamma_{g}} dΓgd_{\Gamma_{g}} to be the dimension of  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} as a Lie group.
Since  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} is a Lie subgroup of  {\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) Aff⁡(d,𝔽){\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}), if  p pp is generic and  n nn is sufficiently large, then  \dim{\rm triv}_{g}(p)=d_{\Gamma_{g}} dimtrivg​(p)=dΓg\dim{\rm triv}_{g}(p)=d_{\Gamma_{g}}.
We use  n_{\Gamma_{g}} nΓgn_{\Gamma_{g}} to denote the minimum integer  n nn such that  \dim{\rm triv}_{g}(p)=d_{\Gamma_{g}} dimtrivg​(p)=dΓg\dim{\rm triv}_{g}(p)=d_{\Gamma_{g}} for some  p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{n} p∈(𝔽d)np\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{n}.



Example 4.5.



The following presents two specific instances that exemplify these definitions.

• 



Suppose  g:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} g:(ℝd)2→ℝg:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} is the Euclidean inner product in  \mathbb{R}^{d} ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Then  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} is the orthogonal group  O(d) O⁡(d)O(d), so  d_{\Gamma_{g}}={d\choose 2} dΓg=(d2)d_{\Gamma_{g}}={d\choose 2}.
The Lie algebra  \mathfrak{g} 𝔤\mathfrak{g} is the set of skew-symmetric matrices,
and  \dim{\rm triv}_{g}(p)={d\choose 2}-{d-n\choose 2} dimtrivg​(p)=(d2)−(d−n2)\dim{\rm triv}_{g}(p)={d\choose 2}-{d-n\choose 2}
if  p pp is a generic set of  n nn points with  n\leq d n≤dn\leq d.
Hence,  n_{\Gamma_{g}}=d-1 nΓg=d−1n_{\Gamma_{g}}=d-1.

Suppose  g:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} g:(ℂd)k→ℂg:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} is as in Equation (6).
Then  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} is the set of matrices of the form  \Gamma D Γ​D\Gamma D with a permutation matrix  \Gamma Γ\Gamma and a diagonal matrix  D DD whose diagonal entries are  k kk-th roots of unity.
Then  d_{\Gamma_{g}}=0 dΓg=0d_{\Gamma_{g}}=0 and the Lie algebra is trivial.
So  n_{\Gamma_{g}}=0 nΓg=0n_{\Gamma_{g}}=0.







Since  {\rm triv}_{g}(p) trivg​(p){\rm triv}_{g}(p) is the space of trivial infinitesimal  g gg-motions, if  |V(G)|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} |V⁡(G)|≥nΓg|V(G)|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} and  p pp is generic, then Definition 4.3 implies that  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid if and only if  \dim\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)=d_{\Gamma_{g}} dimker⁡J​fg,G​(p)=dΓg\dim\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)=d_{\Gamma_{g}}.
Combining this with Proposition 4.4, we have the following linear algebraic characterisation of generic local  g gg-rigidity.



Proposition 4.6.

The following geometric implication of local  g gg-rigidity is also important.



Proposition 4.8.



Let  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} be a polynomial map whose stabiliser is  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g}.
Suppose  p pp is generic and  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) is a  k kk-uniform hypergraph with  |V|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+d_{\Gamma_{g}} |V|≥nΓg+dΓg|V|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+d_{\Gamma_{g}}.
Then  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is locally  g gg-rigid
if and only if  f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma_{g} fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))/Γgf^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma_{g} is finite.




Proof.



Suppose  n=|V| n=|V|n=|V| and  q\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p)) q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))q\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p)). Recall that  {\rm triv}_{g}(q)=\{\dot{\gamma}\cdot p:\dot{\gamma}\in\mathfrak{g}\} trivg​(q)={γ˙⋅p:γ˙∈𝔤}{\rm triv}_{g}(q)=\{\dot{\gamma}\cdot p:\dot{\gamma}\in\mathfrak{g}\}.
We first show that





 \dim{\rm triv}_{g}(q)=d_{\Gamma_{g}}. dimtrivg​(q)=dΓg.\dim{\rm triv}_{g}(q)=d_{\Gamma_{g}}.

(12)


For a vector  x xx over  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}, let
 \mathbb{Q}(x) ℚ⁡(x)\mathbb{Q}(x) be the field extension of  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q} generated by the entries of  x xx.
By Proposition 4.2(iii),
the transcendence degree of  \mathbb{Q}(f_{g,G}(p)) ℚ​(fg,G​(p))\mathbb{Q}(f_{g,G}(p)) over  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q} is  dn-d_{\Gamma_{g}} d​n−dΓgdn-d_{\Gamma_{g}}.
Since  f_{g,G}(p)=f_{g,G}(q) fg,G​(p)=fg,G​(q)f_{g,G}(p)=f_{g,G}(q),
the transcendence degree of  \mathbb{Q}(f_{g,G}(q)) ℚ​(fg,G​(q))\mathbb{Q}(f_{g,G}(q)) over  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q} is also  dn-d_{\Gamma_{g}} d​n−dΓgdn-d_{\Gamma_{g}}.
Hence the transcendence degree of  \mathbb{Q}(q) ℚ⁡(q)\mathbb{Q}(q) over  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q} is at least  dn-d_{\Gamma_{g}} d​n−dΓgdn-d_{\Gamma_{g}}.
Therefore, since  n\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+d_{\Gamma_{g}} n≥nΓg+dΓgn\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+d_{\Gamma_{g}},
there is a vertex subset  X\subset V X⊂VX\subset V such that  |X|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} |X|≥nΓg|X|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} and  q|_{X} q|Xq|_{X} is generic.11
                  1
                  
                  
                  
                Formally the existence of  X XX can be seen by the following algorithm.
First, set  X=\emptyset X=∅X=\emptyset and
repeat the following procedure from  i=1 i=1i=1 through  n nn.
Check if the entries of  q(X\cup\{i\}) q⁡(X∪{i})q(X\cup\{i\}) are algebraically independent over  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}. If yes, update  X XX to  X\cup\{i\} X∪{i}X\cup\{i\}; otherwise reject  i ii.
Since the transcendence degree of  \mathbb{Q}(q) ℚ⁡(q)\mathbb{Q}(q) over  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q} is at least  dn-d_{\Gamma_{g}} d​n−dΓgdn-d_{\Gamma_{g}},
the fact that algebraic independence defines a matroid implies that the number of rejections in the above procedure is at most  d_{\Gamma_{g}} dΓgd_{\Gamma_{g}}.
Since  n\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+d_{\Gamma_{g}} n≥nΓg+dΓgn\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+d_{\Gamma_{g}}, we have
 |X|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} |X|≥nΓg|X|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} at the end of the procedure.

Hence,  \dim{\rm triv}_{g}(q_{|X})=d_{\Gamma_{g}} dimtrivg(q|X)=dΓg\dim{\rm triv}_{g}(q_{|X})=d_{\Gamma_{g}},
and Equation (12) follows.




Parts (i) and (iv) of Proposition 4.2 imply that
 f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) is a smooth manifold
of dimension equal to  \dim\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) dimker⁡J​fg,G​(p)\dim\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p).
On the other hand, Equation (12) implies that, for any  q\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p)) q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))q\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p)),
 \Gamma_{g}\cdot q=\{\gamma\cdot q:\gamma\in\Gamma_{g}\} Γg⋅q={γ⋅q:γ∈Γg}\Gamma_{g}\cdot q=\{\gamma\cdot q:\gamma\in\Gamma_{g}\} is a  d_{\Gamma_{g}} dΓgd_{\Gamma_{g}}-dimensional smooth submanifold of  f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)).

 \dim\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)-d_{\Gamma_{g}} dimker⁡J​fg,G​(p)−dΓg\dim\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)-d_{\Gamma_{g}}.
By Proposition 4.4, the latter value is zero if and only if  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is locally  g gg-rigid.
Hence, if  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is not locally  g gg-rigid,
then  f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma_{g} fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))/Γgf_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma_{g} is not finite.




Conversely, if  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is locally  g gg-rigid, then
the dimension of  f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) is  d_{\Gamma_{g}} dΓgd_{\Gamma_{g}}.
