Since  f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) is also an algebraic variety, it has finitely many components and hence
 f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma_{g} fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))/Γgf_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma_{g} is finite.
∎





In the context of tensor completion,  g gg is defined as in Equation (6) and  d_{\Gamma_{g}}=n_{\Gamma_{g}}=0 dΓg=nΓg=0d_{\Gamma_{g}}=n_{\Gamma_{g}}=0.
As a consequence, Proposition 4.8 holds unconditionally, independent of the number of vertices. In general we believe that the assumption  |V|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+d_{\Gamma_{g}} |V|≥nΓg+dΓg|V|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+d_{\Gamma_{g}} in Proposition 4.8 can be eliminated.
Conversely, the requirement of genericity for the point configuration  p pp is crucial.
To illustrate this significance, an example showcasing non-finite  f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma_{g} fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))/Γgf_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma_{g} is provided in Figure 4 within the realm of ordinary Euclidean rigidity.


Figure 4: Consider the ordinary Euclidean rigidity in the plane.
(Left) a locally  g gg-rigid framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p).
(Right) the framework  (G,q) (G,q)(G,q) satisfies
 f_{g,G}(p)=f_{g,G}(q) fg,G​(p)=fg,G​(q)f_{g,G}(p)=f_{g,G}(q) but  (G,q) (G,q)(G,q) is not locally  g gg-rigid
since the left path on five vertices in the framework can move continuously without changing the edge lengths.
So  f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma_{g} fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))/Γgf_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma_{g} is not finite but  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is locally  g gg-rigid.





4.3 Generic local  g gg-rigidity and coordinate projections of affine varieties




Let  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) be a  k kk-uniform hypergraph,
and consider  \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} im​fg,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}}, that is, the Zariski closure of the image of the  g gg-measurement map  f_{g,G} fg,Gf_{g,G} in the affine space  \mathbb{F}^{E} 𝔽E\mathbb{F}^{E}.
If  p pp is generic, then  f_{g,G}(p) fg,G​(p)f_{g,G}(p) is a generic point in  \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} im​fg,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}}, and hence
 \rank{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) rank⁡J​fg,G​(p)\rank{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) coincides with the dimension of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} im​fg,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}}.
Since the latter value is determined by  G GG (and independent of  p pp), we can define the local  g gg-rigidity as a property of hypergraphs (using Proposition 4.6).
This extends the remarkable idea of Asimov and Roth [7] that used this property as the definition of rigidity in the Euclidean distance case.




Specifically, a hypergraph  G GG with  n=|V| n=|V|n=|V| vertices is said to be locally  g gg-rigid if
 (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid for some/all generic  p pp,
and equivalently





 \dim\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}}=dn-d_{\Gamma_{g}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{if\  }n\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}. dimim​fg,G¯=d​n−dΓgif ​n≥nΓg.\dim\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}}=dn-d_{\Gamma_{g}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{if\  }n\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}.






It is often convenient to analyse each hypergraph as a subgraph of the complete  k kk-uniform hypergraph  K_{n}^{k} KnkK_{n}^{k} with the same vertex set. For this, suppose  G=([n],E) G=([n],E)G=([n],E) is a subgraph of  K_{n}^{k} KnkK_{n}^{k}.
Let  \pi_{G}:\mathbb{F}^{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{E} πG:𝔽{[n]k}→𝔽E\pi_{G}:\mathbb{F}^{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{E} be the coordinate projection to the subspace indexed by the hyperedges in  G GG.
Then,





 f_{g,G}=\pi_{G}\circ f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}. fg,G=πG∘fg,Knk.f_{g,G}=\pi_{G}\circ f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}.



Hence, checking the local  g gg-rigidity of  G GG is equivalent to checking the equality:





 \dim\overline{\pi_{G}({\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}})}=dn-d_{\Gamma_{g}}. dimπG​(im​fg,Knk)¯=d​n−dΓg.\dim\overline{\pi_{G}({\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}})}=dn-d_{\Gamma_{g}}.



One notable advantage of this viewpoint is that
 \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}} im​fg,Knk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}} is often well-investigated in the literature, which allows us to leverage established tools.
For instance, in the case where  g gg represents the squared distance function,
 \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}} im​fg,Knk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}} is known as the Cayley-Menger variety
[12].
Similarly, when  g gg is the product map,
 \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}} im​fg,Knk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}} coincides with the affine cone of the Veronese variety.
We will discuss these examples in more detail in Section 5.





4.4 Secant, non-defectivity, and identifiability of varieties




Let  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} be a homogeneous affine variety in  \mathbb{C}^{m} ℂm\mathbb{C}^{m}.
Its  r rr-secant is defined as





 {\rm Sect}_{r}({\cal V})=\overline{\bigcup_{x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\in{\cal V}}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\rangle}. Sectr​(𝒱)=⋃x1,…,xr∈𝒱⟨x1,…,xr⟩¯.{\rm Sect}_{r}({\cal V})=\overline{\bigcup_{x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\in{\cal V}}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\rangle}.

(13)


In applications, it is often the case that a homogeneous polynomial map  g:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} g:(ℂd)k→ℂg:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} is written as the sum of polynomial maps  h:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} h:(ℂs)k→ℂh:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} for  s<d s<ds<d. In such a case, the concept of  g gg-rigidity is closely related to the non-defectivity and identifiability of varieties.




To formally establish this connection, let us consider a polynomial map  g:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} g:(ℂd)k→ℂg:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.
The argument of  g gg is a tuple  (p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{k}) (p1,p2,…,pk)(p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{k}) consisting of  k kk points  p_{i} pip_{i} in  \mathbb{C}^{d} ℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}. We denote the coordinates of each  p_{i} pip_{i} by  p_{i}=(p_{i1},p_{i2},\dots,p_{id})^{\top} pi=(pi​1,pi​2,…,pi​d)⊤p_{i}=(p_{i1},p_{i2},\dots,p_{id})^{\top}.
Suppose  g gg is separable into coordinate-wise maps, that is,





 g(p_{1},\dots,p_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}h(p_{1i},\dots,p_{ki}) g⁡(p1,…,pk)=∑i=1dh⁡(p1​i,…,pk​i)g(p_{1},\dots,p_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}h(p_{1i},\dots,p_{ki})



for some  h:(\mathbb{C}^{1})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} h:(ℂ1)k→ℂh:(\mathbb{C}^{1})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}. In this case, it follows that  \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} im​fg,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} is the  d dd-secant of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}}.
We need slightly more involved notation to deal with the case when  g\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k} g∈(ℂd)kg\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k} is decomposed into the sum of copies of  h\in(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k} h∈(ℂs)kh\in(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}. (Such situations appear in skew-symmetric completions and Chow decompositions given in Section 3.)
Specifically, consider the identification between  (\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k} (ℂd)k(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k} and  ((\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k})^{t} ((ℂs)k)t((\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k})^{t} with  st=d s​t=dst=d such that
 p\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k} p∈(ℂd)kp\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k} is identified with a  t tt-tuple  (q_{1},\dots,q_{t}) (q1,…,qt)(q_{1},\dots,q_{t}) of  q_{i}\in(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k} qi∈(ℂs)kq_{i}\in(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}.
If  g gg is written as





 g(p)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}h(q_{i}) g⁡(p)=∑i=1th⁡(qi)g(p)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}h(q_{i})

(14)


for some  h:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} h:(ℂs)k→ℂh:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}, then  \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} im​fg,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} is the  t tt-secant of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}}.
In such a case, we simply say that  g gg is the sum of  t tt copies of  h hh.




Computing the dimension of secant varieties has been extensively studied in algebraic geometry (see, e.g., [10, 69]).
In particular, the algebraic notions of non-defectivity and identifiability of these varieties are closely related to the notion of  g gg-rigidity.
Although these concepts are typically defined for projective varieties, we maintain our focus on affine varieties to preserve the connection with rigidity theory.



Non-defectivity and local  g gg-rigidity.




Given an affine variety  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} in  \mathbb{C}^{m} ℂm\mathbb{C}^{m}, the  r rr-th secant of  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} has dimension at most  \min\{r\dim{\cal V},m\} min⁡{r​dim𝒱,m}\min\{r\dim{\cal V},m\}. The latter number is called the expected dimension, and  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} is said to be  r rr-defective if  \dim{\rm Sect}_{r}({\cal V}) dimSectr​(𝒱)\dim{\rm Sect}_{r}({\cal V}) is smaller than the expected dimension.




Assume Setup 2.1, and suppose further that  g gg is the sum of  t tt copies of  h hh.
If  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} is not  t tt-defective, then  \dim(\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}})=\min\{t\dim(\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}}),|E|\} dim(im​fg,G¯)=min⁡{t​dim(im​fh,G¯),|E|}\dim(\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}})=\min\{t\dim(\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}}),|E|\},
and hence checking local  g gg-rigidity is reduced to checking local  h hh-rigidity.
The defectivity of classical algebraic varieties such as Veronese, Segre, and Grassmannian varieties has been extensively studied (see, e.g., [10]). In the classical setting, the underlying hypergraphs of such varieties are always complete, and hence we can apply the existing results to prove the local  g gg-rigidity of complete hypergraphs.
In particular, when the underlying hypergraph is complete, then the notion of local  g gg-rigidity often coincides with the classical notion of non-defectivity of the corresponding secant varieties. See Section 5 for concrete examples.




However, for the analysis of general hypergraphs, we need to understand the influence of coordinate projections on defectivity.
Proposition 4.12, below, shows that the dimension for a general hypergraph can be much smaller than the expected dimension for combinatorial reasons. Therefore, we focus
on the combinatorial properties of the underlying hypergraphs.




It is also important to point out that our primal applications include the case when  g gg and  h hh are quadratic. In such cases,  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} will always be defective whereas  G GG can be still locally  g gg-rigid.
So the non-defectivity and local  g gg-rigidity are different notions in general.
An example is described below.




Tangent spaces.




Suppose  g gg is the sum of  t tt copies of  h hh.
Then, by arranging the column ordering appropriately, we have





 {\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm J}f_{h,G}(q_{1})\dots{\rm J}f_{h,G}(q_{t})\end{pmatrix}. J​fg,G​(p)=(J​fh,G​(q1)​…​J​fh,G​(qt)).{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm J}f_{h,G}(q_{1})\dots{\rm J}f_{h,G}(q_{t})\end{pmatrix}.

(15)


Hence the tangent space of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} im​fg,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} at a generic point  f_{g,G}(p) fg,G​(p)f_{g,G}(p) is the linear span of
those of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} at
 f_{h,G}(q_{1}),\dots,f_{h,G}(q_{t}) fh,G​(q1),…,fh,G​(qt)f_{h,G}(q_{1}),\dots,f_{h,G}(q_{t}).
This fact corresponds to the Teraccini lemma in algebraic geometry (see, e.g., [10]).




Identifiability and global  g gg-rigidity.




Suppose  g gg is homogeneous.
For a variety  {{\mathcal{V}}} 𝒱{{\mathcal{V}}} in  \mathbb{C}^{m} ℂm\mathbb{C}^{m}, a generic point  y\in{\rm Sect}_{r}({{\mathcal{V}}}) y∈Sectr​(𝒱)y\in{\rm Sect}_{r}({{\mathcal{V}}}) can be written as
 y=\sum_{i=1}^{r}y_{i} y=∑i=1ryiy=\sum_{i=1}^{r}y_{i} for some  y_{i}\in{{\mathcal{V}}} yi∈𝒱y_{i}\in{{\mathcal{V}}}.
The variety  {\mathcal{V}} 𝒱{\mathcal{V}} is said to be  r rr-identifiable if  y_{1},\ldots,y_{r} y1,…,yry_{1},\ldots,y_{r} are uniquely determined up to permutations of indices and scaling of each  y_{i} yiy_{i}.
As we will see in Section 7, identifiability is a useful property for checking global  g gg-rigidity. For example, in the case of symmetric tensors, a complete hypergraph is globally  g gg-rigid if and only if the corresponding secant variety is identifiable. However, in general, global  g gg-rigidity and identifiability are not comparable. We now provide two examples to demonstrate this.




Example.




To see the difference among local/global rigidity, defectiveness, and identifiability,
we consider the symmetric rank- r rr matrix completion of order  k kk. In this case
 g:(\mathbb{C}^{r})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} g:(ℂr)k→ℂg:(\mathbb{C}^{r})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} is the sum of  r rr copies of the product map
 h_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} hprod:ℂk→ℂh_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.
Then
 \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} im​fg,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} is the  r rr-secant of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},G}} im​fhprod,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},G}}.




• 



Suppose  k=2 k=2k=2.
This case corresponds to the rank- r rr matrix completion.
It is known (see, e.g., [59]) that
 {\rm J}f_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n}}=n J​fhprod,Kn=n{\rm J}f_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n}}=n and  {\rm J}f_{g,K_{n}}=rn-{r\choose 2} J​fg,Kn=r​n−(r2){\rm J}f_{g,K_{n}}=rn-{r\choose 2}.
So  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n}}} im​fhprod,Kn¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n}}}
is  r rr-defective and not  r rr-identifiable.
However, since  d_{\Gamma_{g}}={r\choose 2} dΓg=(r2)d_{\Gamma_{g}}={r\choose 2},
 K_{n} KnK_{n} is locally  g gg-rigid by Proposition 4.6.
In fact,  K_{n} KnK_{n} is known to be globally  g gg-rigid, see, e.g. [59].

• 



Suppose  r=1 r=1r=1.
Then
 \overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},G}} im​fhprod,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},G}} is  1 11-identifiable by definition.
However, the local rigidity and the global  g gg-rigidity of a generic framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) depends on the combinatorial structure of  G GG. Indeed the global  g gg-rigidity captures the uniqueness of symmetric rank-one tensor completions. So, if  G GG is very sparse,  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) cannot be globally  g gg-rigid.








4.5 Generic  g gg-rigidity matroids




Our goal in this subsection is to establish a connection between  g gg-rigidity and combinatorial properties of hypergraphs. For this purpose, we formulate our problem using the framework of algebraic matroids.



Definition 4.9.



For a  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}-polynomial map  f:\mathbb{F}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{m} f:𝔽n→𝔽mf:\mathbb{F}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{m}, consider the following collections of subsets of  [m] [m][m]:

1. 



For a generic  p\in\mathbb{F}^{n} p∈𝔽np\in\mathbb{F}^{n},





 \mathcal{I}_{1}=\{S\subseteq[m]:\text{the rows of the Jacobian matrix }{\rm J}f(p)\text{ indexed by $S$ are independent}\}. ℐ1={S⊆[m]:the rows of the Jacobian matrix ​J​f​(p)​ indexed by S are independent}.\mathcal{I}_{1}=\{S\subseteq[m]:\text{the rows of the Jacobian matrix }{\rm J}f(p)\text{ indexed by $S$ are independent}\}.



2. 



Let  V_{f}={\rm im}f Vf=im​fV_{f}={\rm im}f and  I(V_{f}) I⁡(Vf)I(V_{f}) be the associated ideal of the variety  \overline{{\rm im}f} im​f¯\overline{{\rm im}f}, and





 \mathcal{I}_{2}=\{S\subseteq[m]:I(V_{f})\cap\mathbb{F}[S]=\{0\}\}. ℐ2={S⊆[m]:I⁡(Vf)∩𝔽⁡[S]={0}}.\mathcal{I}_{2}=\{S\subseteq[m]:I(V_{f})\cap\mathbb{F}[S]=\{0\}\}.



3. 



Let  \pi_{S} πS\pi_{S} be the coordinate projection to the subspace indexed by  S\subseteq[m] S⊆[m]S\subseteq[m], and let





 \mathcal{I}_{3}=\{S\subseteq[m]:\overline{\pi_{S}(V_{f})}=\mathbb{F}^{S}\}. ℐ3={S⊆[m]:πS​(Vf)¯=𝔽S}.\mathcal{I}_{3}=\{S\subseteq[m]:\overline{\pi_{S}(V_{f})}=\mathbb{F}^{S}\}.





The pair  ([m],\mathcal{I}_{1}) ([m],ℐ1)([m],\mathcal{I}_{1}) is called the algebraic matroid of  f ff. It is known that  \mathcal{I}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{2}=\mathcal{I}_{3} ℐ1=ℐ2=ℐ3\mathcal{I}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{2}=\mathcal{I}_{3} (see, for example, [54] which also provides a detailed exposition on algebraic matroids).
It is also known that  \mathcal{I}_{i} ℐi\mathcal{I}_{i} is invariant over the choice of  \mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\} 𝔽∈{ℝ,ℂ}\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}, which underlines the robustness of algebraic matroids in capturing relevant properties of the corresponding function  f ff.




Definition 4.10.



For a  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}-polynomial map  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} and a positive integer  n nn, the algebraic matroid of  f_{g,K_{n}^{k}} fg,Knkf_{g,K_{n}^{k}} is called the  g gg-rigidity matroid  {\cal M}_{g,n} ℳg,n{\cal M}_{g,n} (or simply  {\cal M}_{g} ℳg{\cal M}_{g} if  n nn is not important).





The  g gg-rigidity matroid is the matroid on the hyperedges of  K_{n}^{k} KnkK_{n}^{k}.





4.6 Combinatorial characterisation problem




Given that generic  g gg-rigidity is a property of hypergraphs, it is natural to ask whether this property can be characterised purely in terms of graph theory concepts. This pursuit of a combinatorial characterisation forms a central theme within the field of graph rigidity.
The most famous example is the following Geiringer-Laman theorem [45, 51].



Theorem 4.11.



Suppose  g gg is the squared distance function in two-dimensional Euclidean space. Then  G GG is  g gg-rigid if and only if  G GG has a subgraph  H HH satisfying the following conditions:

• 



 |E(H)|=2|V(G)|-3 |E⁡(H)|=2​|V⁡(G)|−3|E(H)|=2|V(G)|-3, and

• 



for any subgraph  H^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime}) H′=(V′,E′)H^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime}) of  H HH with  |E^{\prime}|\geq 1 |E′|≥1|E^{\prime}|\geq 1,  |E^{\prime}|\leq 2|V^{\prime}|-3 |E′|≤2​|V′|−3|E^{\prime}|\leq 2|V^{\prime}|-3.







Inspired by the goal of extending the aforementioned result to encompass general polynomial maps  g gg and hypergraphs, we review a construction of combinatorial matroids on hypergraphs.




Let  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) be a  k kk-uniform hypergraph.
For nonnegative integers  a aa and  b bb, we say that  G GG is  (a,b) (a,b)(a,b)-sparse if
every sub-hypergraph  G^{\prime} G′G^{\prime} with  E(G^{\prime})\neq\emptyset E⁡(G′)≠∅E(G^{\prime})\neq\emptyset satisfies  |E(G^{\prime})|\leq a|V(G^{\prime})|-b |E⁡(G′)|≤a​|V⁡(G′)|−b|E(G^{\prime})|\leq a|V(G^{\prime})|-b,
and an  (a,b) (a,b)(a,b)-sparse  G GG is said to be  (a,b) (a,b)(a,b)-tight if  |E|=a|V|-b |E|=a​|V|−b|E|=a|V|-b.
It is known that, if  0\leq b\leq ak-1 0≤b≤a​k−10\leq b\leq ak-1, the collection of edge sets of all  (a,b) (a,b)(a,b)-sparse sub-hypergraphs of  K_{n}^{k} KnkK_{n}^{k} forms the independent set of a matroid on  E EE
(see, e.g., [68]),
which is called the  (a,b) (a,b)(a,b)-sparsity matroid  {\cal S}^{a,b}_{n} 𝒮na,b{\cal S}^{a,b}_{n}.




Consider a polynomial map  g gg and a hypergraph  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) with  G\subseteq K_{n}^{k} G⊆KnkG\subseteq K_{n}^{k}.
By Proposition 4.6, if  E EE is independent in the  g gg-rigidity matroid  {\cal M}_{g} ℳg{\cal M}_{g},





 |E^{\prime}|\leq d|V^{\prime}|-d_{\Gamma_{g}} |E′|≤d​|V′|−dΓg|E^{\prime}|\leq d|V^{\prime}|-d_{\Gamma_{g}}



for any subgraph  G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime}) G′=(V′,E′)G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime}) of  G GG with  |V^{\prime}|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} |V′|≥nΓg|V^{\prime}|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}.
If  dk-d_{\Gamma_{g}}\geq 1 d​k−dΓg≥1dk-d_{\Gamma_{g}}\geq 1, the  (a,b) (a,b)(a,b)-sparsity matroid  {\cal S}^{a,b}_{n} 𝒮na,b{\cal S}^{a,b}_{n} is nontrivial, and its rank gives a combinatorially expected rank of  E EE in the  g gg-rigidity matroid. It is known that the rank of  E EE in  {\cal S}^{a,b}_{n} 𝒮na,b{\cal S}^{a,b}_{n} is given by





 \min\left\{|F_{0}|+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(a\left|\bigcup_{e\in F_{i}}e\right|-b\right)\ \Big|\ \begin{array}[]{l}F_{0}\subseteq E\text{ and }\{F_{1},\dots,F_{k}\}\text{ is a partition of $E\setminus F_{0}$}\end{array}\right\}, min⁡{|F0|+∑i=1k(a​|⋃e∈Fie|−b)|F0⊆E​ and ​{F1,…,Fk}​ is a partition of E∖F0},\min\left\{|F_{0}|+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(a\left|\bigcup_{e\in F_{i}}e\right|-b\right)\ \Big|\ \begin{array}[]{l}F_{0}\subseteq E\text{ and }\{F_{1},\dots,F_{k}\}\text{ is a partition of $E\setminus F_{0}$}\end{array}\right\},

(16)


see, e.g., [57].
Hence, we have the following “combinatorially expected dimension” of  \overline{\pi_{G}({\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}})} πG​(im​fg,Knk)¯\overline{\pi_{G}({\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}})},
which can be strictly smaller than a trivial expected dimension in the context of secant varieties.



Proposition 4.12.



Let  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} be a polynomial map, and let  r_{g,n} rg,nr_{g,n} be the rank function of  {\cal M}_{g,n} ℳg,n{\cal M}_{g,n}.
Suppose that  k\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} k≥nΓgk\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} and  dk-d_{\Gamma_{g}}\geq 1 d​k−dΓg≥1dk-d_{\Gamma_{g}}\geq 1.
Then, for any  k kk-uniform hypergraph  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E),





 \dim(\overline{\pi_{G}({\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}}))=r_{g,n}(E)\leq\min\left\{|F_{0}|+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(d\left|\bigcup_{e\in F_{i}}e\right|-d_{\Gamma_{g}}\right)\ \Big|\ \begin{array}[]{l}F_{0}\subseteq E\text{ and }\{F_{1},\dots,F_{k}\}\\
\text{ is a partition of $E\setminus F_{0}$}\end{array}\right\}. OPENdim(πG​(im​fg,KnkCLOSE¯))=rg,n​(E)≤min⁡{|F0|+∑i=1k(d​|⋃e∈Fie|−dΓg)|F0⊆E​ and ​{F1,…,Fk} is a partition of E∖F0}.\dim(\overline{\pi_{G}({\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}}))=r_{g,n}(E)\leq\min\left\{|F_{0}|+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(d\left|\bigcup_{e\in F_{i}}e\right|-d_{\Gamma_{g}}\right)\ \Big|\ \begin{array}[]{l}F_{0}\subseteq E\text{ and }\{F_{1},\dots,F_{k}\}\\
\text{ is a partition of $E\setminus F_{0}$}\end{array}\right\}.







The structure of  (a,b) (a,b)(a,b)-sparsity matroids are well-understood,
and there is an efficient algorithm for computing the right hand side of Equation (4.12), see [46, 62].




A natural question arises concerning whether the inequality presented in Equation (4.12) is actually an equality. While the Geiringer-Laman theorem, Theorem 4.11, establishes this equality for any graph  G GG when  g gg represents the squared distance function in two-dimensional Euclidean space, it is important to note that, in most naturally occurring examples in practical applications, there exists a hypergraph  G GG for which the inequality in Equation (4.12) is strict.
Nevertheless, we conjecture that
the equality always holds if  g gg is sufficiently generic.



Conjecture 4.13.



If  g gg is generic,
then  {\cal M}_{g,n}={\cal S}_{n}^{d,0} ℳg,n=𝒮nd,0{\cal M}_{g,n}={\cal S}_{n}^{d,0}.






4.7 Matroid Union




We now show that the decomposition property of  g gg is inherited by the corresponding matroid  {\cal M}_{g} ℳg{\cal M}_{g}.



Lemma 4.14.



Suppose a polynomial map  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} is the sum of  t tt copies of  h:(\mathbb{F}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} h:(𝔽s)k→𝔽h:(\mathbb{F}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} with  d=st d=s​td=st.
If a set  E EE of hyperedges is independent in  {\cal M}_{g,n} ℳg,n{\cal M}_{g,n}, then
 E EE can be partitioned into  E_{1},E_{2},\dots,E_{t} E1,E2,…,EtE_{1},E_{2},\dots,E_{t} such that
each  E_{i} EiE_{i} is independent in  {\cal M}_{h,n} ℳh,n{\cal M}_{h,n}.




Proof.



For the sake of simplicity, we will provide the proof specifically for the case when  d_{\Gamma_{g}}=0 dΓg=0d_{\Gamma_{g}}=0. However, it is important to note that the proof can be readily adapted to the general case by considering a maximal non-singular submatrix of the Jacobian.
We may further assume that  E EE represents a basis of  {\cal M}_{g,n} ℳg,n{\cal M}_{g,n}, as the property of decomposition holds for all bases, implying the same property for all independent sets.




For a basis  E EE of the matroid  {\cal M}_{g,n} ℳg,n{\cal M}_{g,n}, we denote  G[E] G⁡[E]G[E] for the induced sub-hypergraph on the hyperedges  E EE. Let  p pp be a generic point-configuration of  G[E] G⁡[E]G[E].
Since  E EE is a basis and  d_{\Gamma_{g}}=0 dΓg=0d_{\Gamma_{g}}=0, we have that  |E|=kn |E|=k​n|E|=kn, and so  {\rm J}f_{g,G[E]}(p) J​fg,G⁡[E]​(p){\rm J}f_{g,G[E]}(p) is a square matrix.
Since  g gg is the sum of  t tt copies of  h hh, the structure of  {\rm J}f_{g,G[E]}(p) J​fg,G⁡[E]​(p){\rm J}f_{g,G[E]}(p) has the form of Equation (15).
Hence, the Laplace expansion tells us that





 \det{\rm J}f_{g,G[E]}(p)=\sum_{\{E_{1},\dots,E_{t}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\det{\rm J}f_{h,G[E_{i}]}(q_{i}), detJ​fg,G⁡[E]​(p)=∑{E1,…,Et}∏i=1tdetJ​fh,G⁡[Ei]​(qi),\det{\rm J}f_{g,G[E]}(p)=\sum_{\{E_{1},\dots,E_{t}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\det{\rm J}f_{h,G[E_{i}]}(q_{i}),

(17)


where the sum is taken over all partitions  \{E_{1},\dots,E_{t}\} {E1,…,Et}\{E_{1},\dots,E_{t}\} of  E EE with  |E_{i}|=sn\ (1\leq i\leq t) |Ei|=s​n​(1≤i≤t)|E_{i}|=sn\ (1\leq i\leq t).
Since  E EE is a basis of  {\cal M}_{g,n} ℳg,n{\cal M}_{g,n},  \det{\rm J}f_{g,G[E]}(p)\neq 0 detJ​fg,G⁡[E]​(p)≠0\det{\rm J}f_{g,G[E]}(p)\neq 0.
Hence, there must be at least one non-zero term in the right hand side of Equation (17), and the corresponding partition  \{E_{1},\dots,E_{t}\} {E1,…,Et}\{E_{1},\dots,E_{t}\} is a desired one.
∎





Given matroids  {\cal M}_{1},\dots,{\cal M}_{t} ℳ1,…,ℳt{\cal M}_{1},\dots,{\cal M}_{t}, their union is defined such that a set is independent if it can be partitioned into independent sets of each  {\cal M}_{i} ℳi{\cal M}_{i}. The union of  t tt copies of  {\cal M} ℳ{\cal M} is denoted by  t{\cal M} t​ℳt{\cal M}.
Lemma 4.14 implies that
 {\cal M}_{g,n}\preceq t{\cal M}_{h,n} ℳg,n⪯t​ℳh,n{\cal M}_{g,n}\preceq t{\cal M}_{h,n} in the weak order poset of matroids, i.e., any independent set of  {\cal M}_{g,n} ℳg,n{\cal M}_{g,n} is independent in  t{\cal M}_{h,n} t​ℳh,nt{\cal M}_{h,n}.
A natural question is whether they are indeed different.
This question is closely related to  t tt-defectivity since
 {\cal M}_{g,n}=t{\cal M}_{h,n} ℳg,n=t​ℳh,n{\cal M}_{g,n}=t{\cal M}_{h,n} holds if and only if
 \overline{\pi_{H}({\rm im}f_{h,K_{n}^{k}})} πH​(im​fh,Knk)¯\overline{\pi_{H}({\rm im}f_{h,K_{n}^{k}})} is not  t tt-defective for any  H\subseteq K_{n}^{k} H⊆KnkH\subseteq K_{n}^{k} having no coloop
in  t{\cal M}_{h,n} t​ℳh,nt{\cal M}_{h,n} (i.e., every element is covered by some circuit) by Edmonds’ matroid union theorem, see, e.g., [57].






5 Combinatorial Analysis in Our Example Models




In this section, we will review the concepts and tools introduced in Section 4 by applying them to concrete examples presented in Section 3.



Euclidean rigidity.




In the ordinary Euclidean rigidity,  G GG is 2-uniform (i.e., a graph) and
 g(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2} g⁡(x,y)=∑i=1d(xi−yi)2g(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2} for  x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})^{\top},y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} x=(x1,…,xd)⊤,y=(y1,…,yd)⊤∈ℝdx=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})^{\top},y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
The stabiliser  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} is the Euclidean group  E(d) E⁡(d)E(d).
We have  d_{E(d)}={d+1\choose 2} dE⁡(d)=(d+12)d_{E(d)}={d+1\choose 2} and  n_{E(d)}=d nE⁡(d)=dn_{E(d)}=d. Then
 (d,{d+1\choose 2}) (d,(d+12))(d,{d+1\choose 2})-sparsity is a necessary condition for the independence in the  g gg-rigidity matroid.
When  d=2 d=2d=2, Theorem 4.11
implies that the  g gg-rigidity matroid is equal to the  (2,3) (2,3)(2,3)-sparsity matroid. This is not true for  d\geq 3 d≥3d\geq 3 (see Figure 5 for an example and [34] for a recent discussion) and determining which  (d,{d+1\choose 2}) (d,(d+12))(d,{d+1\choose 2})-sparse graphs are independent is a fundamental open problem in rigidity theory.
See [23] for a new candidate characterisation when  d=3 d=3d=3.


Figure 5: A  (3,6) (3,6)(3,6)-sparse graph that is dependent in the  g gg-rigidity matroid (when  g gg is Euclidean distance and  d=3 d=3d=3).




Let  h(a,b)=(a-b)^{2} h⁡(a,b)=(a−b)2h(a,b)=(a-b)^{2} for  a,b\in\mathbb{C} a,b∈ℂa,b\in\mathbb{C}.
Then  g(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}h(x_{i},y_{i}) g⁡(x,y)=∑i=1dh⁡(xi,yi)g(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}h(x_{i},y_{i}).
So the affine variety defined by  g gg is the  d dd-secant of that of  h hh.
The  h hh-rigidity is easy to understand since it corresponds to 1-dimensional rigidity and it is characterised by graph connectivity.
However, the affine variety defined by  h hh is defective, and currently there is no sufficient condition to guarantee the  d dd-dimensional rigidity in terms of  1 11-dimensional rigidity.




Hendrickson [35] proved two natural necessary conditions for a graph to be globally  g gg-rigid in dimension  d dd. More precisely he proved that, when  g gg is the usual Euclidean distance, any globally  g gg-rigid graph  G GG is  (d+1) (d+1)(d+1)-connected and redundantly  g gg-rigid (which means that the graph obtained from  G GG by deleting any single edge is  g gg-rigid). Connelly [25] proved an algebraic sufficient condition based on stress matrices (weighted Laplacians). Using these two results, and specialised to the case when  d=2 d=2d=2, Jackson and Jordán [37] gave a combinatorial characterisation of generic global  g gg-rigidity. While Hendrickson’s necessary conditions are known to be insufficient when  d\geq 3 d≥3d\geq 3 [24], Gortler, Healy and Thurston [32] proved that generic global  g gg-rigidity does depend only on the underlying graph.




Pseudo-Euclidean rigidity.




Here the situation is similar,  G GG is 2-uniform, the stabiliser is still the Euclidean group,  d_{\Gamma_{g}}={d+1\choose 2} dΓg=(d+12)d_{\Gamma_{g}}={d+1\choose 2} and  n_{\Gamma_{g}}=d nΓg=dn_{\Gamma_{g}}=d. Thus  (d,{d+1\choose 2}) (d,(d+12))(d,{d+1\choose 2})-sparsity is again a necessary condition for independence. In fact there is a direct linear algebraic transformation between independence in the Euclidean case and the pseudo-Euclidean case [55]. This translates, for example, the Geiringer-Laman theorem to pseudo-Euclidean planes.




Suppose  g gg is the usual Euclidean distance and  g^{\prime} g′g^{\prime} is a pseudo-Euclidean distance. Gortler and Thurston [33] proved that a generic framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is globally  g gg-rigid if and only if it is globally  g^{\prime} g′g^{\prime}-rigid. Along the way they extended Connelly’s sufficient condition to pseudo-Euclidean spaces. However in the case of hyperbolic space, it appears to be open to decide if Hendrickson’s redundant rigidity condition applies. Earlier Connelly and Whiteley [27] proved that, generically, global rigidity in the Euclidean and spherical contexts coincide.




 \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-rigidity.




Suppose  p pp is a positive even integer not equal to 2.
Again  G GG is 2-uniform. The stabiliser is the  d dd-dimensional group of translations. We have  d_{\Gamma_{g}}=d dΓg=dd_{\Gamma_{g}}=d,  n_{\Gamma_{g}}=1 nΓg=1n_{\Gamma_{g}}=1 and  (d,d) (d,d)(d,d)-sparsity is a necessary condition for independence. When  d=2 d=2d=2, an analogue of the Geiringer-Laman theorem was obtained in [44] showing that the  g gg-rigidity matroid is equivalent to the  (2,2) (2,2)(2,2)-sparsity matroid on a simple graph.
It is conjectured that the  d dd-dimensional case is characterised by  (d,d) (d,d)(d,d)-sparsity (for simple graphs). This would characterise  d dd-dimensional rigidity in terms of 1-dimensional rigidity. However, such a combinatorial description is currently only known in certain very special cases [29].




It is an open problem to analyse global  g gg-rigidity in the  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p} case. Since  p pp is even,  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-norms are analytic and hence the results of [28] apply; they showed that the natural analogue of Hendrickson’s necessary conditions apply in analytic normed spaces and gave a combinatorial characterisation analogous to Jackson-Jordán when the space is an analytic normed plane.




Volume constraint rigidity.




In the volume constraint rigidity, the map  g:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{d+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} g:(ℝd)d+1→ℝg:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{d+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} is given by





 g(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d+1})=\det\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{d+1}\\
1&1&\dots&1\end{pmatrix}. g⁡(x1,x2,…,xd+1)=det(x1x2…xd+111…1).g(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d+1})=\det\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}&\dots&x_{d+1}\\
1&1&\dots&1\end{pmatrix}.



for  x_{1},\dots,x_{d+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1} x1,…,xd+1∈ℝd+1x_{1},\dots,x_{d+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}.
The stabiliser  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} is the special affine group  SA(d,\mathbb{R}) S​A​(d,ℝ)SA(d,\mathbb{R}).
We have  d_{\Gamma_{g}}=d^{2}+d-1 dΓg=d2+d−1d_{\Gamma_{g}}=d^{2}+d-1 and  n_{\Gamma_{g}}=d+1 nΓg=d+1n_{\Gamma_{g}}=d+1.
Then  (d,d^{2}+d-1) (d,d2+d−1)(d,d^{2}+d-1)-sparsity is a necessary condition for the independence in the  g gg-rigidity matroid, but it is not sufficient in general, see, e.g. [17].
Very recently, Southgate [61] has proved that global  g gg-rigidity, in this volume case, is not a generic property of the underlying hypergraph.




Positive semidefinite matrix completion problem.




In this problem,  G GG is 2-uniform (i.e., a graph) and
 g gg is the Euclidean inner product in  \mathbb{R}^{d} ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}.
The stabiliser  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g} is  O(d) O⁡(d)O(d),
and  d_{E(d)}={d\choose 2},n_{E(d)}=d-1 dE⁡(d)=(d2),nE⁡(d)=d−1d_{E(d)}={d\choose 2},n_{E(d)}=d-1. Then
 (d,{d\choose 2}) (d,(d2))(d,{d\choose 2})-sparsity is a necessary condition for the independence in the  g gg-rigidity matroid.
This sparsity condition is sufficient for  d=1 d=1d=1,
but is not in general for  d\geq 2 d≥2d\geq 2 [59].
It is a challenging open problem to give a good characterisation of the independence in  d=2 d=2d=2, see [11, 41].
The global  g gg-rigidity is also characterised when  d=1 d=1d=1 in [59], but it is not well understood when  d\geq 2 d≥2d\geq 2.
It is known that global  g gg-rigidity is not a generic property of graphs [39], see Section 7.1 for more details.




Symmetric tensor completion problem.




Recall that  h_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} hprod:ℂk→ℂh_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} denotes the product map, i.e.,  h(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{k})=y_{1}y_{2}\dots y_{k} h⁡(y1,y2,…,yk)=y1​y2​…​ykh(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{k})=y_{1}y_{2}\dots y_{k}.
In the symmetric tensor completion with symmetric rank  d dd,
the measurement  g:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} g:(ℂd)k→ℂg:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} is given as the sum of  d dd copies of  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod}.
Hence, we denote this  g gg by  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}.




The  g gg-rigidity property is very different between the cases of  k=2 k=2k=2 and  k\geq 3 k≥3k\geq 3.
When  k=2 k=2k=2, tensors are matrices and we have the same results as the positive semidefinite matrix completion problem discussed above. So in the subsequent discussion we focus on the case when  k\geq 3 k≥3k\geq 3.




The stabiliser  \Gamma Γ\Gamma of  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod} is the set of diagonal matrices over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C} whose diagonal entries are  k kk-th roots of unity (up to permutation of indices).
We have  d_{\Gamma}=0 dΓ=0d_{\Gamma}=0 and  n_{\Gamma}=0 nΓ=0n_{\Gamma}=0.
The  (d,0) (d,0)(d,0)-sparsity is a necessary condition for the independence in the  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigidity matroid, but it is not sufficient since no  k kk-uniform  k kk-partite hypergraph can be  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigid. We will discuss this point in Section 8.




By definition,  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) is locally  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigid if and only if  \rank{\rm J}f_{dh_{\rm prod},G}(p)=d|V| rank⁡J​fd​hprod,G​(p)=d​|V|\rank{\rm J}f_{dh_{\rm prod},G}(p)=d|V| for a generic  p\in(\mathbb{C}^{r})^{k} p∈(ℂr)kp\in(\mathbb{C}^{r})^{k}.
Let  I_{G} IGI_{G} be the edge-vertex incidence matrix of  G GG, that is, the matrix of size  |E|\times|V| |E|×|V||E|\times|V| whose  (e,i) (e,i)(e,i)-th entry is one if vertex  i ii is contained in hyperedge  e ee, and zero otherwise.
When  d=1 d=1d=1, it is easy to check that  {\rm J}f_{dh_{\rm prod},G}(p) J​fd​hprod,G​(p){\rm J}f_{dh_{\rm prod},G}(p) is equivalent to  I_{G} IGI_{G} (in the sense of row and column operations).
Hence, if  d=1 d=1d=1,  G GG is locally  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigid if and only if the edge-vertex incidence matrix is full rank.
The problem becomes much harder when  d\geq 2 d≥2d\geq 2, and checking the locally  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigidity of  K_{n}^{k} KnkK_{n}^{k} is already a nontrivial question.




Since  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod} is the sum of  d dd copies of  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod},
 \overline{{\rm im}f_{dh_{\rm prod},G}} im​fd​hprod,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{dh_{\rm prod},G}} is the  d dd-secant of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},G}} im​fhprod,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},G}}.
When  G=K_{n}^{k} G=KnkG=K_{n}^{k},  f_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n}^{k}} fhprod,Knkf_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n}^{k}} is the Veronese embedding, and  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h^{\rm prod},K_{n}^{k}}} im​fhprod,Knk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h^{\rm prod},K_{n}^{k}}} is the affine cone of the Veronese variety of degree  k kk.
It is relatively recent that Alexander and Hirschowitz [4] gave a concrete list of defective cases of Veronese varieties.
Adapting this result to our language, we have the following.



Theorem 5.1.



Let  k,n,d k,n,dk,n,d be positive integers with  k\geq 3 k≥3k\geq 3,
and  p:[n]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{d} p:[n]→ℂdp:[n]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{d} be a generic point configuration.
Then





 \rk{\rm J}f_{dh_{\rm prod},K_{n}^{k}}(p)=\min\{|E(K_{n}^{k})|,dn\}, rank⁡J​fd​hprod,Knk​(p)=min⁡{|E⁡(Knk)|,d​n},\rk{\rm J}f_{dh_{\rm prod},K_{n}^{k}}(p)=\min\{|E(K_{n}^{k})|,dn\},



except for  (k,n,d) (k,n,d)(k,n,d) in the set  \{(3,5,7),(4,3,5),(4,4,9),(4,5,14)\} {(3,5,7),(4,3,5),(4,4,9),(4,5,14)}\{(3,5,7),(4,3,5),(4,4,9),(4,5,14)\}.
In other words,  K_{n}^{k} KnkK_{n}^{k} is locally  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigid if and only if  |{n+k-1\choose k}|\geq dn |(n+k−1k)|≥d​n|{n+k-1\choose k}|\geq dn
and  (k,n,d)\notin\{(3,5,7),(4,3,5),(4,4,9),(4,5,14)\} (k,n,d)∉{(3,5,7),(4,3,5),(4,4,9),(4,5,14)}(k,n,d)\notin\{(3,5,7),(4,3,5),(4,4,9),(4,5,14)\}.





See, e.g., [10, Theorem 2.12] for more details on the Alexander-Hirschowitz theorem.




The identifiability problem for Veronese varieties has been also solved in [22, 31], which implies a characterisation of the globally  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigid complete hypergraphs in our terminology.
Notably, complete hypergraphs which are both minimally locally  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigid and globally  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigid are classified in [31].
Adapting these results to our language, we have the following.



Theorem 5.2.



Let  k,n,d k,n,dk,n,d be positive integers with  k\geq 3 k≥3k\geq 3.

• 



Suppose  {n+1+k\choose k}>dn (n+1+kk)>d​n{n+1+k\choose k}>dn.
Then  K_{n}^{k} KnkK_{n}^{k} is globally  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigid if and only if  (k,n,d)\notin\{(6,3,9),(4,4,8),(3,6,9)\} (k,n,d)∉{(6,3,9),(4,4,8),(3,6,9)}(k,n,d)\notin\{(6,3,9),(4,4,8),(3,6,9)\}.

• 



Suppose  {n+1+k\choose k}=dn (n+1+kk)=d​n{n+1+k\choose k}=dn.
Then  K_{n}^{k} KnkK_{n}^{k} is globally  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigid if and only if  (k,n,d)\in\{(3,4,5),(5,3,7)\}\cup\{(2s-1,2,s):s\geq 2,s\in\mathbb{Z}\} (k,n,d)∈{(3,4,5),(5,3,7)}∪{(2s−1,2,s):s≥2,s∈ℤ}(k,n,d)\in\{(3,4,5),(5,3,7)\}\cup\{(2s-1,2,s):s\geq 2,s\in\mathbb{Z}\}.







Skew-symmetric tensor completions.




Let  h_{\rm det}:(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} hdet:(ℂk)k→ℂh_{\rm det}:(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} be the determinant as a  k kk-form over  \mathbb{C}^{k} ℂk\mathbb{C}^{k}.
In the skew-symmetric tensor completion with skew-symmetric rank  r rr,
the measurement map  g:(\mathbb{C}^{rk})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} g:(ℂr​k)k→ℂg:(\mathbb{C}^{rk})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} is given as the sum of  r rr copies of  h_{\rm det} hdeth_{\rm det}.
Hence, we denote this  g gg by  rh_{\rm det} r​hdetrh_{\rm det}.




The stabilizer  \Gamma Γ\Gamma of  rh_{\rm det} r​hdetrh_{\rm det} is
 \{(A_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus A_{r})(\Sigma\otimes I_{k})\mid A_{i}\in{\rm SL}(k,\mathbb{C}),\Sigma:\text{ a permutation matrix of size $r$}\} {(A1⊕⋯⊕Ar)(Σ⊗Ik)∣Ai∈SL(k,ℂ),Σ: a permutation matrix of size r}\{(A_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus A_{r})(\Sigma\otimes I_{k})\mid A_{i}\in{\rm SL}(k,\mathbb{C}),\Sigma:\text{ a permutation matrix of size $r$}\}.
We have  d_{\Gamma}=r(k^{2}-1) dΓ=r⁡(k2−1)d_{\Gamma}=r(k^{2}-1) and  n_{\Gamma}=k nΓ=kn_{\Gamma}=k.
The  (rk,r(k^{2}-1)) (r​k,r⁡(k2−1))(rk,r(k^{2}-1))-sparsity is a necessary condition for the independence in the  rh_{\rm det} r​hdetrh_{\rm det}-rigidity matroid, but it is not sufficient even when  r=1 r=1r=1
(see Example 6.5 below for details).




As in the symmetric tensor case, proving the local  rh_{\rm det} r​hdetrh_{\rm det}-rigidity
for simple complete hypergraphs  \tilde{K}_{n}^{k} K~nk\tilde{K}_{n}^{k} is already a difficult question.
Since  rh_{\rm det} r​hdetrh_{\rm det} is the sum of  r rr copies of  h_{\rm det} hdeth_{\rm det},
 \overline{{\rm im}f_{rh_{\rm det},G}} im​fr​hdet,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{rh_{\rm det},G}} is the  r rr-secant of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm det},G}} im​fhdet,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm det},G}}.
When  G=\tilde{K}_{n}^{k} G=K~nkG=\tilde{K}_{n}^{k},  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm det},\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}}} im​fhdet,K~nk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm det},\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}}} is the affine cone of the Grassmannian  {\rm Gr}(k,n) Gr⁡(k,n){\rm Gr}(k,n).
Hence, the locally  rh_{\rm det} r​hdetrh_{\rm det}-rigid of  \tilde{K}_{n}^{k} K~nk\tilde{K}_{n}^{k} is equivalent to the non- r rr-defectivity of  {\rm Gr}(k,n) Gr⁡(k,n){\rm Gr}(k,n).
Unlike the Veronese case, the non-defectivity problem and the identifiability problem of Grassmannian varieties have not been solved yet.
See [2, 48, 47].




Chow decompositions.




The same story holds for Chow decompositions if we replace the determinant function in the above discussion on skew-symmetric tensors with the permanent function.
There is no complete answer to non-defectivity or identifiability of the corresponding varieties, so the local/global rigidity of complete hypergraphs are also not known.
See [64, 66] for recent developments.






6 Sufficient Conditions for Local  g gg-rigidity




The goal of this section is to extend combinatorial techniques in graph rigidity theory to  k kk-uniform hyper-framework  g gg-rigidity.
Since  g gg-rigidity is a rather general concept, we shall restrict our attention to polynomial maps  g gg which are multilinear or multiaffine (defined below).
Based on this assumption, we shall analyse combinatorial patterns of the Jacobians of the measurement maps  g gg.




6.1 Multiaffine forms and combinatorial patterns of Jacobians




A map  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} is said to be a multilinear (resp. multiaffine)  k kk-form on  \mathbb{F}^{d} 𝔽d\mathbb{F}^{d} if
it is linear (resp. affine) on each argument on the vector space  \mathbb{F}^{d} 𝔽d\mathbb{F}^{d}.
As we remarked when defining  g gg-rigidity,  g gg is always assumed to be symmetric or anti-symmetric with respect to permutations of indices.
A symmetric (resp. anti-symmetric) multilinear  k kk-form is nothing but a symmetric (resp. skew-symmetric) tensor of  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k} (𝔽d)k(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}.




Suppose  g gg is a symmetric (or anti-symmetric) multiaffine  k kk-form.
By regarding  g(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}) g⁡(x1,…,xk)g(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}) as an affine function in the variable  x_{j} xjx_{j},
each entry of the gradient of  g gg with respect to  x_{j} xjx_{j} can be considered as a polynomial map in the remaining points  x_{1},\dots,x_{j-1},x_{j+1},\dots,x_{k} x1,…,xj−1,xj+1,…,xkx_{1},\dots,x_{j-1},x_{j+1},\dots,x_{k}.
Moreover, since  g gg is symmetric (or anti-symmetric), the resulting polynomial map is independent of the choice of  j jj (up to the sign if  g gg is anti-symmetric).
Hence, we may consider the gradient of  g gg with respect to a point  x_{j} xjx_{j}
as a polynomial map from  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k-1} (𝔽d)k−1(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k-1} to  \mathbb{F}^{d} 𝔽d\mathbb{F}^{d},
and denote it by  \nabla g ∇g\nabla g.




Based on Proposition 4.6, our analysis of the local  g gg-rigidity of a hypergraph  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) proceeds by
analyzing the rank of the Jacobian  {\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) J​fg,G​(p){\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) at a generic  p pp.
This Jacobian has the following structure.
Given that the map  f_{g,G} fg,Gf_{g,G} is defined from  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} (𝔽d)V(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} to  \mathbb{F}^{E} 𝔽E\mathbb{F}^{E}, we have that
each row of  {\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) J​fg,G​(p){\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) is indexed by a hyperedge in  E EE and
each consecutive  d dd-tuple of columns of  {\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) J​fg,G​(p){\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) are indexed by a vertex in  V VV.
The definition of  f_{g,G} fg,Gf_{g,G} further tells us that
the  1\times d 1×d1\times d block  b(e,v) b⁡(e,v)b(e,v) associated with a pair  (e,v) (e,v)(e,v) for  e\in E e∈Ee\in E and  v\in V v∈Vv\in V
is





 b(e,v)=\begin{cases}\pm m_{e}(v)\nabla g(p(e-v))^{\top}&\text{if $v\in e$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases} b⁡(e,v)={±me(v)∇g(p(e−v))⊤if v∈e0otherwise.b(e,v)=\begin{cases}\pm m_{e}(v)\nabla g(p(e-v))^{\top}&\text{if $v\in e$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}



Here, by  p(e-v) p⁡(e−v)p(e-v), we mean a tuple of  (k-1) (k−1)(k-1) points in  \{p(u):u\in e-v\} {p⁡(u):u∈e−v}\{p(u):u\in e-v\},
and  m_{e}(v) me​(v)m_{e}(v) denotes the multiplicity of  v vv in  e ee.
The sign is always positive if  g gg is symmetric, and it depends on the ordering of  v vv in  e ee if  g gg is anti-symmetric.



Example 6.1.



A primary example arises in the symmetric tensor completion problem.
As we explained in Section 5,
the corresponding map  g gg is the sum  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod} of  d dd copies of  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod}.
Clearly  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod} is symmetric multilinear,
and hence so is  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}.
The map  \nabla(dh_{\rm prod}):(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{d} ∇(d​hprod):(𝔽d)k−1→𝔽d\nabla(dh_{\rm prod}):(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{d} is given by

\vdots\\

6.2 Extension operations




A  d dd-valent extension creates a new  k kk-uniform hypergraph from a given  k kk-uniform hypergraph by adding a new vertex with  d dd distinct new hyperedges containing the new vertex.
We say that an extension operation is simple if each new hyperedge is simple.
See Figure 6 for examples.
It is one of the most basic and widely used theorems in rigidity theory that any  d dd-valent simple extension preserves  d dd-dimensional rigidity.
We shall extend this to the  g gg-rigidity setting for  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}.


Figure 6: (Left) A 3-valent simple extension of a 2-uniform hypergraph. (Right) A 2-valent simple extension of a 3-uniform hypergraph.




For a  d dd-dimensional hyper-framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) and a vertex  v\in V(G) v∈V⁡(G)v\in V(G), consider the matrix  B BB obtained by aligning  \nabla g(p(e-v))^{\top} ∇g(p(e−v))⊤\nabla g(p(e-v))^{\top} as row vectors over all hyperedges in  G GG incident to  v vv, i.e.,





 B=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla g(p(e_{1}-v))^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\nabla g(p(e_{j}-v))^{\top}\end{pmatrix}, B=(∇g(p(e1−v))⊤∇g(p(ej−v))⊤),B=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla g(p(e_{1}-v))^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\nabla g(p(e_{j}-v))^{\top}\end{pmatrix},

(18)


where  e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{j} e1,e2,…,eje_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{j} are hyperedges in  G GG incident to  v vv.
We say that  v vv is stable in  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) if  B BB has full column rank, i.e.,  \rank B=d rank⁡B=d\rank B=d.



Lemma 6.2.



Assume Setup 2.1. Consider  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} and let  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) be a  k kk-uniform hypergraph.

• 



If  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid with  |V|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+1 |V|≥nΓg+1|V|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+1, then every vertex is stable in  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p).

• 



If  G GG is obtained from  H HH by 0-extension by adding a new vertex  v vv and  |V(H)|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} |V⁡(H)|≥nΓg|V(H)|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}, then  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid if and only if
 (H,p_{|V(H)}) (H,p|V(H))(H,p_{|V(H)}) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid and  v vv is stable in  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p).






Proof.

Let  e_{1},\dots,e_{j} e1,…,eje_{1},\dots,e_{j} be the hyperedges in  G GG incident to  v vv.

The second statement follows similarly. If  |V(H)|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} |V⁡(H)|≥nΓg|V(H)|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}},  (H,p_{|V(H)}) (H,p|V(H))(H,p_{|V(H)}) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid and  v vv is stable in  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p), then the block matrix form shown above allows us to deduce, via Proposition 4.6, that  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid. Conversely, if  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid then  v vv is stable in  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) by the first statement and  (H,p_{|V(H)}) (H,p|V(H))(H,p_{|V(H)}) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid.
∎





When  g gg is symmetric, we say that  g gg-rigidity has the extension property if
any  d dd-valent extension of a  g gg-rigid graph  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) with  |V|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} |V|≥nΓg|V|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} is still  g gg-rigid.
When  g gg is anti-symmetric, the extension property is defined by checking only simple extensions since the value of  g gg is zero for a non-simple hyperedge.




We shall now prove the extension property of  g gg-rigidity for a certain class of multiaffine  k kk-forms  g gg. To see this, we need the following notion.
A variety is called degenerate if it is contained in a hyperplane.
We say that  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} is non-degenerate if  \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}} im​fg,Knk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,K_{n}^{k}}} is non-degenerate for any  n nn.
For example,  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod} is non-degenerate since
 \overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n}^{k}}} im​fhprod,Knk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n}^{k}}} is the affine cone of the Veronese variety of degree  k kk, which is known to be non-degenerate.



Lemma 6.3.



Consider  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} written as
the sum of  d dd copies of a non-zero multiaffine  h:(\mathbb{F}^{1})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} h:(𝔽1)k→𝔽h:(\mathbb{F}^{1})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}.
Suppose that  \nabla h ∇h\nabla h is non-degenerate.
Then  g gg-rigidity has the extension property.




Proof.



By Lemma 6.2, it suffices to check that the matrix
 B BB defined by Equation (18) is non-singular for any vertex  v vv,  d dd distinct hyperedges  e_{1},\dots,e_{d} e1,…,ede_{1},\dots,e_{d} incident to  v vv, and a generic  d dd-dimensional point-configuration  B BB.
Note that  \nabla h ∇h\nabla h is a polynomial map from  (\mathbb{F}^{1})^{k-1} (𝔽1)k−1(\mathbb{F}^{1})^{k-1} to  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}.
In particular,  \nabla h ∇h\nabla h is a  (k-1) (k−1)(k-1)-form over  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F},
so the  \nabla h ∇h\nabla h-measurement map is defined for each  (k-1) (k−1)(k-1)-uniform hypergraph.
Let  H HH be the hypergraph consisting of  (k-1) (k−1)(k-1)-uniform hyperedges  e_{1}-v,e_{2}-v,\dots,e_{d}-v e1−v,e2−v,…,ed−ve_{1}-v,e_{2}-v,\dots,e_{d}-v.
Then the columns of  B BB are  f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{1}),f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{2}),\dots,f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{d}) f∇h,H​(x1),f∇h,H​(x2),…,f∇h,H​(xd)f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{1}),f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{2}),\dots,f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{d}) for some  1 11-dimensional generic point-configurations  x_{1},\dots,x_{d} x1,…,xdx_{1},\dots,x_{d}.




We now show that  f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{1}),\dots,f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{d}) f∇h,H​(x1),…,f∇h,H​(xd)f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{1}),\dots,f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{d}) are linearly independent.
Since  \nabla h ∇h\nabla h is non-degenerate,  \overline{{\rm im}f_{\nabla h,K_{n}^{k-1}}} im​f∇h,Knk−1¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{\nabla h,K_{n}^{k-1}}} is non-degenerate.
Since the non-degeneracy is preserved by projection, this in turn implies the non-degeneracy of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{\nabla h,H}} im​f∇h,H¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{\nabla h,H}}. Therefore, any generic  d dd points on  \overline{{\rm im}f_{\nabla h,H}} im​f∇h,H¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{\nabla h,H}} spans the ambient space  \mathbb{F}^{E(H)}\simeq\mathbb{F}^{d} 𝔽E⁡(H)≃𝔽d\mathbb{F}^{E(H)}\simeq\mathbb{F}^{d}. In particular, for generic  x_{1},\dots,x_{d} x1,…,xdx_{1},\dots,x_{d}, we have that
 f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{1}),\dots,f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{d}) f∇h,H​(x1),…,f∇h,H​(xd)f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{1}),\dots,f_{\nabla h,H}(x_{d}) are linearly independent. This implies that  B BB is non-singular, and hence  g gg-rigidity has the extension property.
∎





An important special case is when  g gg is the map for symmetric tensor completions.



Corollary 6.4.



Suppose  g gg is the sum of  d dd copies of  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod}.
Then  g gg-rigidity has the extension property.




Proof.



Observe that  \nabla h_{\rm prod} ∇hprod\nabla h_{\rm prod} is again the product map of  (k-1) (k−1)(k-1) variables.
So  \overline{{\rm im}f_{\nabla h_{\rm prod},K_{n}^{k}}} im​f∇hprod,Knk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{\nabla h_{\rm prod},K_{n}^{k}}} is the affine cone of the Veronese variety of degree  k-1 k−1k-1, which is non-degenerate.
Hence,  \nabla h_{\rm prod} ∇hprod\nabla h_{\rm prod} is non-degenerate and Lemma 6.3 can be applied.
∎





Corollary 6.4 is false in general. The following example shows that the failure of the extension property can actually occur in real applications.



Example 6.5.



Let  h_{\rm det}:(\mathbb{F}^{k})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} hdet:(𝔽k)k→𝔽h_{\rm det}:(\mathbb{F}^{k})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} be the determinant as a multilinear  k kk-form.
For  h_{\rm det} hdeth_{\rm det}-rigidity to have the extension property,
it is necessary that the matrix  B BB in (18) is non-singular for any vertex  v vv
and any  d dd distinct hyperedges  e_{1},\dots,e_{d} e1,…,ede_{1},\dots,e_{d} incident to  v vv.
Denote  e_{i}-v=\{u_{i1},\dots,u_{ik-1}\} ei−v={ui​1,…,ui​k−1}e_{i}-v=\{u_{i1},\dots,u_{ik-1}\}.
Then  \nabla h_{\rm det}(p(e_{i}-v))=\pm p(u_{i1})\wedge p(u_{i2})\wedge\dots\wedge p(u_{ik-1}) ∇hdet​(p⁡(ei−v))=±p⁡(ui​1)∧p⁡(ui​2)∧⋯∧p⁡(ui​k−1)\nabla h_{\rm det}(p(e_{i}-v))=\pm p(u_{i1})\wedge p(u_{i2})\wedge\dots\wedge p(u_{ik-1}) by using the standard basis in the exterior algebra of  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}.
Then  B BB is singular if and only if
 \{p(u_{i1})\wedge p(u_{i2})\wedge\dots\wedge p(u_{ik-1}):1\leq i\leq k\} {p⁡(ui​1)∧p⁡(ui​2)∧⋯∧p⁡(ui​k−1):1≤i≤k}\{p(u_{i1})\wedge p(u_{i2})\wedge\dots\wedge p(u_{ik-1}):1\leq i\leq k\} is linearly dependent.
The latter condition holds if and only if  \langle p(u_{i1}),p(u_{i2}),\dots,p(u_{ik-1})\rangle ⟨p⁡(ui​1),p⁡(ui​2),…,p⁡(ui​k−1)⟩\langle p(u_{i1}),p(u_{i2}),\dots,p(u_{ik-1})\rangle has a common nonzero subspace for  i=1,\dots,k i=1,…,ki=1,\dots,k. If all hyperedges  e_{1},\dots,e_{k} e1,…,eke_{1},\dots,e_{k} contain a common vertex other than  v vv, then the condition fails. ∎





The precise characterisation of when  g gg-rigidity exhibits the extension or simple extension property remains an open question. In the case where  g gg corresponds to the Euclidean distance, several recursive graph operations have been identified that preserve  g gg-rigidity and have been used to characterise classes of  g gg-rigid graphs (see, for example, [51, 68]). One of the most commonly used operations is the 1-extension operation, which operates on a graph by removing an edge  {v_{1},v_{2}} v1,v2{v_{1},v_{2}} and introducing a new vertex  v vv connected to  d+1 d+1d+1 vertices of  G GG, including  v_{1} v1v_{1} and  v_{2} v2v_{2}.
While this 1-extension operation can be extended to hypergraphs, it does not generally preserve  g gg-rigidity. A simple example illustrating this is the 1-dimensional matrix completion problem, where the 1-extension operation corresponds to edge subdivision. In this case, it is well-known that odd cycles are independent in the  g gg-rigidity matroid, whereas even cycles form circuits [59].
Therefore, the simple extension property is not universal for all types of polynomial maps  g gg. Therefore, a deeper understanding of the properties and restrictions imposed by different types of  g gg-rigidity is crucial in order to characterise the conditions under which the extension property holds.





6.3 Packing-type sufficient condition




In this section, we aim to establish a packing-type condition that is sufficient for the local rigidity of hypergraphs  G GG. Let us assume that  g gg can be expressed as the sum of  t tt copies of  h hh. In Lemma 4.14, we demonstrated that any independent set in  {\cal M}_{g,n} ℳg,n{\cal M}_{g,n} can be decomposed into  t tt independent sets in  {\cal M}_{h,n} ℳh,n{\cal M}_{h,n}.
While the converse direction does not hold in general, we will prove in this subsection that a decomposition with an additional property guarantees the validity of the converse direction.




Recall that, for a hyperedge  e\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{k} e∈{[n]k}e\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{k} and  u\in e u∈eu\in e,  e-u e−ue-u (resp.,  e+u e+ue+u) denotes the multiset obtained from  e ee by reducing (resp., increasing) the multiplicity of  u uu by one.
We denote by  {\rm supp}(e) supp⁡(e){\rm supp}(e) the set obtained from  e ee by ignoring the multiplicity of each element.

We give two applications of Theorem 6.6.



Example 6.7.



Consider the product map  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod} and the sum  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod} of  d dd copies of  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod}. This corresponds to the case of symmetric tensor completions.
Consider  k\geq 3 k≥3k\geq 3 and a  k kk-uniform hypergraph  G=([n],E) G=([n],E)G=([n],E) whose edge set is given by





 E=\left\{e\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{k}:m_{e}(v)\geq k-1\text{ for some $v\in[n]$}\right\}. E={e∈{[n]k}:me​(v)≥k−1​ for some v∈[n]}.E=\left\{e\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{k}:m_{e}(v)\geq k-1\text{ for some $v\in[n]$}\right\}.



This hypergraph is the underlying graph of a tridiagonal symmetric tensor.
Suppose  d\leq n d≤nd\leq n.
We choose  {\cal X}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{d}\} 𝒳={X1,…,Xd}{\cal X}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{d}\} with  X_{v}=\{v\} Xv={v}X_{v}=\{v\} for each  v\in[d] v∈[d]v\in[d].
We show  {\cal X} 𝒳{\cal X} satisfies the hypotheses (P1)-(P3) of Theorem 6.6.
We use  v_{1}^{i_{1}}\dots v_{s}^{i_{s}} v1i1​…​vsisv_{1}^{i_{1}}\dots v_{s}^{i_{s}} to denote the hyperedge consisting of  v_{1},\dots,v_{s} v1,…,vsv_{1},\dots,v_{s} with  m_{e}(v_{j})=i_{j} me​(vj)=ijm_{e}(v_{j})=i_{j}.
Then,  E=\{v^{k-1}w:v,w\in[n]\} E={vk−1w:v,w∈[n]}E=\{v^{k-1}w:v,w\in[n]\}.
For each  v\in[d] v∈[d]v\in[d],  E_{G}[X_{v}]=\{v^{k}\} EG​[Xv]={vk}E_{G}[X_{v}]=\{v^{k}\}, and  F_{v}=\{v^{k-1}w:w\in[n]\} Fv={vk−1​w:w∈[n]}F_{v}=\{v^{k-1}w:w\in[n]\}.
The hypergraph  G[X_{v}] G⁡[Xv]G[X_{v}] is locally  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod}-rigid since  G[X_{v}] G⁡[Xv]G[X_{v}] is a single-vertex graph with one edge. So (P2) holds.
Note that  F_{v} FvF_{v} is obtained from  G[X_{v}] G⁡[Xv]G[X_{v}] by a sequence of  1 11-valent extension, implying (P1) by Corollary 6.4.
To see (P3), consider any  e\in F_{v} e∈Fve\in F_{v} and  u\in e u∈eu\in e.

Hence, by  k\geq 3 k≥3k\geq 3,  {\rm supp}(e-u)\not\subset X_{v^{\prime}}=\{v^{\prime}\} supp⁡(e−u)⊄Xv′={v′}{\rm supp}(e-u)\not\subset X_{v^{\prime}}=\{v^{\prime}\} holds for any  v^{\prime} v′v^{\prime} with  v\neq v^{\prime} v≠v′v\neq v^{\prime}, and (P3) follows.
By Theorem 6.6, we conclude that  G GG is locally  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-rigid if  d\leq n d≤nd\leq n.




Example 6.8.



For another (more exciting) application of Theorem 6.6, we need the following notation.
For positive integers  \alpha,\beta α,β\alpha,\beta, an  \alpha α\alpha-uniform family  {\cal X}\subseteq{[n]\choose\alpha} 𝒳⊆([n]α){\cal X}\subseteq{[n]\choose\alpha} is called  \beta β\beta-sparse if
 |X\cap Y|<\beta |X∩Y|<β|X\cap Y|<\beta for any  X,Y\in{\cal X} X,Y∈𝒳X,Y\in{\cal X}.
We define the packing number by





 {\rm packing}(n,\alpha,\beta)=\max\left\{|{\cal X}|:{\cal X}\subseteq{[n]\choose\alpha},{\cal X}\text{ is $\beta$-sparse}\right\}. packing(n,α,β)=max{|𝒳|:𝒳⊆([n]α),𝒳 is β-sparse}.{\rm packing}(n,\alpha,\beta)=\max\left\{|{\cal X}|:{\cal X}\subseteq{[n]\choose\alpha},{\cal X}\text{ is $\beta$-sparse}\right\}.



An asymptotic bound by Rödl [53] gives
 {\rm packing}(n,\alpha,\beta)=(1-o(1)){n\choose\beta}/{\alpha\choose\beta}. packing⁡(n,α,β)=(1−o⁡(1))​(nβ)/(αβ).{\rm packing}(n,\alpha,\beta)=(1-o(1)){n\choose\beta}/{\alpha\choose\beta}.




Recall that  \tilde{K}_{n}^{k} K~nk\tilde{K}_{n}^{k} denotes the simple complete  k kk-uniform hypergraph on  n nn vertices.

Choose  X_{i}\in{\cal X} Xi∈𝒳X_{i}\in{\cal X}.
Since  |X_{i}|=a |Xi|=a|X_{i}|=a, the subgraph  \tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}] K~nk​[Xi]\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}] is isomorphic to  \tilde{K}_{a}^{k} K~ak\tilde{K}_{a}^{k}, so
(P2) follows from the local  h hh-rigidity of  \tilde{K}_{a}^{k} K~ak\tilde{K}_{a}^{k}.




To see (P1), consider  F_{i} FiF_{i} as defined in the statement of Theorem 6.6 in the case when  G=\tilde{K}_{n}^{k} G=K~nkG=\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}, and let  G_{i}=([n],F_{i}) Gi=([n],Fi)G_{i}=([n],F_{i}).
Pick any vertex  u\in[n]\setminus X_{i} u∈[n]∖Xiu\in[n]\setminus X_{i}.
Since  a\geq n_{\Gamma_{h}}+1 a≥nΓh+1a\geq n_{\Gamma_{h}}+1 and  K_{a}^{k} KakK_{a}^{k} is locally  h hh-rigid,
Lemma 6.2 implies that each vertex  v vv in  X_{i} XiX_{i} is stable in a generic framework of  \tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}] K~nk​[Xi]\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}].
Hence each vertex  v vv in  X_{i} XiX_{i} remains stable in a generic framework of  \tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}\cup\{u\}] K~nk​[Xi∪{u}]\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}\cup\{u\}].
Since  \tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}\cup\{u\}] K~nk​[Xi∪{u}]\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}\cup\{u\}] is a simple complete hypergraph, the symmetry further implies that  u uu is also stable in a generic framework of  \tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}\cup\{u\}] K~nk​[Xi∪{u}]\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}\cup\{u\}].
Hence, by Lemma 6.2, a subgraph of  \tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}\cup\{u\}] K~nk​[Xi∪{u}]\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}\cup\{u\}] is obtained from
 \tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}] K~nk​[Xi]\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}] by an  s ss-valent extension which preserves local  g gg-rigidity.
Note that  \tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}\cup\{u\}] K~nk​[Xi∪{u}]\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}\cup\{u\}] is a subgraph of  G_{i} GiG_{i}.
Applying the same argument to each vertex  u\in[n]\setminus X_{i} u∈[n]∖Xiu\in[n]\setminus X_{i},
we can further construct
a spanning subgraph of  G_{i} GiG_{i} from  \tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}] K~nk​[Xi]\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}[X_{i}] by a sequence of  s ss-valent extensions keeping local  g gg-rigidity.
Thus  G_{i} GiG_{i} is locally  g gg-rigid.




Finally, suppose, for a contradiction, that (P3) does not hold.
Then  {\rm supp}(e-v)\subseteq X_{j} supp⁡(e−v)⊆Xj{\rm supp}(e-v)\subseteq X_{j} for some  e\in F_{i} e∈Fie\in F_{i},  v\in e v∈ev\in e, and  X_{j} XjX_{j} with  i\neq j i≠ji\neq j.
Since  e\in F_{i} e∈Fie\in F_{i}, there are some  u,w\in[n] u,w∈[n]u,w\in[n] such that  e-u+w\in E_{\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}}[X_{i}] e−u+w∈EK~nk​[Xi]e-u+w\in E_{\tilde{K}_{n}^{k}}[X_{i}], so  {\rm supp}(e-u-v)\subseteq X_{i}\cap X_{j} supp⁡(e−u−v)⊆Xi∩Xj{\rm supp}(e-u-v)\subseteq X_{i}\cap X_{j}.
Since  |{\rm supp}(e-u-v)|=k-2 |supp⁡(e−u−v)|=k−2|{\rm supp}(e-u-v)|=k-2, this contradicts the fact that  {\cal X} 𝒳{\cal X} is  (k-2) (k−2)(k-2)-sparse.
Thus (P3) holds, and the  g gg-rigidity of  G GG follows from Theorem 6.6.
∎






We note that when  h hh is the determinant function, Corollary 6.9 aligns with an observation in [2].






7 Conditions for Global  g gg-rigidity




In this section we analyse global  g gg-rigidity.




7.1 Generic global  g gg-rigidity




In Section 4, we observed that local  g gg-rigidity is a generic property of the underlying hypergraphs. This notion naturally leads to the concept of local  g gg-rigidity of hypergraphs. By applying Chevalley’s theorem, similar to the approach in Proposition 4.2(iv), we can extend this result to global  g gg-rigidity when considering constraints over the field of complex numbers.



Proposition 7.1.



Global  g gg-rigidity is a generic property of the underlying hypergraphs if the underlying field is complex.





Additionally, in the case of Euclidean distance where  g gg is the squared Euclidean distance function, there are notable results in the real case by Gortler, Healy, and Thurston [33, 40]. They show that global  g gg-rigidity is a generic property in the real setting. However, it is important to note that this result does not hold for other maps. For example, if  g gg represents the Euclidean inner product of two points over  \mathbb{R} ℝ\mathbb{R} [39] or if  g gg is the map for volume rigidity [61], then global  g gg-rigidity is not a generic property over reals.




While global  g gg-rigidity may not be a generic property in general, it would be useful if we can guarantee the global  g gg-rigidity for any generic realization of a given hypergraph. Therefore, we define a hypergraph  G GG to be globally  g gg-rigid (over  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}) if  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is globally  g gg-rigid for any generic point-configuration  p pp over  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}.




The following proposition provides motivation for focusing on the complex case when establishing a sufficient condition for global rigidity.



Proposition 7.2.



If a hypergraph  G GG is globally  g gg-rigid over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C}, then it is globally  g gg-rigid over  \mathbb{R} ℝ\mathbb{R}.




Proof.



Since  G GG is globally  g gg-rigid over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C},
any generic hyperframework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) of  G GG over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C} is globally rigid.
Since the genericity is defined in terms of algebraic independence over  \mathbb{Q} ℚ\mathbb{Q}, any generic configuration  q qq over  \mathbb{R} ℝ\mathbb{R} is also generic over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C}.
So for any generic real configuration  q qq,  (G,q) (G,q)(G,q) is a generic hyperframework over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C}, and it is globally rigid.
∎






7.2 Identifiability and global  g gg-rigidity




The following proposition highlights the usefulness of identifiability in verifying global rigidity.



Proposition 7.3.



Suppose  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} is the sum of  t tt copies of a homogeneous  h:(\mathbb{F}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} h:(𝔽s)k→𝔽h:(\mathbb{F}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}.
Suppose further that  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} is  t tt-identifiable.
Then  G GG is globally  g gg-rigid if and only if  G GG is globally  h hh-rigid.




Proof.



Clearly global  h hh-rigidity is necessary for global  g gg-rigidity.
To see the converse direction, we take any generic  p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} p∈(𝔽d)Vp\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}
and show that, if  G GG is globally  h hh-rigid then  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is globally  g gg-rigid.




Since  g gg is the sum of  t tt copies of  h hh,
 p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} p∈(𝔽d)Vp\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} can be decomposed into  (q_{1},\dots,q_{t}) (q1,…,qt)(q_{1},\dots,q_{t}) with  q_{i}\in(\mathbb{F}^{s})^{V} qi∈(𝔽s)Vq_{i}\in(\mathbb{F}^{s})^{V} such that
 f_{g,G}(p)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}f_{h,G}(q_{i}) fg,G​(p)=∑i=1tfh,G​(qi)f_{g,G}(p)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}f_{h,G}(q_{i}).
Since  p pp is generic, each  q_{i} qiq_{i} is generic.
So  (G,q_{i}) (G,qi)(G,q_{i}) is globally  h hh-rigid.
Hence, the  t tt-identifiability of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} implies the global  g gg-rigidity of  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p).
∎





We next review some sufficient conditions for checking the identifiability of varieties.
A key notion is that of tangential weak defectiveness due to Chiantini and Ottaviani [20].
Let  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} be a variety over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C},
and denote by  T_{x}{\cal V} Tx​𝒱T_{x}{\cal V} the tangent space at  x\in{\cal V} x∈𝒱x\in{\cal V}.
The  t tt-tangential contact locus  {\cal C} 𝒞{\cal C} at generic  t tt points  x_{1},\dots,x_{t} x1,…,xtx_{1},\dots,x_{t} is defined as
 {\cal C}=\overline{\{y\in{\cal V}:T_{y}{\cal V}\subseteq\langle T_{x_{1}}{\cal V},\dots,T_{x_{t}}{\cal V}\rangle\}}. 𝒞={y∈𝒱:Ty​𝒱⊆⟨Tx1​𝒱,…,Txt​𝒱⟩}¯.{\cal C}=\overline{\{y\in{\cal V}:T_{y}{\cal V}\subseteq\langle T_{x_{1}}{\cal V},\dots,T_{x_{t}}{\cal V}\rangle\}}.
A variety  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} is said to be  t tt-tangentially weakly defective
if an irreducible component of  {\cal C} 𝒞{\cal C} that contains  x_{i} xix_{i} has dimension at least two for some  i ii.
(Since we are working in affine space, the tangential contact locus always contains a one-dimensional subspace of scaling.)




It was shown that  t tt-tangential weak non-defectiveness is a sufficient condition for  t tt-identifiability.



Theorem 7.4 (Chiantini and Ottaviani [20]).



Suppose an affine variety  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C} is  t tt-tangentially weakly nondefective.
Then  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} is  t tt-identifiable.





A combination of Proposition 7.3 and Theorem 7.4 leads to the following criterion for global  g gg-rigidity.



Corollary 7.5.



Suppose a homogeneous  g:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} g:(ℂd)k→ℂg:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} is the sum of  t tt copies of  h:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} h:(ℂs)k→ℂh:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.
Then  G GG is globally  g gg-rigid if

• 



 \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} is  t tt-tangentially weakly nondefective, and

• 



 G GG is globally  h hh-rigid.







The tangential contact locus can be characterised algebraically, allowing for the development of numerical algorithms to test for  t tt-tangentially weak defectiveness. Chiantini, Ottaviani, and Vannieuwenhoven [21] proposed an algorithm based on this description, which we will briefly review as it is closely related to a well-known method in rigidity.




Let  G GG be a  k kk-uniform hypergraph with  n nn vertices and  m mm edges,
and suppose  g:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} g:(ℂd)k→ℂg:(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} is the sum of  t tt copies of  h:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} h:(ℂs)k→ℂh:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.
In view of Corollary 7.5, we want to check
the  t tt-tangentially weakly defectiveness of
 \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}}.
Let  p\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{n} p∈(ℂd)np\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{n} be a generic point-configuration,
and let  \omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{m} ω1,…,ωk∈ℂm\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{m} be a basis of the orthogonal complement of the tangent space of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} im​fg,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} at  f_{g,G}(p) fg,G​(p)f_{g,G}(p).
Since the tangent space of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} at  f_{h,G}(q) fh,G​(q)f_{h,G}(q) is the image of  {\rm J}f_{h,G}(q) J​fh,G​(q){\rm J}f_{h,G}(q),
the  t tt-tangential contact locus  {\cal C} 𝒞{\cal C} is the closure of
 \{q\in(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{n}:\omega_{i}\in\ker{\rm J}f_{h,G}(q)^{\top}\ (i=1,\dots,k)\} {q∈(ℂs)n:ωi∈ker⁡J​fh,G​(q)⊤​(i=1,…,k)}\{q\in(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{n}:\omega_{i}\in\ker{\rm J}f_{h,G}(q)^{\top}\ (i=1,\dots,k)\}.
Note that  \omega_{i}\in\ker{\rm J}f_{h,G}(q)^{\top} ωi∈ker⁡J​fh,G​(q)⊤\omega_{i}\in\ker{\rm J}f_{h,G}(q)^{\top} forms a polynomial system in  q qq (where  \omega_{i} ωi\omega_{i} is fixed). Therefore, there is a polynomial map  \alpha_{i}:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{n}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{n} αi:(ℂs)n→(ℂs)n\alpha_{i}:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{n}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{n}
such that
 \omega_{i}\in\ker{\rm J}f_{h,G}(q)^{\top} ωi∈ker⁡J​fh,G​(q)⊤\omega_{i}\in\ker{\rm J}f_{h,G}(q)^{\top} if and only if  \alpha_{i}(q)=0 αi​(q)=0\alpha_{i}(q)=0.
So, the tangential contact locus is written as the closure of
 \{q\in(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{n}:\alpha_{i}(q)=0\ (i=1,\dots,k)\} {q∈(ℂs)n:αi​(q)=0​(i=1,…,k)}\{q\in(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{n}:\alpha_{i}(q)=0\ (i=1,\dots,k)\}.




It was shown in [21] that, if an affine variety  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} is  t tt-tangentially weakly defective, then the  t tt-tangential contact locus formed by generic points  x_{1},\dots,x_{t} x1,…,xtx_{1},\dots,x_{t} contains a smooth path connecting any  x_{i} xix_{i} to the locus.
Hence, in our terminology above, this implies that one can determine  t tt-tangential weak non-defectiveness by examining the rank of the Jacobian matrix of  \alpha_{i} αi\alpha_{i}.



Remark 7.6.



It is worth mentioning that the resulting Jacobian matrix of  \alpha_{i} αi\alpha_{i} is the Laplacian matrix of  G GG weighted by  \omega_{i} ωi\omega_{i}, in the case that  g gg is the squared Euclidean distance. So, the numerical condition in [21] has a similarity to the stress matrix condition for global rigidity due to Connelly [25]
(or more generally to the shared stress kernel condition due to Gortler-Healy-Thurston [33]). It should be noted, however, that in the case of the squared  d dd-dimensional Euclidean distance the underlying variety is always  d dd-tangentially weakly defective if  d>1 d>1d>1.





A recent result of Massarenti and Mella [47] gives a powerful criterion for checking  t tt-identifiability.



Theorem 7.7 ([47]).



Let  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} be a non-degenerate affine variety in  \mathbb{C}^{m} ℂm\mathbb{C}^{m}.
Suppose that  (t+1)\dim{\cal V}\leq m (t+1)​dim𝒱≤m(t+1)\dim{\cal V}\leq m and  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} is  (t+1) (t+1)(t+1)-nondefective and  1 11-tangentially weakly nondefective.
Then  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} is  t tt-identifiable.





Combining Proposition 7.3 and Theorem 7.7, we have the following criterion for global  g gg-rigidity.



Corollary 7.8.



For a homogeneous
 h:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} h:(ℂs)k→ℂh:(\mathbb{C}^{s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} and a positive integer  d dd, let  dh d​hdh be the sum of  d dd copies of  h hh, and suppose  d_{\Gamma_{(d+1)h}}=(d+1)d_{\Gamma_{h}} dΓ(d+1)​h=(d+1)​dΓhd_{\Gamma_{(d+1)h}}=(d+1)d_{\Gamma_{h}}.
Then, for a positive integer  d dd,  G GG is globally  dh d​hdh-rigid if

• 



 G GG is locally  (d+1)h (d+1)​h(d+1)h-rigid,

• 



 \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} is 1-tangentially weakly nondefective, and

• 



 G GG is globally  h hh-rigid.







The  1 11-tangential contact locus is just an ordinary contact locus, so  1 11-tangential weak defectiveness is equivalent to the degeneracy of the Gauss map.





7.3 Sufficient conditions




Corollary 7.5 or Corollary 7.8 provides a way to reduce the global  g gg-rigidity problem to the global  h hh-rigidity problem, however it is a non-trivial problem to establish global  h hh-rigidity.
In order to accomplish this, we will extend Connelly’s sufficient condition [25] from graph rigidity theory.




The result is described in terms of the adjacency matrix of an edge weighted hypergraph.
Suppose  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) is a  k kk-uniform hypergraph and  w\in\mathbb{F}^{E} w∈𝔽Ew\in\mathbb{F}^{E} is a vector representing the weight of each edge.
Consider the collection
 E^{(k-1)}:=\{\sigma\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{V}{k-1}:\sigma\subseteq e\in E\} E(k−1):={σ∈{Vk−1}:σ⊆e∈E}E^{(k-1)}:=\{\sigma\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{V}{k-1}:\sigma\subseteq e\in E\} of multisets of size  (k-1) (k−1)(k-1) contained in some hyperedge in  G GG.
We define the adjacency matrix  A_{G,w} AG,wA_{G,w} to be
an  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}-matrix of size
 |V|\times|E^{(k-1)}| |V|×|E(k−1)||V|\times|E^{(k-1)}| such that:





 A_{G,w}[v,\sigma]=\begin{cases}m_{e}(v)w(e)&(\text{if $e=\sigma+v$ is in $G$})\\
0&(\text{otherwise}),\end{cases} AG,w​[v,σ]={me​(v)​w​(e)(if e=σ+v is in G)0(otherwise),A_{G,w}[v,\sigma]=\begin{cases}m_{e}(v)w(e)&(\text{if $e=\sigma+v$ is in $G$})\\
0&(\text{otherwise}),\end{cases}



where each row is indexed by a vertex  v\in V v∈Vv\in V and each column is indexed by  \sigma\in E^{(k-1)} σ∈E(k−1)\sigma\in E^{(k-1)}.
We will often look at the case when the edge weight  w ww is in the left kernel of  {\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) J​fg,G​(p){\rm J}f_{g,G}(p).
See Example 7.9 for an example.




In order to deal with the case of anti-symmetric functions, we consider the signed variant of the adjacency matrix.
For this, we assume that the elements of  V VV are totally ordered, and consider  A^{s}_{G,w} AG,wsA^{s}_{G,w} with:





 A^{s}_{G,w}[v,\sigma]=\begin{cases}{\rm sign}(e,v)w(e)m_{e}(v)&(\text{if $e=\sigma+v$ is in $G$})\\
0&(\text{otherwise})\end{cases} AG,ws​[v,σ]={sign⁡(e,v)​w​(e)​me​(v)(if e=σ+v is in G)0(otherwise)A^{s}_{G,w}[v,\sigma]=\begin{cases}{\rm sign}(e,v)w(e)m_{e}(v)&(\text{if $e=\sigma+v$ is in $G$})\\
0&(\text{otherwise})\end{cases}



for each  v\in V v∈Vv\in V and  \sigma\in E^{(k-1)} σ∈E(k−1)\sigma\in E^{(k-1)},
where  {\rm sign}(e,v) sign⁡(e,v){\rm sign}(e,v) denotes the standard sign function of permutations, which is positive (resp. negative) if the ordering of  v vv in  e ee is odd (resp. even).



Example 7.9.



Consider a  4 44-uniform hypergraph  G_{1}=(\{a,b\},\{aaaa,abbb,bbbb\}) G1=({a,b},{a​a​a​a,a​b​b​b,b​b​b​b})G_{1}=(\{a,b\},\{aaaa,abbb,bbbb\}) with edge-weight  \omega_{1} ω1\omega_{1} and
a  4 44-uniform hypergraph  G_{2}=(\{a,b\},\{aaaa,aabb,bbbb\}) G2=({a,b},{a​a​a​a,a​a​b​b,b​b​b​b})G_{2}=(\{a,b\},\{aaaa,aabb,bbbb\}) with edge-weight  \omega_{2} ω2\omega_{2}.
Then
