Proposition 7.10.



Suppose  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} is a multilinear  k kk-form over  \mathbb{F}^{d} 𝔽d\mathbb{F}^{d}, and  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is a  k kk-uniform hyper-framework.
Let  P PP be a matrix of size  d\times|E^{(k-1)}| d×|E(k−1)|d\times|E^{(k-1)}| obtained by aligning  \nabla g(p(\sigma)) ∇g​(p​(σ))\nabla g(p(\sigma)) as a column vector for each  \sigma\in E^{(k-1)} σ∈E(k−1)\sigma\in E^{(k-1)}.

(i) 



If  g gg is symmetric, then  A_{G,w}P^{\top}=w^{\top}{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) AG,w​P⊤=w⊤​J​fg,G​(p)A_{G,w}P^{\top}=w^{\top}{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) for any edge weight  w ww of  G GG.

(ii) 



If  g gg is anti-symmetric, then  A^{s}_{G,w}P^{\top}=w^{\top}{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) AG,ws​P⊤=w⊤​J​fg,G​(p)A^{s}_{G,w}P^{\top}=w^{\top}{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) for any edge weight  w ww of  G GG.

(iii) 



If  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid, then  \rank P=d rank⁡P=d\rank P=d.






Proof.



Assume  g gg is symmetric. By definition, the  v vv-th entry of  w^{\top}{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) w⊤​J​fg,G​(p)w^{\top}{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)
is





 \sum_{\sigma\in E^{(k-1)}:\sigma+v\in E}w(\sigma+v)m_{\sigma+v}(v)\nabla g(p(\sigma)). ∑σ∈E(k−1):σ+v∈Ew(σ+v)mσ+v(v)∇g(p(σ)).\sum_{\sigma\in E^{(k-1)}:\sigma+v\in E}w(\sigma+v)m_{\sigma+v}(v)\nabla g(p(\sigma)).



Reshaping this in terms of  \nabla g(p(\sigma)) ∇g​(p​(σ))\nabla g(p(\sigma)),
we obtain  A_{G,w}P^{\top}. AG,w​P⊤.A_{G,w}P^{\top}. The same argument also applies to the case when  g gg is anti-symmetric. We thus obtain (i) and (ii).




To see (iii), assume  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid.
By Lemma 6.2,
every vertex is stable in  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p).
So the columns of  P PP indexed by  d dd hyperedges incident to a vertex forms an independent set, which means that  P PP has full row-rank.
∎




Proposition 7.11.



Suppose  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} is a multilinear  k kk-form and  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is a generic locally  g gg-rigid  k kk-uniform hyper-framework.
Suppose

• 



 \dim\left(\bigcap_{\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,\omega}\right)=d dim(⋂ω∈ker⁡J​fG,g​(p)⊤ker⁡AG,ω)=d\dim\left(\bigcap_{\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,\omega}\right)=d if  f ff is symmetric, and

• 



 \dim\left(\bigcap_{\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}}\ker A^{s}_{G,\omega}\right)=d dim(⋂ω∈ker⁡J​fG,g​(p)⊤ker⁡AG,ωs)=d\dim\left(\bigcap_{\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}}\ker A^{s}_{G,\omega}\right)=d if  f ff is anti-symmetric.



Then for each  q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) there exists  T\in\mathbb{F}^{d\times d} T∈𝔽d×dT\in\mathbb{F}^{d\times d} such that





 \nabla g(q(\sigma))=T\nabla g(p(f))\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\sigma\in E^{(k-1)}$}. ∇g(q(σ))=T∇g(p(f))for all σ∈E(k−1).\nabla g(q(\sigma))=T\nabla g(p(f))\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\sigma\in E^{(k-1)}$}.






Proof.



We give the proof only in the case when  f ff is symmetric
since the anti-symmetric case is identical.




By Proposition 4.2(iv) and the generic assumption on  p pp,  f_{g,G}(p) fg,G​(p)f_{g,G}(p) is a regular value of  f_{g,G} fg,Gf_{g,G}, so for any  q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p))
the image of  {\rm J}f_{g,G}(q) J​fg,G​(q){\rm J}f_{g,G}(q) is the tangent space of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} im​fg,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}} at  f_{g,G}(q) fg,G​(q)f_{g,G}(q). We have that:





 \ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(q)^{\top}=\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)^{\top}, ker⁡J​fg,G​(q)⊤=ker⁡J​fg,G​(p)⊤,\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(q)^{\top}=\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)^{\top},

(23)


since  f_{g,G}(p)=f_{g,G}(q) fg,G​(p)=fg,G​(q)f_{g,G}(p)=f_{g,G}(q). For any point-configuration  q qq of  G GG, let
 Q QQ be a matrix of size  d\times|E^{(k-1)}| d×|E(k−1)|d\times|E^{(k-1)}| obtained by aligning  \nabla g(q(\sigma)) ∇g​(q​(σ))\nabla g(q(\sigma)) as the columns for all  \sigma\in E^{(k-1)} σ∈E(k−1)\sigma\in E^{(k-1)}.
By Proposition 7.10 and Equation (23),





 A_{G,w}Q^{\top}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for any }\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}. AG,w​Q⊤=0for any ​ω∈ker⁡J​fG,g​(p)⊤.A_{G,w}Q^{\top}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for any }\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}.

(24)


In particular,  A_{G,w}P^{\top}=0 AG,w​P⊤=0A_{G,w}P^{\top}=0.
Hence each row of  P PP belongs to  \bigcap_{\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,\omega} ⋂ω∈ker⁡J​fG,g​(p)⊤ker⁡AG,ω\bigcap_{\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,\omega}.




Note that we have assumed the dimension of  \bigcap_{\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,\omega} ⋂ω∈ker⁡J​fG,g​(p)⊤ker⁡AG,ω\bigcap_{\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,\omega} is  d dd.
Moreover, we have  \rank P=d rank⁡P=d\rank P=d by Proposition 7.10.
By combining those two facts with  A_{G,w}P^{\top}=0 AG,w​P⊤=0A_{G,w}P^{\top}=0,
we may conclude that
the rows of  P PP form a basis of
 \bigcap_{\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,\omega} ⋂ω∈ker⁡J​fG,g​(p)⊤ker⁡AG,ω\bigcap_{\omega\in\ker{\rm J}f_{G,g}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,\omega}.
Since each row of  Q QQ belongs to this space by Equation (24),
there must be  T\in\mathbb{F}^{d\times d} T∈𝔽d×dT\in\mathbb{F}^{d\times d} such that
 Q=TP Q=T​PQ=TP.
This is equivalent to the statement.
∎




Proposition 7.12.



Suppose  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} is a multilinear  k kk-form,  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is a generic locally  g gg-rigid  k kk-uniform hyper-framework, and  q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)).
Suppose there is  T\in\mathbb{F}^{d\times d} T∈𝔽d×dT\in\mathbb{F}^{d\times d} such that





 \nabla g(q(\sigma))=T\nabla g(p(\sigma))\hskip 20.00003pt(\sigma\in E^{(k-1)}). ∇g(q(σ))=T∇g(p(σ))(σ∈E(k−1)).\nabla g(q(\sigma))=T\nabla g(p(\sigma))\hskip 20.00003pt(\sigma\in E^{(k-1)}).



Then





 p(v)=T^{\top}q(v)\hskip 20.00003pt(v\in V). p⁡(v)=T⊤​q​(v)(v∈V).p(v)=T^{\top}q(v)\hskip 20.00003pt(v\in V).






Proof.



Since  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is generic and locally  g gg-rigid,
every vertex is stable by Lemma 6.2.
So there are  d dd hyperedges  e_{1},\dots,e_{d} e1,…,ede_{1},\dots,e_{d} incident to  v vv in  G GG
such that  \{\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v)):i=1,\dots,d\} {∇g(p(ei−v)):i=1,…,d}\{\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v)):i=1,\dots,d\} is a basis of  \mathbb{F}^{d} 𝔽d\mathbb{F}^{d}.
Since  q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)), we have
 f_{g,G}(q)=f_{g,G}(p) fg,G​(q)=fg,G​(p)f_{g,G}(q)=f_{g,G}(p). By the multilinearity of  g gg, this implies that





 \langle q(v),\nabla g(q(e_{i}-v))\rangle=\langle p(v),\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v))\rangle, ⟨q⁡(v),∇g​(q⁡(ei−v))⟩=⟨p⁡(v),∇g​(p⁡(ei−v))⟩,\langle q(v),\nabla g(q(e_{i}-v))\rangle=\langle p(v),\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v))\rangle,



and hence





 \langle T^{\top}q(v)-p(v),\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v))\rangle=0\hskip 10.00002pt(i=1,\dots,d). ⟨T⊤​q​(v)−p⁡(v),∇g​(p⁡(ei−v))⟩=0(i=1,…,d).\langle T^{\top}q(v)-p(v),\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v))\rangle=0\hskip 10.00002pt(i=1,\dots,d).



The fact that  \{\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v)):i=1,\dots,d\} {∇g(p(ei−v)):i=1,…,d}\{\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v)):i=1,\dots,d\} is a basis now gives
 p(v)=T^{\top}q(v) p⁡(v)=T⊤​q​(v)p(v)=T^{\top}q(v) for all  v vv.
∎





When  g gg is the determinant, a combination of Propositions 7.11 and 7.12 gives the following sufficient condition for global  g gg-rigidity.



Theorem 7.13.



Suppose  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)d→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} is the determinant as a multilinear  d dd-form over  \mathbb{F}^{d} 𝔽d\mathbb{F}^{d}.
Then  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) is globally  g gg-rigid if
there exists a point-configuration  p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} p∈(𝔽d)V→𝔽p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} such that

• 



 (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid, and

• 



 \dim\left(\bigcap_{w\in\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)^{\top}}\ker A^{s}_{G,w}\right)=d dim(⋂w∈ker⁡J​fg,G​(p)⊤ker⁡AG,ws)=d\dim\left(\bigcap_{w\in\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)^{\top}}\ker A^{s}_{G,w}\right)=d.






Proof.



The infinitesimal rigidity of  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) implies that the rank of  {\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) J​fg,G​(p){\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) takes the maximum possible value over all point-configurations.
Note that  \ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) ker⁡J​fg,G​(p)\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) changes continuously if we perturb the entries of  p pp continuously.
So any sufficiently small continuous perturbation of the entries of  p pp does not change  \dim\left(\bigcap_{w\in\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,w}\right) dim(⋂w∈ker⁡J​fg,G​(p)⊤ker⁡AG,w)\dim\left(\bigcap_{w\in\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,w}\right).
Hence, we may assume that  p pp is generic.




To see the global  g gg-rigidity of  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p), pick any  q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)). By Propositions 7.11 and 7.12,
there is  T\in\mathbb{F}^{d\times d} T∈𝔽d×dT\in\mathbb{F}^{d\times d} such that
 q(v)=Tp(v) q⁡(v)=T​p​(v)q(v)=Tp(v) for all  v\in V(G) v∈V⁡(G)v\in V(G).
Pick any hyperedge  e ee in  G GG, and assume  e ee consists of  k kk vertices  v_{1},\dots,v_{k} v1,…,vkv_{1},\dots,v_{k}.
Since  q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)) q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))q\in f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p)), we have





 \det\begin{pmatrix}p(v_{1})&\dots&p(v_{k})\end{pmatrix}=\det\begin{pmatrix}q(v_{1})&\dots&q(v_{k})\end{pmatrix}=\det T\det\begin{pmatrix}p(v_{1})&\dots&p(v_{k})\end{pmatrix}, det(p⁡(v1)…p⁡(vk))=det(q⁡(v1)…q⁡(vk))=detT​det(p⁡(v1)…p⁡(vk)),\det\begin{pmatrix}p(v_{1})&\dots&p(v_{k})\end{pmatrix}=\det\begin{pmatrix}q(v_{1})&\dots&q(v_{k})\end{pmatrix}=\det T\det\begin{pmatrix}p(v_{1})&\dots&p(v_{k})\end{pmatrix},



so  \det T=1 detT=1\det T=1.
This implies that  T TT is in the stabilizer of  g gg, and the global  g gg-rigidity of  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) follows.
∎





By combining Theorem 7.13 with either Corollary 7.5 or Corollary 7.8, we can establish a sufficient condition for global rigidity when  g gg is the sum of copies of the determinant map. However, when  g gg is the sum of copies of the product map (as in the case of symmetric tensor completion), we can provide a direct sufficient condition without the need for identifiability tests.
A key ingredient in establishing this condition is the following consequence of a generalised trisecant lemma given in [19].



Proposition 7.14.



Let  h:=h_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} h:=hprod:ℂk→ℂh:=h_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} be the product map,  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) a hypergraph,
and  x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{t} x1,x2,…,xtx_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{t} some generic points in  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}}.
If  t\leq|E|-\rank{\rm J}f_{h,G} t≤|E|−rank⁡J​fh,Gt\leq|E|-\rank{\rm J}f_{h,G}, then any point in the intersection of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} and  {\rm span}\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{t}\} span⁡{x1,x2,…,xt}{\rm span}\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{t}\} is a scalar multiple of some  x_{i} xix_{i}.




Proof.



A generalised trisecant lemma [19] states the following.
Suppose  {\cal X} 𝒳{\cal X} is an irreducible, nondegenerate,  d dd-dimensional projective variety in  \mathbb{P}^{n} ℙn\mathbb{P}^{n}.
Then for any set  \{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t}\} {y1,y2,…,yt}\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t}\} of generic points in  {\cal X} 𝒳{\cal X}, we have  {\rm span}\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t}\}\cap{\cal X}=\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t}\} span⁡{y1,y2,…,yt}∩𝒳={y1,y2,…,yt}{\rm span}\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t}\}\cap{\cal X}=\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t}\}, provided  t\leq n-d t≤n−dt\leq n-d.
We apply this lemma to  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}}.
Note that  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} is an affine variety in  \mathbb{F}^{|E|} 𝔽|E|\mathbb{F}^{|E|}, whose dimension is equal to  \rank{\rm J}f_{h,G}(z) rank⁡J​fh,G​(z)\rank{\rm J}f_{h,G}(z) at a generic  z zz.
So it remains to check that  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} is irreducible and nondegenerate.
The irreducibility of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} follows from the fact that it is the image of a polynomial map.
To see the nondegeneracy, recall that  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,K_{n}^{k}}} im​fh,Knk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,K_{n}^{k}}} is the affine cone of the Veronese variety of degree  k kk, which is known to be nondegenerate (and is easily checked).
Since  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} is a projection of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,K_{n}^{k}}} im​fh,Knk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,K_{n}^{k}}} and a projection preserves nondegeneracy,  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} im​fh,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,G}} is nondegenerate.
∎




Theorem 7.15.



Suppose that  g gg is the sum of  d dd copies of  h_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{F}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} hprod:𝔽k→𝔽h_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{F}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}.
Then  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) is globally  g gg-rigid if
there exists a point-configuration  p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} p∈(𝔽d)V→𝔽p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} such that

• 



 (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid,

• 



 |E^{(k-1)}|\geq|V|+d |E(k−1)|≥|V|+d|E^{(k-1)}|\geq|V|+d,
and

• 



 \dim\left(\bigcap_{w\in\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,w}\right)=d dim(⋂w∈ker⁡J​fg,G​(p)⊤ker⁡AG,w)=d\dim\left(\bigcap_{w\in\ker{\rm J}f_{g,G}(p)^{\top}}\ker A_{G,w}\right)=d.






Proof.



In the same manner as in the proof of Theorem 7.13, we may suppose, by a sufficiently small perturbation, that  p pp is generic.
Also, by Proposition 7.2, we may assume  \mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C} 𝔽=ℂ\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}.
By Proposition 7.11, there is  T\in\mathbb{F}^{d\times d} T∈𝔽d×dT\in\mathbb{F}^{d\times d} such that
 \nabla g(q(\sigma))=T\nabla g(p(\sigma)) ∇g(q(σ))=T∇g(p(σ))\nabla g(q(\sigma))=T\nabla g(p(\sigma)) for every  \sigma\in E^{(k-1)} σ∈E(k−1)\sigma\in E^{(k-1)}.
Let  P PP be a matrix of size  d\times|E^{(k-1)}| d×|E(k−1)|d\times|E^{(k-1)}| obtained by aligning  \nabla g(p(\sigma)) ∇g​(p​(σ))\nabla g(p(\sigma)) for all  \sigma\in E^{(k-1)} σ∈E(k−1)\sigma\in E^{(k-1)},
and let  Q QQ be the corresponding matrix for  q qq.
Then  Q=TP Q=T​PQ=TP.




Let us denote the row vectors of  P PP by  x_{1},\dots,x_{d} x1,…,xdx_{1},\dots,x_{d} and those of  Q QQ by  y_{1},\dots,y_{d} y1,…,ydy_{1},\dots,y_{d}.
Also, let  H HH be a  (k-1) (k−1)(k-1)-uniform hypergraph on  V VV with the edge set  E^{(k-1)} E(k−1)E^{(k-1)}.
Since  g gg is the sum of  d dd copies of the product map of  k kk variables, each coordinate of  \nabla g(p(\sigma)) ∇g​(p​(σ))\nabla g(p(\sigma)) is also an image of the product map of  (k-1) (k−1)(k-1) variables.
So, each  x_{i} xix_{i} is the image of  f_{h,H} fh,Hf_{h,H} at a generic point configuration, where  h hh denotes the product map of  (k-1) (k−1)(k-1) variables.
Since the dimension of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,H}} im​fh,H¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,H}} is upper bounded by  n nn (as it is a projection of the affine cone of a Veronese variety), we can apply Proposition 7.14 to deduce that
any point in the intersection of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,H}} im​fh,H¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,H}} and
 \Span\{x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\} span⁡{x1,…,xd}\Span\{x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\} is a scalar multiple of some  x_{i} xix_{i}.
Since each  y_{j} yjy_{j} belongs to  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h,H}} im​fh,H¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h,H}} and  Q=TP Q=T​PQ=TP,





each  y_{j} yjy_{j} is a scalar multiple of some  x_{i} xix_{i}.

(25)


Also, by Proposition 4.2,  q qq is also regular, so  (G,q) (G,q)(G,q) is infinitesimally  g gg-rigid.
Hence, by Proposition 7.10,  \rank Q=d rank⁡Q=d\rank Q=d.
So by  Q=TP Q=T​PQ=TP and (25),
 T TT is written as  T=\Sigma D T=Σ​DT=\Sigma D for some permutation matrix  \Sigma Σ\Sigma and a diagonal matrix  D DD.
By reordering the coordinates of  q qq if necessary, we may assume  \Sigma Σ\Sigma is the identity matrix, so  T=D T=DT=D.




Let  t_{i} tit_{i} be the  i ii-th diagonal entry  T TT.
By Proposition 7.12,  q(v)=Tp(v) q⁡(v)=T​p​(v)q(v)=Tp(v) for all  v\in V(G) v∈V⁡(G)v\in V(G).
Since  f_{g,G}(p)=f_{g,G}(q) fg,G​(p)=fg,G​(q)f_{g,G}(p)=f_{g,G}(q), comparing the  e ee-th coordinate for each  e=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k}\}\in E e={i1,i2,…,ik}∈Ee=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k}\}\in E, we obtain





 \left\langle(t_{1}^{k}\ t_{2}^{k}\ \dots t_{k}^{k}),\bigodot_{j=1}^{k}p(i_{j})\right\rangle=\bigodot_{j=1}^{k}p(i_{j}), ⟨(t1k​t2k​…​tkk),⨀j=1kp⁡(ij)⟩=⨀j=1kp⁡(ij),\left\langle(t_{1}^{k}\ t_{2}^{k}\ \dots t_{k}^{k}),\bigodot_{j=1}^{k}p(i_{j})\right\rangle=\bigodot_{j=1}^{k}p(i_{j}),

(26)


where  (t_{1}^{k}\ t_{2}^{k}\ \dots t_{k}^{k}) (t1k​t2k​…​tkk)(t_{1}^{k}\ t_{2}^{k}\ \dots t_{k}^{k}) is the  k kk-dimensional vector whose  i ii-th coordinate is  t_{i}^{k} tikt_{i}^{k}.
Using the same trick as the second paragraph of this proof,
one can check from Proposition 7.14 that
there are  d dd linearly independent vectors among
 \{\bigodot_{j=1}^{k}p(i_{j}):e=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k}\}\in E\} {⨀j=1kp⁡(ij):e={i1,i2,…,ik}∈E}\{\bigodot_{j=1}^{k}p(i_{j}):e=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k}\}\in E\}.
So Equation (26) implies that
 t_{j}^{k}=1 tjk=1t_{j}^{k}=1 for every  j=1,\dots,k j=1,…,kj=1,\dots,k. In other words, each diagonal entry of  T TT is the  k kk-th root of unity.
So  T TT belongs to the stabilizer of  g gg, and the global  g gg-rigidity of  G GG follows.
∎





Note that the theorem is analogous to [38, Theorem 6.2] which gives a sufficient condition in the matrix completion case. However the proof technique does not apply in the matrix completion case. For example, the second bullet point in the hypotheses always fails in the matrix completion context.

up to simultaneous multiplication by a 4-th root of unity).

7.4 Necessary conditions




In the context of Euclidean rigidity theory, Hendrickson [35] established two necessary conditions for a graph to be globally  g gg-rigid in dimension  d dd. However, we will now provide examples that demonstrate that both of these conditions fail to be necessary for global  g gg-rigidity.




As described in [39] Hendrickson’s argument does not extend to the context of matrix completion, and hence does not extend to an arbitrary polynomial map  g gg. Hendrickson’s approach involves considering a generic globally  g gg-rigid hyper-framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) and assuming that removing an edge  e\in E(G) e∈E⁡(G)e\in E(G) leads to a configuration  (G-e,p) (G−e,p)(G-e,p) that is not  g gg-rigid. The key step in Hendrickson’s strategy is to prove that the configuration space  f_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))/Γf_{g,G}^{-1}(f_{g,G}(p))/\Gamma is compact. However, it turns out that relatively often, this configuration space can be unbounded. While it is possible that some configuration spaces might satisfy the required property to continue Hendrickson’s strategy, this is not the case in several natural examples. This indicates that redundant rigidity is not necessarily a prerequisite for global  g gg-rigidity in the multilinear case, as demonstrated by the following lemma, providing an infinite family of examples.



Lemma 7.17.



Assume Setup 2.1,
and suppose  g gg is multilinear.
Then any simple  d dd-valent extension that preserves local  g gg-rigidity preserves global  g gg-rigidity.
In other words, if  G GG is globally  g gg-rigid
and a simple  d dd-valent extension  H HH of  G GG is locally  g gg-rigid, then  H HH is globally  g gg-rigid.




Proof.



Let  G GG be a globally  g gg-rigid graph
and suppose  H HH is obtained from  G GG by a simple  d dd-valent extension by adding a new vertex  v vv.
Pick any generic  p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V(H)} p∈(𝔽d)V⁡(H)p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V(H)}. We will show that  (H,p) (H,p)(H,p) is globally  g gg-rigid.




For this, choose any  q\in f_{g,H}^{-1}(f_{g,H}(p)) q∈fg,H−1​(fg,H​(p))q\in f_{g,H}^{-1}(f_{g,H}(p)).
Since  G GG is globally  g gg-rigid, we may assume
 q(u)=p(u) q⁡(u)=p⁡(u)q(u)=p(u) for all  u\in V(G) u∈V⁡(G)u\in V(G).
Since  g gg is multilinear,  f_{g,H}(p)=f_{g,G}(q) fg,H​(p)=fg,G​(q)f_{g,H}(p)=f_{g,G}(q) leads to the equality:





 \langle q(v)\nabla g(q(e_{i}-v))\rangle=\langle p(v),\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v))\rangle, ⟨q(v)∇g(q(ei−v))⟩=⟨p(v),∇g(p(ei−v))⟩,\langle q(v)\nabla g(q(e_{i}-v))\rangle=\langle p(v),\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v))\rangle,



for all hyperedges  e_{1},\dots,e_{d} e1,…,ede_{1},\dots,e_{d} incident to  v vv.
Since the  d dd-valent extension is simple,  e_{i}-v ei−ve_{i}-v does not contain  v vv, we have that  q(u)=p(u) q⁡(u)=p⁡(u)q(u)=p(u) for any  u\in V(G) u∈V⁡(G)u\in V(G) and so:





 \langle(p(v)-q(v)),\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v))\rangle=0, ⟨(p⁡(v)−q⁡(v)),∇g​(p⁡(ei−v))⟩=0,\langle(p(v)-q(v)),\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v))\rangle=0,



for  i=1,\dots,d i=1,…,di=1,\dots,d.
From the fact that the extension preserves local  g gg-rigidity and Lemma 6.2,
 \{\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v)):i=1,\dots,d\} {∇g(p(ei−v)):i=1,…,d}\{\nabla g(p(e_{i}-v)):i=1,\dots,d\} is a basis of  \mathbb{F}^{d} 𝔽d\mathbb{F}^{d}.
So we get  p(v)=q(v) p⁡(v)=q⁡(v)p(v)=q(v), implying that  p=q p=qp=q and  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is globally  g gg-rigid.
∎





By repeatedly applying the lemma, we can construct an infinite family of hypergraphs that exhibit both minimally local  g gg-rigidity and global  g gg-rigidity when  g gg is a multilinear polynomial map.
There are also infinite families of hypergraphs which are locally  g gg-rigid but not globally  g gg-rigid.




We next consider Hendrickson’s second necessary condition which is in terms of graph connectivity. We will present an analogous condition where the level of connectivity depends strongly on the map  g gg.




Let  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) be an  k kk-uniform hypergraph.
A subset  X\subset V X⊂VX\subset V is called a separator if
 G-X G−XG-X is disconnected.
We say that  G GG is  r rr-connected if  |V(G)|\geq r+1 |V⁡(G)|≥r+1|V(G)|\geq r+1 and it has no separator of size less than  r rr.




The following lemma extends the well-known fact that, in the  d dd-dimensional Euclidean rigidity,  d dd-connectivity is necessary for local rigidity.



Lemma 7.18.



Assume Setup 2.1, and suppose  |V|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+1 |V|≥nΓg+1|V|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}+1.
If  G GG is locally  g gg-rigid, then  G GG is  n_{\Gamma_{g}} nΓgn_{\Gamma_{g}}-connected.




Proof.



We first introduce some terminology for the proof.
Recall that  \mathfrak{g} 𝔤\mathfrak{g} denotes the Lie algebra of  \Gamma_{g} Γg\Gamma_{g}.
For a point configuration  q\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{n} q∈(𝔽d)nq\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{n},
let  {\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(q)=\{\gamma\in\mathfrak{g}:\gamma\cdot q=0\} stab𝔤​(q)={γ∈𝔤:γ⋅q=0}{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(q)=\{\gamma\in\mathfrak{g}:\gamma\cdot q=0\}.
Clearly  {\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(q) stab𝔤​(q){\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(q) is a linear subspace of  \mathfrak{g} 𝔤\mathfrak{g}.
By definition of  n_{\Gamma_{g}} nΓgn_{\Gamma_{g}},
 {\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(q)=\{0\} stab𝔤​(q)={0}{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(q)=\{0\} if and only if  n\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} n≥nΓgn\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}.




We now move to the proof.
Let  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) be a generic framework of  G GG,
and suppose that  G GG has a separator of size less than  n_{\Gamma_{g}} nΓgn_{\Gamma_{g}}.
Since  |V(G)|\geq n_{\Gamma}+1 |V⁡(G)|≥nΓ+1|V(G)|\geq n_{\Gamma}+1,  G GG has a separator  X XX of size equal to  n_{\Gamma_{g}}-1 nΓg−1n_{\Gamma_{g}}-1.
Then we can take  V_{1},V_{2}\subseteq V(G) V1,V2⊆V⁡(G)V_{1},V_{2}\subseteq V(G) such that
 V_{i}\setminus X\neq\emptyset(i=1,2),V(G)=V_{1}\cup V_{2},X=V_{1}\cap V_{2} Vi∖X≠∅​(i=1,2),V⁡(G)=V1∪V2,X=V1∩V2V_{i}\setminus X\neq\emptyset(i=1,2),V(G)=V_{1}\cup V_{2},X=V_{1}\cap V_{2}, and there is no hyperedge intersecting  V_{1}\setminus X V1∖XV_{1}\setminus X and  V_{2}\setminus X V2∖XV_{2}\setminus X.
Moreover, since  |X|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}-1 |X|≥nΓg−1|X|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}-1, we have that  |V_{i}|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} |Vi|≥nΓg|V_{i}|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}}.




Since  |X|<n_{\Gamma_{g}} |X|<nΓg|X|<n_{\Gamma_{g}},
 {\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|X})\neq\{0\} stab𝔤(p|X)≠{0}{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|X})\neq\{0\}.
Also, since  p pp is generic and  V_{2}\setminus X\neq\emptyset V2∖X≠∅V_{2}\setminus X\neq\emptyset, we have  {\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|V_{2}})\subsetneq{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|X}) stab𝔤(p|V2)⊊stab𝔤(p|X){\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|V_{2}})\subsetneq{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|X}).
Pick any  \gamma\in{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|X})\setminus{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|V_{2}}) γ∈stab𝔤(p|X)∖stab𝔤(p|V2)\gamma\in{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|X})\setminus{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|V_{2}}),
and define  \dot{p}\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V(G)} p˙∈(𝔽d)V⁡(G)\dot{p}\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V(G)} by





 \dot{p}(v)=\begin{cases}0&(v\in V_{1}\setminus X)\\
0=\gamma\cdot p(v)&(v\in X)\\
\gamma\cdot p(v)&(v\in V_{2}\setminus X).\end{cases} p˙​(v)={0(v∈V1∖X)0=γ⋅p⁡(v)(v∈X)γ⋅p⁡(v)(v∈V2∖X).\dot{p}(v)=\begin{cases}0&(v\in V_{1}\setminus X)\\
0=\gamma\cdot p(v)&(v\in X)\\
\gamma\cdot p(v)&(v\in V_{2}\setminus X).\end{cases}



From the fact that  \gamma\in\mathfrak{g} γ∈𝔤\gamma\in\mathfrak{g} and there is no hyperedge intersecting  V_{1}\setminus X V1∖XV_{1}\setminus X and  V_{2}\setminus X V2∖XV_{2}\setminus X,  \dot{p} p˙\dot{p} is an infinitesimal  g gg-motion of  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p).
Also,  \dot{p} p˙\dot{p} is nonzero since  \gamma\notin{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|V_{2}}) γ∉stab𝔤(p|V2)\gamma\notin{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|V_{2}}).
Moreover,  \dot{p} p˙\dot{p} is not trivial.
Indeed, if  \dot{p} p˙\dot{p} is trivial,
then there must be some  \gamma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g} γ′∈𝔤\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g} such that  \dot{p}(v)=\gamma^{\prime}\cdot p(v) p˙​(v)=γ′⋅p⁡(v)\dot{p}(v)=\gamma^{\prime}\cdot p(v) for all  v\in V(G) v∈V⁡(G)v\in V(G).
Then, for all  v\in V_{1} v∈V1v\in V_{1},  \gamma^{\prime}\cdot p(v)=\dot{p}(v)=0 γ′⋅p⁡(v)=p˙​(v)=0\gamma^{\prime}\cdot p(v)=\dot{p}(v)=0, implying
 \gamma^{\prime}\in{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|V_{1}}) γ′∈stab𝔤(p|V1)\gamma^{\prime}\in{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|V_{1}}).
Since  |V_{1}|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} |V1|≥nΓg|V_{1}|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}} and hence
 {\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|V_{1}})=\{0\} stab𝔤(p|V1)={0}{\rm stab}_{\mathfrak{g}}(p_{|V_{1}})=\{0\}, this further implies
 \gamma^{\prime}=0 γ′=0\gamma^{\prime}=0.
On the other hand, for any  v\notin V_{1} v∉V1v\notin V_{1},
 \dot{p}(v)=\gamma^{\prime}\cdot p(v)=0 p˙​(v)=γ′⋅p⁡(v)=0\dot{p}(v)=\gamma^{\prime}\cdot p(v)=0, contradicting that  \dot{p} p˙\dot{p} is nonzero.
Thus,  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) admits a non-trivial infinitesimal  g gg-motion,
and  G GG is not locally  g gg-rigid.
∎





Since local  g gg-rigidity is a necessary condition for global  g gg-rigidity, the same necessary connectivity condition holds for global  g gg-rigidity. The rank 1 tensor completion problem shows that this cannot be improved. However in many applications, such as Euclidean rigidity and higher rank tensor completions, one may add one to the connectivity condition; that is for many choices of  g gg, global  g gg-rigidity implies  (n_{\Gamma_{g}}+1) (nΓg+1)(n_{\Gamma_{g}}+1)-connectivity.






8 Multipartite Rigidity Model




In certain applications, we will encounter the identifiability question defined using a structure of  k kk-partiteness of  k kk-partite hypergraphs. We now introduce the corresponding rigidity concept and analyse the unique tensor completion problem as an illustrative example.




8.1 Birigidity




We say that a hypergraph  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) is  k kk-partite if
there is a partition  \{V_{1},\dots,V_{k}\} {V1,…,Vk}\{V_{1},\dots,V_{k}\} of  V VV into  k kk nonempty subsets  V_{i} ViV_{i} such that  |V_{i}\cap e|=1 |Vi∩e|=1|V_{i}\cap e|=1 for any  e\in E e∈Ee\in E and  i=1,\dots,k i=1,…,ki=1,\dots,k.
For example, when  k=2 k=2k=2, a  k kk-partite hypergraph is an ordinary bipartite graph.




Suppose  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is a  k kk-partite hyper-framework.
When we denote a hyperedge  e ee as  e=(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in E e=(v1,…,vk)∈Ee=(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in E, we mean that  v_{i}\in V_{i} vi∈Viv_{i}\in V_{i} for  i=1,\ldots,k i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
As in Setup 2.1,
for a polynomial map  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F},
we define
the  g gg-measurement map  f_{g,G}:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{E} fg,G:(𝔽d)V→𝔽Ef_{g,G}:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{E}.
Since  G GG is  k kk-partite,  g gg may not be symmetric or anti-symmetric.




The main difference from the rigidity model given in Section 2 occurs in the action of the affine group to  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k} (𝔽d)k(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}.
Suppose the general affine group  {\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) Aff⁡(d,𝔽){\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) acts on  \mathbb{F}^{d} 𝔽d\mathbb{F}^{d} by  \gamma\cdot x=Ax+t γ⋅x=A​x+t\gamma\cdot x=Ax+t for  \gamma=(A,t) γ=(A,t)\gamma=(A,t), as before.
In the multipartite model, we consider the direct product  {\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F})^{k} Aff​(d,𝔽)k{\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F})^{k} of  k kk copies of  {\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}) Aff⁡(d,𝔽){\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F}),
and consider the component-wise action of  {\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F})^{k} Aff​(d,𝔽)k{\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F})^{k} to  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k} (𝔽d)k(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}.
The induced action on  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} is given by  \gamma\cdot g(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=g(\gamma_{1}^{-1}\cdot x_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}^{-1}x_{k}) γ⋅g⁡(x1,…,xk)=g⁡(γ1−1⋅x1,…,γk−1​xk)\gamma\cdot g(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=g(\gamma_{1}^{-1}\cdot x_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}^{-1}x_{k}) for  x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\mathbb{F}^{d} x1,…,xk∈𝔽dx_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\mathbb{F}^{d} and  \gamma=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k})\in{\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F})^{k} γ=(γ1,…,γk)∈Aff​(d,𝔽)k\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k})\in{\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F})^{k}.
The stabilizer of  g gg is denoted by  \Gamma_{g}^{\times} Γg×\Gamma_{g}^{\times}.




Since in this  k kk-partite model the underlying vertex set  V VV is assumed to be partitioned into  \{V_{1},\dots,V_{k}\} {V1,…,Vk}\{V_{1},\dots,V_{k}\}, we can also define the action of  {\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F})^{k} Aff​(d,𝔽)k{\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F})^{k} to  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} (𝔽d)V(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} such that
 (\gamma\cdot p)(v)=\gamma_{i}\cdot p(v) (γ⋅p)​(v)=γi⋅p⁡(v)(\gamma\cdot p)(v)=\gamma_{i}\cdot p(v) for each  v\in V_{i} v∈Viv\in V_{i} for
 p\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} p∈(𝔽d)Vp\in(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} and  \gamma=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k})\in{\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F})^{k} γ=(γ1,…,γk)∈Aff​(d,𝔽)k\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k})\in{\rm Aff}(d,\mathbb{F})^{k}.



Definition 8.1.



We say that  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is globally  g gg-birigid if
for any  q\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p)) q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))q\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p)) there is  \gamma\in\Gamma_{G}^{\times} γ∈ΓG×\gamma\in\Gamma_{G}^{\times} such that
 q=\gamma\cdot p q=γ⋅pq=\gamma\cdot p.
We say that  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is locally  g gg-birigid if there is an open neighbourhood  N NN of  p pp in  (\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} (𝔽d)V(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{V} (in the Euclidean topology) such that
for any  q\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p))\cap N q∈fg,G−1​(fg,G​(p))∩Nq\in f^{-1}_{g,G}(f_{g,G}(p))\cap N there is  \gamma\in\Gamma_{G}^{\times} γ∈ΓG×\gamma\in\Gamma_{G}^{\times} such that  q=\gamma\cdot p q=γ⋅pq=\gamma\cdot p.




Example 8.2.



Consider the case when  G GG is 2-uniform (i.e., a graph) and  g(x,y)=\langle x,y\rangle g⁡(x,y)=⟨x,y⟩g(x,y)=\langle x,y\rangle for  x,y\in\mathbb{F}^{d} x,y∈𝔽dx,y\in\mathbb{F}^{d}.
In the model in Section 2,
the stabilizer of  g gg is  O(d) O⁡(d)O(d) and its Lie algebra is  {d\choose 2} (d2){d\choose 2}-dimensional.
On the other hand, in the  k kk-partite model,
the stabilizer of  g gg is  \{(A,A^{-\top}):A\in{\rm GL}(d,\mathbb{F})\} {(A,A−⁣⊤):A∈GL⁡(d,𝔽)}\{(A,A^{-\top}):A\in{\rm GL}(d,\mathbb{F})\},
and its Lie algebra is  d^{2} d2d^{2}-dimensional.





We define the infinitesimal  g gg-birigidity in the same manner as described in Section 4.2. To do so, we denote  d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}} dΓg×d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}} for the dimension of the variety  \Gamma_{g}^{\times} Γg×\Gamma_{g}^{\times},
and let  {\rm triv}_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}(p) trivΓg×​(p){\rm triv}_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}(p) be the space of trivial infinitesimal motions of  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p).
If  |V_{i}| |Vi||V_{i}| is sufficiently large for all  i ii, then  \dim{\rm triv}_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}(p)=d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}} dimtrivΓg×​(p)=dΓg×\dim{\rm triv}_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}(p)=d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}.
The minimum size of  V_{i} ViV_{i} that guarantees this property is denoted by  n_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}} nΓg×n_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}.
Specifically, let





 n_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}:=\min\{|V_{i}|:\dim{\rm triv}_{\Gamma}(p)=d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}\text{ for some }p:V\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{d}\}. nΓg×:=min{|Vi|:dimtrivΓ(p)=dΓg× for some p:V→𝔽d}.n_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}:=\min\{|V_{i}|:\dim{\rm triv}_{\Gamma}(p)=d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}\text{ for some }p:V\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{d}\}.






All the materials in Section 4 can be extended to the multipartite rigidity model.
For example, the following proposition can be shown easily.



Proposition 8.3.



Let  g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} g:(𝔽d)k→𝔽g:(\mathbb{F}^{d})^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F} be a polynomial map and  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) be a  k kk-partite hypergraph with
 |V_{i}|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}} |Vi|≥nΓg×|V_{i}|\geq n_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}.
Then the following are equivalent:

• 



 (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is locally  g gg-birigid for some generic  p pp.

• 



 (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) is infinitesimally  g gg-birigid for some generic  p pp.

• 



The rank of  {\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) J​fg,G​(p){\rm J}f_{g,G}(p) is equal to  d|V|-d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}} d​|V|−dΓg×d|V|-d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}} for some generic  p pp.

• 



 \dim(\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}(p)})=d|V|-d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}} dim(im​fg,G​(p)¯)=d​|V|−dΓg×\dim(\overline{{\rm im}f_{g,G}(p)})=d|V|-d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}.







Let  K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k} Kn1,…,nkkK_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k} be the  k kk-uniform complete hypergraph with  |V_{i}|=n_{i} |Vi|=ni|V_{i}|=n_{i}.
The generic  g gg-birigidity matroid  {\cal M}_{g,n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{\times} ℳg,n1,…,nk×{\cal M}_{g,n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{\times} is defined on  E(K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k}) E⁡(Kn1,…,nkk)E(K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k}) whose independence is defined by the row independence in  {\rm J}f_{g,K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{r}}(p) J​fg,Kn1,…,nkr​(p){\rm J}f_{g,K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{r}}(p) at a generic  p pp.
If  g gg is symmetric or anti-symmetric,  {\cal M}_{g,n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{\times} ℳg,n1,…,nk×{\cal M}_{g,n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{\times} is the restriction of the  g gg-rigidity matroid  {\cal M}_{g,n} ℳg,n{\cal M}_{g,n} of  K_{n}^{r} KnrK_{n}^{r} to the edge set of  K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k} Kn1,…,nkkK_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k} by regarding
 K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k} Kn1,…,nkkK_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k} as a subgraph of  K_{n}^{k} KnkK_{n}^{k} with  n=n_{1}+\dots+n_{k} n=n1+⋯+nkn=n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}.





8.2 Tensor Completion




A primary example of  g gg-birigidity occurs in the rectangular tensor completion problem.




Let  W_{1},\dots,W_{k} W1,…,WkW_{1},\dots,W_{k} be a collection of finite dimensional vector spaces over  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C}. We denote  W_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes W_{k} W1⊗⋯⊗WkW_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes W_{k} for the set of all order  k kk tensors of dimensions  n_{1}=\dim(W_{1}),\dots,n_{k}=\dim(W_{k}) n1=dim(W1),…,nk=dim(Wk)n_{1}=\dim(W_{1}),\dots,n_{k}=\dim(W_{k}).
We fix a basis for each  W_{i} WiW_{i}, and assume that each  T\in W_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes W_{k} T∈W1⊗⋯⊗WkT\in W_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes W_{k} is represented by a  k kk-dimensional array of numbers in  \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C}.
In particular, any tensor can be written as





 T=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{1}\otimes x_{i}^{2}\otimes\dots\otimes x_{i}^{k} T=∑i=1dxi1⊗xi2⊗⋯⊗xikT=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{1}\otimes x_{i}^{2}\otimes\dots\otimes x_{i}^{k}

(27)


for some vectors  x_{i}^{j}\in W_{j} xij∈Wjx_{i}^{j}\in W_{j} and  \lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{r}\in\mathbb{F} λ1,…,λr∈𝔽\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{r}\in\mathbb{F}.
The smallest possible  d dd for which we can write  T TT in the form of Equation (27) is called the (CP) rank of  T TT.




As in the case of matrix completion or symmetric tensor completion, we can ask the low rank tensor completion problem as the problem of filling the missing entries of a given partially-filled matrix. Let  V_{i}=[n_{i}] Vi=[ni]V_{i}=[n_{i}] for  1\leq i\leq k 1≤i≤k1\leq i\leq k and  V VV be the disjoint union of all  V_{i} ViV_{i}.
We can use a subset  E EE of  V_{1}\times\dots\times V_{k} V1×⋯×VkV_{1}\times\dots\times V_{k} to represent the known entries in the tensor completion problem. In this manner, we encode the underlying combinatorics of each instance of the completion problem using a  k kk-partite hypergraph  (V,E) (V,E)(V,E).




As in the symmetric tensor completion, the decomposition in Equation (27) can be converted to a form of an algebraic relation among points in  \mathbb{C}^{d} ℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}.
This gives the following equivalent formulation of the tensor completion problem: Given a  k kk-partite hypergraph  G=(V,E) G=(V,E)G=(V,E) with  E\subseteq V_{1}\times\dots\times V_{k} E⊆V1×⋯×VkE\subseteq V_{1}\times\dots\times V_{k} and  a_{e}\in\mathbb{C} ae∈ℂa_{e}\in\mathbb{C} for  e\in E e∈Ee\in E, find  p:V\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{r} p:V→ℂrp:V\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{r} such that





 \mathbf{1}\cdot\bigodot_{v\in e}p(v)=a_{e}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $e\in E$}. 𝟏⋅⨀v∈ep⁡(v)=aefor e∈E.\mathbf{1}\cdot\bigodot_{v\in e}p(v)=a_{e}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $e\in E$}.

(28)


Observe that this equation is the same as Equation (5), and we can recast the rectangular tensor completion problem as a special case of the symmetric tensor completion when the underlying hypergraph is  k kk-partite.




The unique completion problem can be formulated as the  g gg-birigidity problem using the same polynomial map  g gg as that in the symmetric tensor case, that is, the sum of  d dd copies of the product function  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod}. The key difference from the symmetric tensor case is the stabilizer  \Gamma_{g}^{\times} Γg×\Gamma_{g}^{\times}.
When  k\geq 3 k≥3k\geq 3,
 \Gamma_{g}^{\times} Γg×\Gamma_{g}^{\times} is the set of  k kk-tuples  (D_{1}\Sigma,D_{2}\Sigma,\dots,D_{k}\Sigma) (D1​Σ,D2​Σ,…,Dk​Σ)(D_{1}\Sigma,D_{2}\Sigma,\dots,D_{k}\Sigma) such that each  D_{i} DiD_{i} is a diagonal matrix of size  d dd with  \prod_{i=1}^{k}D_{i}=I_{d} ∏i=1kDi=Id\prod_{i=1}^{k}D_{i}=I_{d}
and  \Sigma Σ\Sigma is a permutation matrix.
Then  d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}=d(k-1) dΓg×=d⁡(k−1)d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}=d(k-1) and
 n_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}=1 nΓg×=1n_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}=1.




The fact that  d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}=d(k-1) dΓg×=d⁡(k−1)d_{\Gamma_{g}^{\times}}=d(k-1), in particular, implies that the rank of the  g gg-birigidity matroid of  K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{r} Kn1,…,nkrK_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{r} is at most  d(n_{1}+\dots+n_{k})-d(r-1) d⁡(n1+⋯+nk)−d⁡(r−1)d(n_{1}+\dots+n_{k})-d(r-1).
An obvious question is whether the equality always holds (equivalently, whether  K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{r} Kn1,…,nkrK_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{r} is locally  g gg-birigid).
This question is equivalent to determining the non-defectivity of Segre varieties.




Indeed, since  g=dh_{\rm prod} g=d​hprodg=dh_{\rm prod} is the sum of  d dd copies of  h_{\rm prod} hprodh_{\rm prod},
 \overline{{\rm im}f_{dh_{\rm prod},G}} im​fd​hprod,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{dh_{\rm prod},G}} is the  d dd-secant of  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},G}} im​fhprod,G¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},G}}.
When  G=K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k} G=Kn1,…,nkkG=K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k},  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k}}} im​fhprod,Kn1,…,nkk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k}}} is the affine cone of the Segre variety with parameter  (n_{1},\dots,n_{k}) (n1,…,nk)(n_{1},\dots,n_{k}) whose dimension is  n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}-(k-1) n1+⋯+nk−(k−1)n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}-(k-1).
Therefore,  K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k} Kn1,…,nkkK_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k} is locally  g gg-birigid
if and only if  \overline{{\rm im}f_{h^{\rm prod},K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k}}} im​fhprod,Kn1,…,nkk¯\overline{{\rm im}f_{h^{\rm prod},K_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}^{k}}} is not  d dd-defective.




Unlike the Veronese case, the non-defectivity problem of Segre varieties has not been fully resolved, and it remains an open question in algebraic geometry. Although there is a long-standing conjecture that aims to provide a complete answer, a conclusive proof is still lacking, see, e.g. [10].






9 Applications




Our formulation of  g gg-rigidity clarifies a connection between graph rigidity theory and the analysis of secant varieties. We believe this connection is fruitful, and we can obtain further results if we explore structures of specific varieties. As motivating evidence, we briefly provide two examples.




9.1 Random projection of Secant varieties along coordinate axis




Let  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} be an affine variety of dimension  m mm.
The Noether normalization theorem says that
if  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} is projected to a random  t tt-dimensional space
then with high probability the Zariski closure of the resulting set has dimension equal to  \min\{m,t\} min⁡{m,t}\min\{m,t\}.
Hence  t=m t=mt=m is the threshold to preserve the dimension by a random projection.
As we have seen in Section 4.6, this fact is no longer true if the projection is an orthogonal projection along the coordinate axis.
The question we want to address here is to estimate the threshold dimension  t tt such that a random axis-aligned projection to a  t tt-dimensional subspace preserves the dimension.
We give an asymptotic answer to this question.




Although our strategy works in the full generality of  g gg-rigidity, for simplicity of the description we shall focus on the case when  {\cal V} 𝒱{\cal V} is the  d dd-secant of the balanced Segre variety.
Indeed this is one of the most important cases in both theory and applications since it answers a question about the sampling probability of entries in the low-rank tensor completion problem to guarantee the unique recovery.
It is an important problem in machine learning to identify the sampling complexity for the unique recovery of low-rank tensors, see, e.g. [8, 60].




Let us recall the setting of Section 8.2.
Let  K_{n,\dots,n}^{k} Kn,…,nkK_{n,\dots,n}^{k} be the complete  k kk-partite hypergraph with
 |V_{i}|=n |Vi|=n|V_{i}|=n,
and let  h_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} hprod:ℂk→ℂh_{\rm prod}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} be the product function. Then  {\cal S}_{n}:=\overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n,\dots,n}^{k}}} 𝒮n:=im​fhprod,Kn,…,nk¯{\cal S}_{n}:=\overline{{\rm im}f_{h_{\rm prod},K_{n,\dots,n}^{k}}} is the affine cone of the Segre variety with balanced parameter  (n,\dots,n) (n,…,n)(n,\dots,n) whose dimension is  kn-(k-1) k​n−(k−1)kn-(k-1).




We are interested in the dimension of a random axis-parallel projection of  {\rm Sect}_{d}({\cal S}_{n}) Sectd​(𝒮n){\rm Sect}_{d}({\cal S}_{n}) (recalling the definition of  d dd-secant given in Equation (13)).
Namely, we analyse the dimension of  \pi_{G}({\rm Sect}_{d}({\cal S}_{n})) πG​(Sectd​(𝒮n))\pi_{G}({\rm Sect}_{d}({\cal S}_{n})) for a random subgraph  G GG of  K_{n,\dots,n}^{k} Kn,…,nkK_{n,\dots,n}^{k}.
We consider the Erdős-Rényi type random subgraph model  \mathbb{G}_{n,t} 𝔾n,t\mathbb{G}_{n,t}, which is
a probability distribution over the subgraphs of  K_{n,\dots,n}^{r} Kn,…,nrK_{n,\dots,n}^{r}
defined by selecting each hyperedge with probability  t tt independently.




Our main result in this subsection is the following.



Theorem 9.1.



Let  n,k,d n,k,dn,k,d be positive integers with  n\geq d n≥dn\geq d, and  c cc be any number with  c>1 c>1c>1.
Let  \mathbb{G}_{n,t} 𝔾n,t\mathbb{G}_{n,t} be the random subgraph model of  K_{n,\dots,n}^{k} Kn,…,nkK_{n,\dots,n}^{k}, and  G\sim\mathbb{G}_{n,t} G∼𝔾n,tG\sim\mathbb{G}_{n,t}.
If





 1\geq t\geq\frac{kd^{k-1}\log(cdk^{2}n)}{n^{k-1}}, 1≥t≥k​dk−1​log⁡(c​d​k2​n)nk−1,1\geq t\geq\frac{kd^{k-1}\log(cdk^{2}n)}{n^{k-1}},



then with probability at least  1-\frac{1}{c} 1−1c1-\frac{1}{c},
 G GG is locally  dh_{prod} d​hp​r​o​ddh_{prod}-birigid, i.e.,
the projection  \pi_{G} πG\pi_{G} preserves the dimension of  {\rm Sect}_{d}({\cal S}_{n}) Sectd​(𝒮n){\rm Sect}_{d}({\cal S}_{n}).





Theorem 9.1 implies that,
among  n^{k} nkn^{k} hypereges in  K_{n,\dots,n}^{k} Kn,…,nkK_{n,\dots,n}^{k}, only  O(n\log n) O⁡(n​log⁡n)O(n\log n) hyperedges are enough to make a random sub-hypergraph  dh_{prod} d​hp​r​o​ddh_{prod}-birigid.
This bound is asymptotically tight (in  n nn)
since  G GG has an isolated vertex with high probability if
 t<\frac{\log n}{n^{k-1}} t<log⁡nnk−1t<\frac{\log n}{n^{k-1}}.




We prove Theorem 9.1 by adapting the argument in [42] for proving a corresponding statement for Euclidean rigidity.
The argument in [42] is based on the following Matrix Chernoff bound.
For a non-zero positive semidefinite matrix  X XX,  \lambda_{\max}(X) λmax​(X)\lambda_{\max}(X) denotes the largest eigenvalue and  \lambda_{\min}(X) λmin​(X)\lambda_{\min}(X) denotes the smallest non-zero eigenvalue.



Theorem 9.2.



Let  E EE be a finite set,  c\geq 1 c≥1c\geq 1, and
 X_{e} XeX_{e} be a positive semidefinite  m\times m m×mm\times m matrix indexed by  e ee for each  e\in E e∈Ee\in E.
Let  r=\rank\sum_{e\in E}X_{e} r=rank∑e∈EXer=\rank\sum_{e\in E}X_{e}
and  h=\max_{e\in E}{\lambda_{\max}(X_{e})} h=maxe∈E⁡λmax​(Xe)h=\max_{e\in E}{\lambda_{\max}(X_{e})}.
If





 1\geq t\geq\frac{h\log(rc)}{\lambda_{\min}(\sum_{e\in E}X_{e})}, 1≥t≥h​log⁡(r​c)λmin​(∑e∈EXe),1\geq t\geq\frac{h\log(rc)}{\lambda_{\min}(\sum_{e\in E}X_{e})},



then for a subset  E(t) E⁡(t)E(t) of  E EE obtained by taking each  e\in E e∈Ee\in E independently with probability  t tt,
we have
 \rank\sum_{e\in E(t)}X_{e}=r rank∑e∈E⁡(t)Xe=r\rank\sum_{e\in E(t)}X_{e}=r with probability at least  1-\frac{1}{c} 1−1c1-\frac{1}{c}.





Theorem 9.2 is an adaptation of the Matrix Chernoff bound (see, e.g., [67]), and a detailed exposition of this form can be seen in [42].




Theorem 9.1 is a direct corollary of Theorem 9.2 and the following lemma concerning the smallest nonzero singular value of the Jacobian of the measurement map.
The following lemma is based on a discussion with Kota Nakagawa.
The proof can be considered as a quantitative extension of the proof of Theorem 6.6.



Lemma 9.3.



Let  n,d,k n,d,kn,d,k be positive integers with  k\geq 3 k≥3k\geq 3 and  n\geq d n≥dn\geq d,
and denote  V=V(K_{n,\dots,n}^{k}) V=V⁡(Kn,…,nk)V=V(K_{n,\dots,n}^{k})
and  f=f_{dh_{\rm prod},K_{n,\dots,n}^{k}} f=fd​hprod,Kn,…,nkf=f_{dh_{\rm prod},K_{n,\dots,n}^{k}}.
Then there exists  p:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d} p:V→ℝdp:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d} such that

 \rank{\rm J}f(p)=d(kn-(k-1)) rank⁡J​f​(p)=d⁡(k​n−(k−1))\rank{\rm J}f(p)=d(kn-(k-1)),

(ii)

(iii) 



each row of  {\rm J}f(p) J​f​(p){\rm J}f(p) has Euclidean norm at most  \sqrt{k} k\sqrt{k} as a vector.






Proof.

We show the properties of the statement for  {\rm J}f(p) J​f​(p){\rm J}f(p).




The idea of the proof is the same as that of Theorem 6.6.
Since the definition of  p pp above is very special, the entries of  {\rm J}f(p) J​f​(p){\rm J}f(p) is well structured as in Claim 1 and

Then we will analyse each block by applying a known fact from spectral graph theory.




For such an analysis, we first partition a subset of  E(K_{n\dots,n}^{k}) E⁡(Kn​…,nk)E(K_{n\dots,n}^{k}) into  2d 2​d2d subsets as follows: for  j=1,\dots,d j=1,…,dj=1,\dots,d,

∎





Observe that, for  (u,j),(v,j)\in I^{\prime}_{j} (u,j),(v,j)∈Ij′(u,j),(v,j)\in I^{\prime}_{j},

Thus,  \dim\ker Q_{j}=k-1 dimker⁡Qj=k−1\dim\ker Q_{j}=k-1 and  \lambda_{\min}(Q_{j})=\frac{n^{k-1}}{d^{k-1}} λmin​(Qj)=nk−1dk−1\lambda_{\min}(Q_{j})=\frac{n^{k-1}}{d^{k-1}}.
This completes the proof.
∎




Proof of Theorem 9.1.



Pick  p:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d} p:V→ℝdp:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d} given in Lemma 9.3,
and for simplicity let us denote  R={\rm J}f_{dh_{\rm prod},K_{n,\dots,n}^{k}}(p) R=J​fd​hprod,Kn,…,nk​(p)R={\rm J}f_{dh_{\rm prod},K_{n,\dots,n}^{k}}(p).

 R_{e} ReR_{e} be the row vector of  R RR associated with  e ee.

and denote  R_{G}={\rm J}f_{dh_{\rm prod},G}(p) RG=J​fd​hprod,G​(p)R_{G}={\rm J}f_{dh_{\rm prod},G}(p).
Then  R_{G}^{\top}R_{G}=\sum_{e\in E(G)}X_{e} RG⊤​RG=∑e∈E⁡(G)XeR_{G}^{\top}R_{G}=\sum_{e\in E(G)}X_{e},
and one can apply Theorem 9.2 to get a lower bound of  t tt for  G\sim\mathbb{G}_{n,t} G∼𝔾n,tG\sim\mathbb{G}_{n,t} to have  \rank R_{G}^{\top}R_{G}=\rank R^{\top}R rank⁡RG⊤​RG=rank⁡R⊤​R\rank R_{G}^{\top}R_{G}=\rank R^{\top}R.
Specifically, since  \lambda_{\min}(R^{\top}R)\geq\frac{n^{k-1}}{d^{k-1}} λmin​(R⊤​R)≥nk−1dk−1\lambda_{\min}(R^{\top}R)\geq\frac{n^{k-1}}{d^{k-1}}
by Lemma 9.3(ii)

when





 1\geq t\geq\frac{kd^{k-1}\log(cdk(kn-(k-1)))}{n^{k-1}}. 1≥t≥k​dk−1​log⁡(c​d​k​(k​n−(k−1)))nk−1.1\geq t\geq\frac{kd^{k-1}\log(cdk(kn-(k-1)))}{n^{k-1}}.



Since  \rank R_{G}=\rank R=d(kn-(k-1)) rank⁡RG=rank⁡R=d⁡(k​n−(k−1))\rank R_{G}=\rank R=d(kn-(k-1)),
the local  dh_{\rm prod} d​hproddh_{\rm prod}-birigidity of  G GG follows.
∎





It is an open problem to establish the global rigidity counterpart of Theorem 9.1.
For Euclidean rigidity, there are various methods available to establish a connection between local rigidity and global rigidity. Extending these methods would be a fruitful direction for further research.





9.2 Identifiability of projections of  p pp-Cayley-Menger varieties and  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-norm global rigidity




In Section 3, we briefly looked at graph rigidity in  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-space, where
instead of conventional Euclidean distances
we are concerned with  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-distances between points in a real vector space.
An extensive study has been done for  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p} local rigidity,
but  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p} global rigidity is not yet well understood [28, 30].
A major open problem in this context is whether global rigidity in  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-space is a generic property of graphs as in the case of Euclidean global rigidity by Gortler-Hearly-Thurston [32],
see a discussion in Section 7.




The difficulty to extend techniques in Euclidean global rigidity is the lack of an obvious counterpart definition of stress matrices.
The theory of stress matrices, developed by Connelly [24, 25], is currently the central tool in Euclidean global rigidity analysis, and it heavily relies on the fact that the squared Euclidean distance is quadratic.
Interestingly,  t tt-tangentially weakly non-defectiveness discussed in Section 7.2 provides a counterpart tool.




To see this, we shall introduce the  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-analogue of the Cayley-Menger variety.
For a positive integer  p pp,
define  h_{p}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} hp:ℂ2→ℂh_{p}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} by





 h_{p}(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{1}-x_{2})^{p}. hp​(x1,x2)=(x1−x2)p.h_{p}(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{1}-x_{2})^{p}.



When  p=2 p=2p=2 and the domain is real,  f_{h_{2},K_{n}}(x) fh2,Kn​(x)f_{h_{2},K_{n}}(x) is
the list of square Euclidean distances among  n nn points  x=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}) x=(x1,x2,…,xn)x=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}) on a line,
and the image of  f_{h_{2},K_{n}} fh2,Knf_{h_{2},K_{n}} is called the Cayley-Menger variety.
Motivated by this, we call the image of  f_{h_{p},K_{n}} fhp,Knf_{h_{p},K_{n}} the  p pp-Cayley-Menger variety  {\cal CM}_{n}^{p} 𝒞ℳnp{\cal CM}_{n}^{p}.




Let  g_{p} gpg_{p} be the sum of  d dd copies of  h_{p} hph_{p}.
If  p pp is a positive even integer and the domain is restricted to real numbers, then  f_{g_{p},K_{n}} fgp,Knf_{g_{p},K_{n}} is the measurement map that outputs the list of  p pp-th powered  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-distances in  d dd-dimensional real vector space.
Hence, the global  g_{p} gpg_{p}-rigidity of a  d dd-dimensional framework  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) in our terminology is equivalent to the global rigidity of  (G,p) (G,p)(G,p) in  d dd-dimensional  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-space.




Since  g_{p} gpg_{p} is the sum of  d dd copies of  h_{p} hph_{p},
one may try to apply Corollary 7.5 to show the global  g_{p} gpg_{p}-rigidity of generic frameworks.
To apply Corollary 7.5, we need to understand when
 \overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}_{n}^{p})} πG​(𝒞ℳnp)¯\overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}_{n}^{p})} is  d dd-tangentially weakly nondefective
and when  G GG is globally  h_{p} hph_{p}-rigid.
It is a folklore fact that global rigidity in 1-dimensional  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-space is characterised by the 2-connectivity of the underlying graph,
which in turn implies that  G GG is globally  h_{p} hph_{p}-rigid if and only if  G GG is 2-connected.
In [63], Sugiyama and the fourth author
further gave a graph theoretical characterisation of the  2 22-tangentially weak non-defectiveness of  \overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}_{n}^{p})} πG​(𝒞ℳnp)¯\overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}_{n}^{p})}.



Theorem 9.4 (Sugiyama and Tanigawa [63]).



Let  p pp be an even positive integer with  p\neq 2 p≠2p\neq 2,  n\geq 3 n≥3n\geq 3 a positive integer, and  G GG a connected graph with  n nn vertices.
Then the following are equivalent:

(i) 



Some/every generic  2 22-dimensional framework of  G GG is globally rigid in the  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-plane.

(ii) 



 \overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}^{p}_{n})} πG​(𝒞ℳnp)¯\overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}^{p}_{n})} is  2 22-identifiable.

(iii) 



 \overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}^{p}_{n})} πG​(𝒞ℳnp)¯\overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}^{p}_{n})} is  2 22-tangentially weakly nondefective.

(iv) 



 G GG is  2 22-connected and  G-e G−eG-e contains two edge-disjoint spanning trees for every  e\in E(G) e∈E⁡(G)e\in E(G).

(v) 



 G GG is  2 22-connected and  G-e G−eG-e is locally rigid in the  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-plane for every  e\in E(G) e∈E⁡(G)e\in E(G).







To the best of our knowledge, such a purely graph-theoretical characterisation of the identifiability of an orthogonal projection of a variety is new.
Also, the theorem solves the generic global rigidity problem in  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-planes.




We should remark that the proof of Theorem 9.4 relies on sophisticated graph theoretical techniques from graph rigidity theory.
In particular, the proof of (iv)  \Rightarrow ⇒\Rightarrow (iii) uses the inductive construction due to Dewar, Hewetson and Nixon [29], who showed that a graph satisfying the combinatorial condition (iv) can be built up from a small graph by recursively applying two local graph-operations, called  K_{4}^{-} K4−K_{4}^{-}-extension and generalised vertex splitting.
Sugiyama and Tanigawa [63] have confirmed that each local graph-operation preserves the 2-tangentially weakly nondefective of  \overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}^{p}_{n})} πG​(𝒞ℳnp)¯\overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}^{p}_{n})}, which implies (iv)  \Rightarrow ⇒\Rightarrow (iii).
The proof of (iii)  \Rightarrow ⇒\Rightarrow (ii)  \Rightarrow ⇒\Rightarrow (i)
is done by applying a general tool from algebraic geometry explained in Section 7.2.
Finally, (i)  \Rightarrow ⇒\Rightarrow (v) and (v)  \Rightarrow ⇒\Rightarrow (iv)
have been already shown in [29] and [44], respectively.




We believe Theorem 9.4 is valid in general dimension.



Conjecture 9.5.



Let  p pp be an even positive integer with  p\neq 2 p≠2p\neq 2,  n\geq 3 n≥3n\geq 3 be a positive integer, and  G GG be a connected graph with  n nn vertices.
Then the following are equivalent:

(i) 



Some/every generic  d dd-dimensional framework of  G GG is globally rigid in  d dd-dimensional  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-space.

(ii) 



 \overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}^{p}_{n})} πG​(𝒞ℳnp)¯\overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}^{p}_{n})} is  d dd-identifiable.

(iii) 



 \overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}^{p}_{n})} πG​(𝒞ℳnp)¯\overline{\pi_{G}({\cal CM}^{p}_{n})} is  d dd-tangentially weakly nondefective.

(iv) 



 G GG is 2-connected and  G-e G−eG-e contains  d dd edge-disjoint spanning trees for every  e\in E(G) e∈E⁡(G)e\in E(G).

(v) 



 G GG is 2-connected and  G-e G−eG-e is locally rigid in the  \ell_{p} ℓp\ell_{p}-plane for every  e\in E(G) e∈E⁡(G)e\in E(G).







By the theory in Section 7,
the proofs of (iii)  \Rightarrow ⇒\Rightarrow (ii)  \Rightarrow ⇒\Rightarrow (i)  \Rightarrow ⇒\Rightarrow (v) in Theorem 9.4 still work in general dimension.
(v)  \Rightarrow ⇒\Rightarrow (iv) also follows from the well-known Maxwell count argument.
Currently, the proof for (iv)  \Rightarrow ⇒\Rightarrow (iii) is missing.






10 Conclusion




We have introduced a far reaching generalisation of graph rigidity theory to the general setting of identifiability of points, which encompasses diverse applications. Throughout the paper, we have provided analogues of several fundamental theorems in this generalised framework. We now conclude the paper by describing a number of open problems and potential extensions of our work.




From a rigidity theoretic perspective, a natural question arises regarding the search for combinatorial characterisations of (global)  g gg-rigidity. A notable instance is presented in Conjecture 4.13, where we propose a potential characterisation of  g gg-rigidity in the case when  g gg is of a generic form. Establishing such combinatorial characterisations would provide insights into the fundamental properties of  g gg-rigidity.




As mentioned earlier, we expect that Theorem 7.13 holds for a broader class of polynomial maps  g gg, extending beyond the specific cases explored in the paper. A particularly interesting to resolve would be the case when  g gg is the permanent function. Exploring the rigidity properties associated with the permanent could shed light on its connection to other combinatorial structures.




In Section 7 we have shown a sufficient condition for global  g gg-rigidity using weighted adjacent matrices,
which is an analogue of Connelly’s stress-matrix condition. An important remaining question is to establish a graph theoretical property to guarantee the applicability of the theorem.




Another interesting direction for future research would be to develop sufficient conditions for (global)  g gg-rigidity in the multipartite model discussed in Section 8.




We would like to add that Corollary 6.9 also has significant application in statistics, in particular in the identifiability problem of Gaussian mixture models, which aims to determine the minimum number of parameters necessary to uniquely determine the statistical model given  n nn sample points. The goal is to find parameters whose corresponding mixture function approximates the sample points. The method of moments is a commonly used approach for parameter recovery in Gaussian mixture models (see, e.g. [3, 6]). In the method of moments, the identifiability problem is reduced to determining the non-defectivity of secants of the corresponding varieties. However, this is only done for the specific cases, e.g. when the covariance matrix is zero, as in these cases the moment variety corresponds to the classical Veronese varieties, whose secants’ non-defectivity are determined by the Alexander-Hirschowitz Theorem.
Therefore, we hope that our arguments will shed light on the more general case of the problem. In particular, it would be interesting to capture the identifiability of Gaussian mixture models as specific instances of the  g gg-rigidity model.

Instructions for reporting errors
    
We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below:
    
      Click the "Report Issue" (
            
          ) button, located in the page header.
    
    
Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.
    
Our team has already identified the following issues. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all.
    
Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion, and welcome developer contributions.
